# Sūrah al-Raḥmān (Chapter 55) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Named After the Name of Mercy**

Sūrah al-Raḥmān takes its name from its very first word — *al-Raḥmān*, "The Compassionate" — and in doing so it becomes the only chapter of the Qur'an that opens with a divine Name standing entirely alone, a single word occupying an entire verse. This is not an accident of arrangement. The whole sūrah is, in effect, an unfolding of what that one Name contains. Every blessing enumerated across its seventy-eight verses — revelation, speech, the sun and moon, the seas, the fruits of the earth, the creation of man and jinn, and finally the gardens of Paradise with all their rivers and companions — is presented as a single continuous outpouring from *al-Raḥmān*. The chapter reads like a divine ledger of gifts, and its recurring question, repeated thirty-one times, asks the two responsible species of creation, humankind and the jinn, which of these gifts they could possibly have the audacity to deny.

The Name itself is singular in Islamic theology. Of all the ninety-nine Names, *al-Raḥmān* is the one the Qur'an places on a par with the supreme Name *Allāh* itself:

> *"Say, 'Call upon God (Allāh), or call upon the Compassionate (al-Raḥmān). Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names.'"* (Qur'an 17:110)

*Al-Raḥmān* denotes a mercy that is boundless, prior to the created order, and universal — a mercy by which existence itself is brought into being and sustained, extended even to those who reject God, for it is the mercy that makes the disbeliever's next breath possible. This is why the classical scholars observed that *al-Raḥmān*, unlike *al-Raḥīm*, can never be applied to a human being: no creature possesses a mercy that undergirds existence itself. The whole sūrah, therefore, is an argument delivered through gratitude — a demonstration that the entire cosmos, from the star to the pomegranate to the gardens of the Hereafter, is the handiwork of a single overflowing compassion.

**When and Why It Was Revealed**

The majority of scholars, including those cited by al-Ālūsī and al-Qurṭubī, hold that al-Raḥmān is a Makkan sūrah, revealed during the years when the Prophet ﷺ was calling the idolaters of Makkah to the worship of the one God. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have made an exception for verse 29, considering it Madinan, and a minority held the entire sūrah to be Madinan — but the dominant view places it in the Makkan phase, and its themes support this: the insistence on God's oneness, the vivid portrayal of resurrection and judgment, and the direct confrontation with a people who scoffed at the very Name *al-Raḥmān*.

That last point supplies a likely occasion for its revelation. The Qur'an records that the pagans of Makkah recoiled from this particular Name, feigning ignorance of it:

> *"And when it is said unto them, 'Prostrate before the Compassionate,' they say, 'And what is the Compassionate? Shall we prostrate before that [to] which you command us?' And it increases them in aversion."* (Qur'an 25:60)

And again:

> *"...yet they disbelieve in the Compassionate. Say, 'He is my Lord; there is no god but He. In Him do I trust and unto Him do I turn.'"* (Qur'an 13:30)

Several commentators therefore read al-Raḥmān as a sustained answer to those who asked, mockingly, "What is *al-Raḥmān*?" The sūrah replies: *This* is al-Raḥmān — the One who taught the Qur'an, created man, taught him speech, set the sun and moon upon their courses, raised the heaven, laid down the earth, and prepared the gardens. The Name they pretended not to recognize is answered not with an abstract definition but with an inventory of everything they depend upon every moment of their lives.

**The Bride of the Qur'an**

Muslims across the centuries have called this sūrah *ʿArūs al-Qurʾān*, "The Bride of the Qur'an," on the strength of a well-known (though unauthenticated) report: *"For everything there is a bride, and the bride of the Qur'an is Sūrat al-Raḥmān."* Even setting aside the report's chain, the epithet has endured because it captures something real about the chapter's character. It is adorned like a bride: its cadences are measured and rhythmic, its imagery luminous — pearls and coral, brocade and cushions, dark-green gardens and bubbling springs — and its refrain returns like a garland threaded again and again through the whole. Where much of the Qur'an argues, warns, legislates, and narrates, al-Raḥmān sings its case. It persuades through beauty, and its beauty is itself part of the argument: a God whose speech is this lovely is a God whose claim on gratitude is self-evident.

**The Architecture of Duality**

The single most striking structural feature of the sūrah is its pervasive use of the dual. The refrain — *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — is addressed grammatically to two parties (*rabbukumā*, "your Lord" in the dual; *tukadhdhibān*, "you two deny"), understood throughout the tradition as humankind and the jinn, the two species burdened with moral responsibility. And the world the sūrah describes is likewise doubled at every turn: two easts and two wests, two seas, two kinds of every fruit, two gardens and then two more gardens, pairs of springs, pairs of companions. Creation is presented as woven from complementary pairs, and this grammatical and thematic duality is not decoration but doctrine. It teaches that the created order is inherently relational, balanced, and paired — and that at the center of all these pairs stands the One who is not paired, the Single, the Unmatched.

That center is marked with precision. In the middle of the sūrah, amid all the dualities, come the words:

> *"All that is upon it passes away. And there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty."* (Qur'an 55:26–27)

Everything doubled and created fades; only the Face of the One remains. And the sūrah's final verse echoes this exactly: *"Blessed is the Name of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty."* Thus the chapter is framed by the permanence of God on both sides of a vast catalogue of impermanent gifts. The blessings of earth (verses 10–25) and the blessings of Heaven (verses 46–76) are both bracketed by the reminder that the Giver alone endures. The reader is carried on a tour of everything God has made and everything He has promised, and is returned, at both the midpoint and the end, to the one truth that outlasts it all.

**How This Commentary Proceeds**

What follows is a verse-by-verse expanded commentary, treating each verse — including every occurrence of the refrain — as an independent, fully developed discourse, as the verse-by-verse method requires. Each occurrence of *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* is anchored to the specific blessing it follows, so that its meaning is drawn freshly from its context rather than repeated mechanically; the Qur'an itself never intended that refrain as filler, and neither does this commentary. Throughout, the discussion draws upon the Arabic language and its roots, cross-references within the Qur'an with the relevant verses quoted, authenticated words of the Prophet ﷺ with their collections named, the views of the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and others), and the living reasoning that connects this Makkan revelation to the psychology, ethics, and daily experience of the human being who reads it today.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:1

> **The Compassionate**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Whole Verse in a Single Name**

The Qur'an's longest chapter opened with three disconnected letters; this chapter opens with one word — and that word is a Name of God. Nowhere else in the Qur'an does a divine Name stand as a complete verse by itself. The effect is arresting. Before any statement is made, before any command is issued, before the reader is told anything about what God does or asks, he is given the Name under which everything that follows will unfold: *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate. The entire sūrah is thereby placed beneath a single canopy. Whatever comes next — teaching, creation, judgment, reward — is to be understood as an act of *this* Name.

**The Root of Mercy**

*Al-Raḥmān* derives from the Arabic root *r-ḥ-m*, the root of *raḥmah*, "mercy," and of *raḥim*, "the womb." This is the same root from which *al-Raḥīm*, "the Merciful," is drawn, and the pairing of the two Names in the opening formula of nearly every sūrah — *bismi'llāhi'l-Raḥmāni'l-Raḥīm* — has occupied the commentators deeply. The grammatical form of *al-Raḥmān* follows the intensive pattern *faʿlān*, the same pattern as *ghaḍbān* (one filled with anger) and *ʿaṭshān* (one filled with thirst). This pattern in Arabic conveys fullness, abundance, an overflowing state that saturates its bearer. *Al-Raḥmān* is therefore not merely one who shows mercy but one whose very being brims and overflows with a mercy so vast it spills across all of existence.

*Al-Raḥīm*, by contrast, follows the pattern *faʿīl*, which conveys constancy and permanence — a mercy that is enduring and reliably applied. The classical distinction, expressed by al-Qurṭubī and many others, runs thus: *al-Raḥmān* is the mercy that embraces all creation without distinction, believer and disbeliever, human and animal, in this life; *al-Raḥīm* is the particular, abiding mercy reserved especially for the believers, above all in the Hereafter. As the commentators put it, *al-Raḥmān* relates more directly to the Divine Essence, the source, while *al-Raḥīm* relates to the extension of that mercy into specific acts.

**A Name That Belongs to God Alone**

One of the most important theological points about *al-Raḥmān* is that it is among the Names that can be applied to no one but God. A human being may be called *raḥīm*, merciful, in a limited sense — indeed the Qur'an calls the Prophet ﷺ himself *raʾūf raḥīm*, "kind and merciful," toward the believers (9:128). But no one may be called *al-Raḥmān*. The reason is that *al-Raḥmān* denotes a mercy prior to and constitutive of existence itself — the mercy by which things are brought into being and held in being. No creature possesses such a mercy, for no creature grants existence. When the false prophet Musaylimah attempted to arrogate this Name to himself, calling himself *Raḥmān al-Yamāmah*, the entire Muslim community recognized it as blasphemy, precisely because the Name is God's alone.

The Qur'an itself equates *al-Raḥmān* with the supreme Name in a way it does for no other attribute:

> *"Say, 'Call upon God (Allāh), or call upon the Compassionate (al-Raḥmān). Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names.'"* (Qur'an 17:110)

And it is *al-Raḥmān* specifically who is described as having established Himself over the Throne — the seat of universal sovereignty:

> *"The Compassionate, established upon the Throne."* (Qur'an 20:5)

The pairing is deliberate: supreme power (the Throne) is exercised through supreme mercy (al-Raḥmān). The universe is governed not by an indifferent force but by an overflowing compassion.

**Why the Sūrah Must Begin Here**

There is profound logic in opening a chapter about blessings with the Name of mercy rather than, say, the Name of power or the Name of majesty. A gift given by a powerful being might be a display of power; a gift given by a majestic being might be a demand for awe. But when the Giver is named *al-Raḥmān* first, every subsequent gift is reframed as an act of tenderness. The sun is not merely evidence of God's might; it is a mercy that warms and orders time. Speech is not merely proof of design; it is a compassionate honoring of the creature. Even the fearsome verses later in the sūrah about the Fire and the rending of the sky are delivered under this same Name — a reminder that warning itself is a form of mercy, for the one who is warned is the one being given the chance to escape.

**The Reply to a Mocking Question**

Recall that the pagans of Makkah had sneered, *"And what is the Compassionate?"* (25:60). By opening with this exact Name, standing alone and undefended, the sūrah answers their scorn with serene confidence. It does not argue for the Name; it simply pronounces it and then, verse after verse, lets the entire cosmos testify on its behalf. There is a psychological wisdom here that any reader can feel. The most powerful answer to mockery is often not a rebuttal but a demonstration. The mockers asked "What is al-Raḥmān?" as though the word were empty; the sūrah replies by showing that the word is so full that seventy-seven verses cannot exhaust it.

**Living Beneath the Name**

For the reader today, the opening word sets a posture. To begin anything — a day, a task, a relationship, a hardship — under the recognition that the ultimate reality is *al-Raḥmān* is to begin in trust rather than dread. The person who believes that the deepest truth of existence is an overflowing mercy will interpret their experiences differently from the person who believes existence is indifferent or hostile. When difficulty comes, the believer in *al-Raḥmān* asks not "Why is the universe against me?" but "What mercy might be folded even within this?" This is not naïve optimism; it is a reasoned trust grounded in the recognition that the same Name which made the sun and the sea and the human tongue is the Name presiding over one's present moment. The sūrah opens by naming that Name, and everything else it says is a commentary upon it.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:2

> **taught the Quran;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Act of Mercy Is Teaching**

Immediately after naming Himself the Compassionate, God names His first act: *ʿallama al-Qurʾān*, "taught the Qur'an." The sequence is theologically dense. Of all the things God could have listed first — creation, provision, power — He lists teaching, and specifically the teaching of revelation. This tells us that in the divine economy, guidance is not an afterthought added on top of existence; it is among the first and highest expressions of mercy. A God who merely created and then abandoned His creatures to grope in the dark would not be *al-Raḥmān* in the full sense. The Compassionate does not only give life; He gives light. He teaches.

Notice, too, that teaching the Qur'an is mentioned *before* the creation of man in the next verse. Grammatically and rhetorically, revelation is placed ahead of the human being it is meant for. The commentators drew a striking conclusion from this ordering: human beings were made *for* knowledge and revelation; the gift was prepared before the recipient. As the commentator al-Ālūsī notes, this indicates that humanity was created to know revelation even before its creation in this world. The purpose precedes the creature.

**The Meaning of ʿAllama**

The verb *ʿallama* comes from the root *ʿ-l-m*, the root of *ʿilm*, knowledge. The doubled form *ʿallama* (as opposed to the simple *ʿalima*, "he knew") carries the causative and intensive sense of "he caused to know," "he taught thoroughly," "he instructed." Throughout the Qur'an this verb marks the great turning points of divine generosity toward the human intellect. God *taught* Adam the names of all things (2:31); He *taught* man that which he knew not (96:5); and here He *taught* the Qur'an. Teaching is presented as one of God's essential relational functions toward humanity — the function that precedes command, law, and judgment, because one cannot justly be commanded, legislated for, or judged without first being taught.

**A Direct Answer to a Slander**

Several commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, read this verse as a pointed response to a specific accusation the Makkans leveled against the Prophet ﷺ. They alleged that his recitation was not divine at all but had been taught to him by some human tutor:

> *"And We certainly know that they say, 'It is only a human being who teaches him.' The tongue of the one they refer to is foreign, while this is a clear Arabic tongue."* (Qur'an 16:103)

Against this charge, verse 2 declares the true Teacher: it is *al-Raḥmān* who taught the Qur'an, not any human being. The Book has one source, and that source is the overflowing mercy of God. This reading gives the verse a polemical edge beneath its serene surface: it quietly demolishes the conspiracy theory of a hidden human author by naming the real Author in a single word.

**God Made the Qur'an Easy to Learn**

Other commentators read the phrase differently, and their reading is equally illuminating. They understand *"taught the Qur'an"* as an allusion to the way God made the Qur'an *learnable* — memorizable, recitable, accessible to ordinary tongues and hearts. This connects directly to the refrain of the preceding sūrah, al-Qamar:

> *"And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who will remember?"* (Qur'an 54:17, 22, 32, 40)

And to the verse:

> *"We have only made it easy upon thy tongue, that haply they may remember."* (Qur'an 44:58)

On this reading, the mercy is not merely that God revealed a Book, but that He revealed it in a form the human being could carry — a Book that a child can memorize entirely, that an unlettered shepherd can recite, that has been preserved word-for-word in millions of hearts across every generation. This is itself a mercy of *al-Raḥmān*: the accessibility of guidance. A cure locked in a language no one can read would be a mockery of the sick; the Compassionate gave a cure written in a script the heart can hold.

**Why Teaching Belongs to Compassion — The Logic**

There is a deep logic in the placement of "teaching" directly after "the Compassionate." Consider what teaching actually is. To teach is to lift another out of ignorance at one's own expense of time and effort, seeking nothing in return but the other's benefit. Teaching is, at its core, an act of self-giving directed at the mind of another. It is therefore intrinsically an act of mercy. A parent who teaches a child to read, a mentor who trains an apprentice, a doctor who explains a diagnosis so the patient can heal — all these are exercising a small human echo of the divine attribute displayed here. God, being *al-Raḥmān*, could not withhold the highest knowledge from the creatures He loved into existence. To create a being capable of knowing God and then to leave it without the knowledge of God would be a cruelty incompatible with the Name. So mercy overflows into instruction.

**Application: The Dignity of the Learner and the Teacher**

For the reader, this verse establishes two enduring principles. First, it dignifies the act of seeking knowledge as a participation in the very first thing God is said to do in this sūrah. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by Ibn Mājah, *"Seeking knowledge is an obligation upon every Muslim."* When a person sits to learn the Qur'an — or, by extension, any beneficial knowledge — they are aligning themselves with the opening act of the Compassionate. Second, it dignifies teaching as an imitation of a divine attribute. The one who teaches the Qur'an is doing, in miniature, what *al-Raḥmān* does. This is why the Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī, *"The best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it."* The best of people are those who most closely mirror the first act named after the Name of Mercy.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:3

> **created man;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation Placed After Revelation**

Having named the teaching of the Qur'an, God now names the creation of man — *khalaqa al-insān*. The ordering has fascinated the commentators. In ordinary logic, one would expect creation to be mentioned first: surely the creature must exist before it can be taught. Yet the Qur'an reverses the sequence, mentioning the teaching of revelation before the creation of the human being. The lesson the classical scholars drew is that from God's perspective the purpose is prior to the product. Revelation was not devised to fit man after the fact; man was fashioned to receive revelation. The human being is, so to speak, the vessel shaped for a knowledge already prepared. This is why the commentator al-Ālūsī says the arrangement indicates that human beings were made to know revelation before their very creation in this world.

**Who Is "Man" Here?**

The Arabic *al-insān* is the general word for the human being, and most commentators take it here as a reference to all of humanity. A few, recorded by al-Qurṭubī, suggested it might specifically indicate the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ or Adam, the first man. But the dominant and most natural reading is universal: the Compassionate created the human race. The word *al-insān* itself is rich. Some philologists derive it from *uns*, "intimacy, sociability, familiarity," suggesting that the human being is by nature a creature made for relationship — with God, and with fellow beings. Others derive it from *nisyān*, "forgetfulness," pointing to the human tendency to forget the covenant with God, a tendency the Qur'an repeatedly addresses. Both derivations illuminate the creature described: made for intimacy, prone to forgetting, and therefore in constant need of the reminder that revelation provides. This is precisely why teaching precedes creation in these verses — the forgetful creature needs the reminder built in from the start.

**The Parallel with the First Revelation**

The pairing of revelation and the creation of man here echoes what is widely held to be the very first passage of the Qur'an revealed chronologically:

> *"Recite in the Name of thy Lord who created — created man from a clinging clot. Recite, and thy Lord is most Generous — who taught by the pen, taught man that which he knew not."* (Qur'an 96:1–5)

In both places, the creation of the human being and the gift of divinely taught knowledge are bound together. The correspondence is not coincidental. It reflects a settled Qur'anic doctrine: the human being and knowledge belong together as body and soul. To be human is to be the creature designed for knowing. Where the animal is equipped for survival, the human is equipped for understanding — and, above all, for the understanding of God.

**A Hidden Allusion to Mercy in the Word for Womb**

There is a subtle and beautiful point that the commentators draw out here, connecting the creation of man to the very Name that opens the sūrah. The Arabic word for the womb, *raḥim*, derives from the same root, *r-ḥ-m*, as *al-Raḥmān*. When God says He "created man" — and human beings are created within the womb — the language itself whispers that human origin is embedded in mercy. We are, quite literally, formed in an organ whose name is drawn from the Name of the Compassionate. The Prophet ﷺ made this connection explicit in a famous ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"God said: 'I am al-Raḥmān (the Compassionate). I created the raḥim (womb) and derived for it a name from My Name. So whoever maintains its ties, I will maintain ties with him, and whoever severs it, I will sever ties with him.'"* (al-Bukhārī 5988; and in Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī with similar wording)

This ḥadīth transforms the verse. The creation of man is not merely a display of power; it is an act embedded in mercy from its first moment, in a place whose name is a fragment of the Divine Name. And it carries a direct moral consequence: because our very origin is named after mercy, the maintenance of family ties (*ṣilat al-raḥim*) becomes a sacred duty, and their severance a grave offense that God Himself promises to answer in kind. The one who cuts off his kin cuts himself off from the mercy in whose organ he was formed.

**The Logic of a Merciful Creation**

Why is the creation of man an act of *al-Raḥmān* rather than merely of *al-Khāliq*, the Creator? Because to bring a creature into existence and equip it with the capacity for joy, love, knowledge, and nearness to God — and then to guide it toward its fulfillment — is an act of generosity, not obligation. God gained nothing by creating man; the human being adds nothing to God's perfection. Creation is therefore pure gift, and pure gift given for the recipient's benefit is the definition of mercy. The verse invites the reader to consider his own existence not as a brute fact but as an unearned kindness. You did not ask to be created; you did not merit it; it was given. And it was given by the Compassionate.

**Application: Reading Your Own Origin Rightly**

For the modern reader, this verse offers a corrective to two opposite errors. The first is the error of self-inflation — the belief that one's existence is self-caused, self-owned, and self-directed, answerable to no one. The verse says: you were *created*, and by *al-Raḥmān*; your existence is a gift held in trust, not a possession to dispose of as you please. The second is the error of self-abasement — the belief that one is an accident, a meaningless byproduct of blind processes, of no particular worth. The verse says: you were created deliberately, by the Compassionate, and equipped before your birth with a revelation prepared for you; you are the intended reader of the Book of God. Between arrogance and despair, the verse installs the correct posture: humble gratitude. You are a beloved creature of a merciful Creator, made for knowledge and honored with speech — as the next verse will say.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:4

> **taught him speech.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gift That Distinguishes the Human Being**

Having created man, the Compassionate now names the faculty that sets him apart from every other creature: *ʿallamahu al-bayān*, "taught him speech." The verb *ʿallama* returns — the same verb used for the teaching of the Qur'an in verse 2 — binding the two teachings together. God taught the Qur'an, and God taught man *bayān*: the capacity to articulate, to express, to make clear. The parallel is deliberate. The Book to be understood and the faculty that understands it are both gifts of the same Teacher. Revelation would be useless to a creature incapable of comprehending and articulating it; so the Compassionate, having prepared the Book, prepared the tongue and mind to receive it.

**The Meaning of Bayān**

The word *bayān* comes from the root *b-y-n*, which carries the sense of separation, distinction, and clarity — the making distinct of one thing from another. *Bayān* is therefore not merely the physical production of sound (many animals produce sound) but the capacity for *articulate, meaningful expression*: the ability to distinguish concepts, to name them, to arrange them into coherent thought, and to communicate them to others. It is the faculty by which the inner world of the mind becomes shareable. Where a bird's call conveys a fixed signal, human *bayān* can compose a sentence never before spoken, express a subtle degree of feeling, argue a case, tell a history, or pray. The commentators observed that human beings are distinguished from all other creatures precisely by this — the power both to comprehend meaning and to articulate it.

**More Than the Tongue: The Power to Discern**

Because *bayān* is rooted in the idea of "making distinct," several commentators (including those cited by al-Qurṭubī and al-Shawkānī) understood the verse to encompass more than verbal speech. It also embraces the God-given power to *distinguish* — to tell good from evil, permitted from forbidden, truth from falsehood, benefit from harm. Speech and discernment are, after all, deeply linked: we cannot articulate a distinction we cannot perceive, and we sharpen our perceptions by articulating them. On this reading, "taught him speech" means God endowed the human being with the whole apparatus of clarity — the intellect that discerns and the tongue that expresses. This is the faculty that makes moral responsibility possible, for a creature that could not distinguish right from wrong could not justly be held accountable.

**Bayān and the Teaching of the Names**

Some commentators linked this verse to the account of Adam in Sūrat al-Baqarah:

> *"And He taught Adam the names, all of them."* (Qur'an 2:31)

The teaching of "the names" to Adam is understood as the bestowal of language and conceptual knowledge — the very foundation of *bayān*. In this light, verse 4 recapitulates the primordial honoring of the human being: the same gift that raised Adam above the angels, who confessed *"We have no knowledge save what You have taught us"* (2:32), is the gift renewed in every human child who learns to speak. Every time an infant begins to name the world, the honoring of Adam is re-enacted.

**Bayān as a Name of the Qur'an Itself**

There is a further layer. *Bayān* is one of the names the Qur'an gives to itself:

> *"This is an exposition (bayān) for mankind, and a guidance and exhortation for the reverent."* (Qur'an 3:138)

So the same word names both the human faculty of clear expression and the divine Book of clear exposition. The symmetry is exquisite: God, whose speech is the ultimate *bayān*, gave the human being a created *bayān* by which to receive it. Human articulate speech is the finite mirror of the infinite clarity of revelation. This is why the four verses 1–4 form a single arc that the commentators love to trace: *the Compassionate* (the merciful Source) *taught the Qur'an* (the perfect divine speech) *created man* (the intended recipient) *taught him speech* (the faculty that receives). Mercy, message, messenger's kind, and medium — laid out in four short strokes.

**The Logic and Wonder of Speech**

Pause on how astonishing the gift actually is. In ordinary life we take speech utterly for granted, yet it is arguably the single most consequential faculty humanity possesses. Every civilization, law, science, poem, prayer, and act of love that has ever been communicated rests upon *bayān*. Through speech, knowledge accumulates across generations rather than dying with each individual; through speech, a person alone in the dark can call upon God and be heard; through speech, the invisible contents of one mind cross into another. No wealth, no technology, no empire could exist without this faculty — and it is given, freely, to virtually every human child, unearned. That the Qur'an numbers *bayān* among the primary boons of *al-Raḥmān*, listing it immediately after existence itself, is a summons to recognize a mercy so constant that we have gone blind to it.

**Application: The Weight of the Tongue**

If speech is a divine gift, then its use is a trust. The same tongue that can recite the Qur'an, teach the ignorant, comfort the grieving, and testify to the truth can also lie, slander, wound, and blaspheme. The Prophet ﷺ warned of exactly this double edge, as recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."*

And he warned, as recorded by al-Tirmidhī, that a person may utter a word pleasing to God that raises him in ranks, and another may utter a word displeasing to God that casts him into the Fire farther than the distance between east and west. To recognize *bayān* as a gift of the Compassionate is to feel the responsibility that comes with it: the faculty that most distinguishes us is the faculty by which we are most tested. Gratitude for the gift of speech is expressed not merely by acknowledging it but by guarding it — using the tongue for clarity, truth, kindness, and remembrance, and restraining it from the harm it is equally capable of causing.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:5

> **The sun and the moon are upon a reckoning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Gifts of the Soul to the Order of the Cosmos**

The first four verses spoke of gifts to the human interior — revelation, existence, and speech, the mercies that nourish the mind and soul. Now the sūrah lifts its gaze to the heavens and begins to enumerate the mercies woven into the fabric of the cosmos, beginning with the two great luminaries: *"The sun and the moon are upon a reckoning."* The transition is natural. Having honored the human being with knowledge and speech, God now shows the human being a universe made intelligible — a cosmos that behaves according to measurable, dependable law, precisely the kind of order that a knowing, speaking creature can study, calculate, and rely upon.

**The Meaning of Ḥusbān**

The phrase translated "upon a reckoning" renders the Arabic *bi-ḥusbān*. The word *ḥusbān* comes from the root *ḥ-s-b*, the root of *ḥisāb*, "calculation, accounting, reckoning." To say the sun and moon are *bi-ḥusbān* is to say they move according to an exact, computed measure — a precise mathematical order from which they never deviate. They are not wandering randomly across the sky; they run on an appointed calculation, as regular as a ledger. The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, explain that this means the sun and moon follow a known, established pattern that never fails, so dependable that human beings can build their entire measurement of time upon it.

The Qur'an makes this function explicit elsewhere:

> *"He it is Who made the sun a radiance, and the moon a light, and determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning [of time]."* (Qur'an 10:5)

The very regularity of the heavens is presented as a mercy aimed at the human need to measure time — to know when to plant and harvest, when to travel, when to mark the seasons and the years.

**A Blessing With Sacred Consequences**

For the Muslim, this celestial reckoning is not merely practical; it is the foundation of worship itself. Three of the five pillars of Islam depend directly upon the measured motions of the sun and moon. The five daily prayers are timed by the sun's position — dawn, midday, afternoon, sunset, and night. The fast of Ramadan is governed by the sighting of the moon, beginning and ending the lunar month. And the pilgrimage, the ḥajj, falls in a fixed lunar month and on days determined by the calendar the moon establishes. The dependable motion of the heavens is therefore woven into the very structure of Muslim devotional life. A God who wished His creatures to worship Him at appointed times gave them, in the sky, a clock that never stops and never lies. The commentators note this as a particular mercy: the timing of worship is legible in the heavens to anyone, anywhere, without instrument.

**A Rebuke to Those Who Worship the Heavens**

There is a second dimension to this verse. To say that the sun and moon are "upon a reckoning" is to say that they are *governed* — subject to a law imposed upon them, not laws unto themselves. This is an implicit rebuke to the many peoples, ancient and modern, who have worshipped the sun and moon as deities. A true god is not subject to a reckoning; a true god imposes the reckoning. The Qur'an draws this conclusion openly:

> *"And of His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon. Prostrate not unto the sun, nor unto the moon. Prostrate unto God, Who created them, if it is He Whom you worship."* (Qur'an 41:37)

The very obedience of the sun and moon to their calculated course is proof that they are servants, not masters. They point beyond themselves to the One who set their measure. This is a recurring Qur'anic logic: the orderliness of a thing is evidence of an Orderer, and the subjection of a thing is evidence that it is not the ultimate.

**The Transience of the Luminaries**

The verse also carries an implication of impermanence. A thing that runs "for a term appointed" is a thing with a term — a beginning and an end. The Qur'an makes this explicit:

> *"...and He made the sun and the moon subservient, each running for a term appointed."* (Qur'an 39:5)

The sun and moon, however ancient and vast, are creatures on a leash of law and time. This anticipates the sūrah's central turn at verses 26–27: *"All that is upon it passes away. And there remains the Face of thy Lord."* Even the mightiest lights in the sky are counted, measured, and destined to end. Only the One who counts them endures.

**The Logic: Order as a Sign of Mercy**

Why should the mere regularity of the heavens count as a *mercy* worth denying or affirming? Consider what a chaotic cosmos would mean. If the sun rose at unpredictable intervals, if the seasons came in no order, if the moon's phases followed no pattern, no agriculture, navigation, calendar, or planning would be possible. Human life as we know it — indeed human civilization — is only possible because the universe is reliable. Science itself is only possible because nature obeys constant laws. The believer sees in this reliability not a cold fact but a deliberate kindness: the Compassionate made a universe a knowing creature could actually understand and depend upon. The modern physicist who marvels that the cosmos is mathematically describable is marveling, in secular language, at exactly what this verse proclaims: the heavens are *bi-ḥusbān*, upon a reckoning.

**Application: Living by a Trustworthy Order**

For the reader, this verse invites a double response. First, gratitude for dependability — the quiet mercy of a world that keeps its appointments, so that we can keep ours. Second, a summons to align one's own life with a higher reckoning. If the sun and moon submit perfectly to the measure God set for them, the human being is invited to bring his own life into a comparable order — prayers at their times, obligations met, a life run "upon a reckoning" rather than drifting in chaos. And beyond even that, the verse whispers of a final reckoning: the same word, *ḥisāb*, names the Day of Account. The heavens that keep their reckoning are a daily reminder that we, too, will be brought to ours.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:6

> **And the stars and the trees prostrate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**All Creation Bows**

Having shown that the sun and moon obey a fixed reckoning, the sūrah now extends the theme of cosmic submission from the heavens to the earth: *"And the stars and the trees prostrate."* The image is one of universal worship. From the most distant star to the humblest tree, creation is depicted as bowing before God. This picks up a theme found throughout the Qur'an — that everything in existence, in its own mode, submits to and glorifies its Maker:

> *"Hast thou not considered that unto God prostrates whosoever is in the heavens and whosoever is on the earth, and the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the beasts, and many among mankind?"* (Qur'an 22:18)

And:

> *"Unto God prostrates whosoever is in the heavens and on the earth, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows in the morning and the evening."* (Qur'an 13:15)

The verse thus places the human worshipper within a vast, silent congregation. When the believer prostrates in prayer, he joins a cosmos already in prostration.

**Najm: Stars or Herbs?**

The Arabic word translated "stars," *najm*, is grammatically singular here, though it is understood collectively as a reference to all the stars. But the word carries a second meaning that the commentators found significant. *Najm* also denotes soft-stemmed plants — herbs, grasses, and creeping vegetation that spread low along the ground, as opposed to *shajar*, hard-stemmed woody plants and trees. On this reading, recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Ālūsī, the verse pairs two categories of plant life: *najm*, the low herbs, and *shajar*, the tall trees. The verse would then read: "And the herbs and the trees prostrate" — a merism embracing the entire plant kingdom, from the smallest ground-cover to the loftiest tree.

Both readings are accepted, and each is beautiful. If *najm* means "stars," the verse spans the vertical axis of creation, pairing the highest bodies (stars) with earthly growth (trees) — heaven and earth bowing together. If *najm* means "herbs," the verse encompasses the whole botanical world, low and high, in a single act of submission. The Arabic language, by holding both meanings at once, lets the verse mean both: the whole vertical sweep of creation and the whole horizontal spread of plant life, all in prostration.

**What It Means for a Star or Tree to "Prostrate"**

The word for "prostrate" here is *yasjudān*, from *sujūd*, the same word used for the physical prostration of the worshipper, forehead to the ground. What does it mean for a star or a tree to prostrate? The commentators explain it as an expression of *complete submission* — the total conformity of these creatures to the will and law of God. A star cannot disobey the laws by which it burns and moves; a tree cannot refuse the seasons, the pull toward the light, the cycle of growth God ordained for it. Their "prostration" is their unbroken obedience. Some scholars add that this prostration may be a real act of glorification whose modality we cannot perceive, in line with:

> *"...and there is no thing but that it glorifies His praise, but you do not understand their glorification."* (Qur'an 17:44)

Whether understood as metaphorical submission or as a real glorification hidden from human perception, the point stands: all things worship, each in its own way.

**The Human Being as the Exception That Must Choose**

Here lies the verse's most piercing lesson. The stars and trees prostrate *by nature* — they have no choice, and so their submission, though perfect, is not moral. The human being alone (with the jinn) is given the freedom to prostrate or refuse. Notice the same verse from Sūrat al-Ḥajj quoted above ends: *"...and many among mankind"* prostrate — implying that many do not. The whole non-human cosmos bows without exception; only among humanity are there rebels. This is the paradox of human dignity: we are the one creature whose worship *counts* precisely because it can be withheld. The sun's obedience is magnificent but automatic; a human being's obedience is precious because it is freely given.

This reframes the sūrah's coming refrain. When God asks the jinn and mankind, *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*, He is addressing the only creatures capable of denial in a universe otherwise unanimous in submission. The verse gently shames the human tendency to withhold what all creation freely offers. The stars, which owe you nothing, bow; the trees, which possess no intellect, bow; will you, who were taught the Qur'an and given speech, stand aloof?

**The Logic: Submission as the Grain of the Universe**

There is a profound coherence in this vision. If the entire cosmos operates by submission to God's law — and modern science confirms that every particle obeys constant physical laws — then submission is not an alien imposition upon reality but its very grain. For the human being to submit to God (the literal meaning of *islām*) is therefore to align oneself with the deepest pattern of existence, to stop being the one jarring note in a symphony of obedience. Rebellion against God is, in this light, not liberation but dissonance — the single creature straining against the current that carries everything else. To worship is to come home to the way things actually are.

**Application: Joining the Silent Congregation**

For the reader, this verse offers both consolation and challenge. The consolation is that the worshipper is never truly alone: to pray is to join a congregation that spans the galaxies. The challenge is to make one's freely chosen prostration match, in sincerity, the involuntary prostration of the stars — to render as a gift what they render as necessity. When you next look up at the night sky or walk beneath trees, the verse invites you to see not inert objects but fellow worshippers, and to feel the summons to add your voice, willingly, to theirs.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:7

> **Heaven He has raised and the Balance He has set,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Raising of the Heaven**

The sūrah continues its tour of creation, now naming two acts together: God raised the heaven and set the Balance. *"Heaven He has raised"* — *wa'l-samāʾa rafaʿahā* — evokes the vast canopy of the sky lifted high above the earth, a recurring Qur'anic image of divine power and care:

> *"God is He who raised the heavens without pillars that you can see."* (Qur'an 13:2)

The raising of the heaven is a mercy as much as a marvel. The sky sheltered above, holding sun, rain, and air, is the roof of the habitable world. But the commentators noticed something in the *pairing* of the raised heaven with the set Balance. The verse does not merely list two separate acts; it joins the physical elevation of the heavens with the moral establishment of the Balance, suggesting a correspondence between the order above and the order that ought to govern below.

**The Balance: Justice, Law, and Cosmic Harmony**

The word translated "Balance" is *al-mīzān*, from the root *w-z-n*, "to weigh." A *mīzān* is literally the scale used for weighing goods. But the commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and others — understood it here in a far broader sense. Most take *al-mīzān* as a reference to **justice** (*al-ʿadl*): the principle of giving each thing and each person its due. Others take it to mean the revealed **Law** (*al-sharīʿah*), the divine code that establishes right measure in human affairs, or even the **Qur'an** itself, which the Qur'an elsewhere pairs with the Balance:

> *"God it is Who sent down the Book with truth, and the Balance."* (Qur'an 42:17)

> *"Indeed We sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and We sent down with them the Book and the Balance, that mankind may uphold justice."* (Qur'an 57:25)

And beyond these, *al-mīzān* can be read as the **balance and harmony of the created order** itself — the exquisite equilibrium by which the cosmos holds together, from the proportions of the atmosphere to the equilibria of ecosystems. The Balance, in this widest sense, is the principle of *right measure* running through both nature and morality.

**Why Physical and Moral Order Are Named Together**

The genius of the verse lies in its juxtaposition. By naming the raising of the heaven and the setting of the Balance in a single breath, the Qur'an suggests that justice is as fundamental to reality as the sky overhead. Just as God built physical equilibrium into the cosmos — the balanced forces that keep the heavens suspended and the earth stable — so He established a moral equilibrium that human beings are meant to honor. The message is that justice is not a human invention, a mere social convention that could have been otherwise. It is woven into the structure of existence by the same hand that raised the heavens. To violate justice is therefore to violate the grain of the universe, as surely as to defy a law of physics.

This is confirmed by a saying attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, cited in the commentaries: *"The heavens and the earth are founded upon justice."* The cosmos itself, on this view, rests on balance. Remove justice, and existence would collapse into chaos as surely as the sky would fall if its equilibrium were undone.

**The Logic: Balance as the Condition of Existence**

There is a deep rational insight here that resonates powerfully with modern understanding. The universe exists in a state of extraordinarily fine equilibrium. The forces that govern matter, the composition of the air, the balance of the water cycle, the proportion of gases that make life possible — all these subsist within narrow tolerances. Tip the balance and life becomes impossible. Nature is, quite literally, a system of balances. The verse invites the reader to see human justice as the moral extension of this cosmic fact: just as the natural world flourishes only within balance, so human society flourishes only within justice. Oppression, fraud, and exploitation are, in effect, attempts to cheat the moral balance — and like tampering with a physical equilibrium, they eventually bring collapse.

**Application: The Human Vocation to Uphold the Balance**

The verse does not merely describe the Balance; it prepares the reader to be its custodian. The next two verses will command the human being not to transgress in the balance and to set right the weight. The logic is inescapable: because God set the Balance into the very fabric of creation, human beings are charged with preserving it in their own dealings. This has vast application. In commerce, it means honest weights and fair prices. In speech, it means truthful testimony that does not tilt the scale. In relationships, it means giving others their due — their rights, their respect, their fair share. In society, it means systems that do not privilege the strong at the expense of the weak. And in the self, it means the inner balance of a soul that gives each faculty its proper measure, neither starving the body nor indulging it, neither neglecting the world nor drowning in it. To live in balance is to honor, in one's small sphere, the same principle by which God raised the heavens.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:8

> **that you transgress not in the balance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose of the Balance Revealed**

Having stated that God set the Balance, the sūrah now names its purpose in relation to the human being: *"that you transgress not in the balance"* — *allā taṭghaw fī'l-mīzān*. The Balance was not established as an abstract cosmic ornament; it was set so that human beings would honor it and not violate it. This verse turns the description of verse 7 into a moral charge. The equilibrium God built into creation lays an obligation upon the creature endowed with freedom: do not tip the scale that God has set level.

**The Meaning of Taghā**

The verb *taṭghaw* comes from the root *ṭ-gh-y*, which denotes exceeding proper limits, overflowing, tyrannizing, rebelling. It is the same root from which comes *ṭāghūt* (a false object of worship, one who transgresses all bounds) and *ṭughyān* (insolent rebellion). The Qur'an uses it of Pharaoh — *"Go to Pharaoh; indeed he has transgressed (ṭaghā)"* (20:24) — and of the flood that "*overflowed all bounds*" (69:11). To *taṭghaw fī'l-mīzān* is thus not a minor miscalculation but an act of overreach, of grasping more than one's due, of trampling the limit. The choice of this strong word signals that violating the balance is a species of the same insolence by which tyrants and rebels defy God. The merchant who shortchanges his customer and the tyrant who oppresses his people are, at root, committing the same sin: transgressing a God-set limit.

**Justice as a Limit, Not Merely an Ideal**

Notice how the verse frames justice negatively — not "that you achieve the balance" but "that you *transgress not* in the balance." There is wisdom in this framing. Justice is often best understood as a boundary one must not cross rather than a peak one must scale. The Balance is already set by God; the human task is not to invent it but to refrain from disturbing it. This is psychologically realistic. Most injustice does not come from failing to imagine an ideal society; it comes from the ordinary, repeated temptation to take a little more than one's share, to tilt the scale a fraction in one's own favor. By phrasing the command as a prohibition against transgression, the verse targets that everyday temptation directly.

**The Balance in Every Domain**

Because *al-mīzān* was understood broadly in verse 7 — as justice, law, and cosmic harmony — the prohibition here extends across every domain of life:

In **trade**, it forbids false weights and measures, a sin the Qur'an condemns with particular severity:

> *"Woe to the defrauders — who, when they take a measure from people, take in full, but when they measure for them or weigh for them, give less."* (Qur'an 83:1–3)

In **judgment and testimony**, it forbids the tilting of verdicts and the shading of testimony, for these too are weighings:

> *"And let not the hatred of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just; that is nearer to reverence."* (Qur'an 5:8)

In **the natural order**, on the reading of *mīzān* as cosmic balance, it warns against disturbing the equilibrium of creation itself — a warning that reads with startling relevance in an age of ecological imbalance.

**The Logic: Why Transgressing the Balance Harms the Transgressor**

There is a subtle logic worth drawing out. When a person transgresses the balance — cheats, oppresses, takes more than his due — he imagines he has gained. But the Qur'anic view is that he has actually damaged the very order that sustains him. A marketplace in which everyone shortchanges everyone else collapses into distrust and ruin; a society in which the powerful trample the weak breeds the resentment and disorder that eventually consume it; a world in which humanity tips the ecological balance reaps drought, flood, and scarcity. The transgressor is like someone sawing through the branch on which he sits. This is why the Qur'an so often frames injustice as ultimately self-destructive: *"They wronged not Us, but themselves did they wrong"* (2:57). To transgress the balance is to profit for a moment and lose in the end.

**Application: Guarding the Scale in Small Things**

For the reader, this verse turns justice from a grand political abstraction into a daily discipline of the hands and the heart. It asks: In the small transactions of your life, do you give full measure? Do you complete the work you are paid for, honor the terms you agreed to, return what you borrowed, credit others for what they did, refrain from taking the larger portion? The Balance is guarded or violated not chiefly in courtrooms and parliaments but in the countless minor weighings of ordinary life. The verse trains a conscience sensitive to the scale — a person who feels the small tilt of unfairness and refuses to make it, because he knows that the same God who raised the heavens set that balance and is watching over it.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:9

> **So set right the weight and fall not short in the balance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Prohibition to Positive Command**

Where verse 8 forbade transgressing the balance, verse 9 turns to the positive command: *"So set right the weight and fall not short in the balance"* — *wa aqīmū al-wazna bi'l-qisṭi wa lā tukhsirū al-mīzān*. Justice, the verse teaches, has two faces. It is not enough merely to refrain from taking too much (verse 8); one must also actively give the full measure and not give too little (verse 9). Together, the two verses forbid both sides of injustice: taking more than one's due and giving less than another's due. This completeness is characteristic of the Qur'an's moral vision — it closes both doors, the door of grasping and the door of withholding.

**The Vocabulary of Uprightness**

The command *aqīmū al-wazna* means "establish the weighing," "make the weighing stand upright." The verb *aqīmū* is from the root *q-w-m*, "to stand, to be upright," the same root from which comes *iqāmat al-ṣalāh*, "the establishing of prayer." Just as prayer is to be *established* — performed fully, uprightly, not merely gone through — so the weighing of goods and dealings is to be *established* uprightly. The pairing of the word for weighing with the word for uprightness suggests that honest measure is itself a kind of worship, a standing upright before God in one's dealings.

The phrase *bi'l-qisṭ*, "with justice/equity," reinforces this. *Qisṭ* is a rich Qur'anic term for balanced fairness, the just apportioning that gives everyone exactly their right. And *lā tukhsirū al-mīzān*, "fall not short in the balance," uses the verb *khasira*, from the root *kh-s-r*, "to lose, to fall short, to suffer loss." The same root gives *khusrān*, ruin, the loss of the soul. The choice of word is pointed: the one who shortchanges others in the balance is, in the deeper accounting, the one who suffers *loss*. He thinks he is gaining by giving short measure; he is in fact enrolling himself among the losers.

**Threefold Emphasis on the Balance**

The commentators drew attention to a remarkable feature of verses 7–9: the word *mīzān* (balance) appears three times in three consecutive verses. God set the Balance (7); do not transgress in the balance (8); do not fall short in the balance (9). This threefold repetition is not redundancy but emphasis. Repetition in the Qur'an marks fundamental importance, and here it signals that justice is not a peripheral virtue but a cornerstone of the created and moral order. As al-Ālūsī and others note, the triple mention underscores that justice is intrinsic both to God's creation and to the fabric of human transactions. When the Qur'an says a thing once, it is a teaching; when it says it three times in three verses, it is planting a pillar.

**Two Sins, One Root**

Verse 8 addressed transgression — taking too much; verse 9 addresses falling short — giving too little. The commentators observed that these are the two forms of the same underlying vice: the refusal to give others their due. The tyrant transgresses by seizing; the fraud falls short by withholding. Both cheat the balance; both prefer self over fairness. By naming both, the Qur'an forecloses the excuse of the one who says, "I did not steal from him — I merely gave him a little less than I owed." That "little less" is precisely the sin verse 9 names. Falling short is not a lesser crime than transgression; it is the same crime wearing a quieter face.

**The Prophet and the Peoples Destroyed for Cheating the Measure**

The gravity of shortchanging the balance is underscored by the story of the people of Madyan, to whom the Prophet Shuʿayb was sent. Their besetting sin was precisely the giving of short measure, and the Qur'an records Shuʿayb's plea and their destruction:

> *"O my people, give full measure and weight in justice and do not deprive people of their due, and do not commit abuse on the earth, spreading corruption."* (Qur'an 11:85)

When they persisted, they were destroyed. The lesson is sobering: an entire nation was overthrown not primarily for idolatry alone but for the systematic cheating of the balance. This elevates honest measure from a matter of commercial etiquette to a matter of communal survival before God.

**The Logic: Why Full Measure Builds and Short Measure Destroys**

Consider the social physics of the command. A community in which people give full measure is a community of trust — where a person can buy without fear of being cheated, work without fear of being defrauded, and rely on the word of others. Trust is the invisible capital on which all cooperation, commerce, and civilization depend. Every act of giving full measure deposits into that shared capital; every act of falling short withdraws from it. A society that habitually gives short measure erodes its own foundation of trust until cooperation becomes impossible and everyone, cheaters included, is poorer. Thus the command to "set right the weight" is not merely a moral nicety; it is the maintenance of the trust that makes human flourishing possible. The Qur'an's word *khasira*, loss, names exactly what a cheating society ultimately reaps.

**Application: The Discipline of Full Measure**

For the reader, verses 8 and 9 together prescribe a practical discipline: in every dealing, give full measure, and take only your due. This applies far beyond the marketplace. The employee who gives a full day's work for a full day's wage is establishing the weight. The employer who pays fairly and promptly — the Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by Ibn Mājah, *"Give the worker his wages before his sweat dries"* — is establishing the weight. The student who does honest work, the professional who bills honestly, the friend who returns favors, the spouse who carries a fair share of the burden — all are, in their own scales, obeying this command. And the deeper self-interest the verse reveals is this: to give full measure is to enroll oneself among the winners in God's accounting, while to fall short is to suffer *khusrān*, the only loss that finally matters.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:10

> **The earth has He laid down for creatures.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Heavens to the Earth Beneath Our Feet**

The sūrah's survey now descends from the raised heaven and the cosmic Balance to the earth itself: *"The earth has He laid down for creatures"* — *wa'l-arḍa waḍaʿahā li'l-anām*. Having spoken of the sky above and the justice that mirrors its order, the sūrah turns to the ground below, the stable platform on which all earthly life is set. This begins a new sub-section: verses 1–9 named the realities that nourish the spirit — revelation, speech, order, justice — and verses 10–12 now turn to the realities that nourish the body: the earth and its produce. As al-Ālūsī observes, the sūrah moves from spiritual nourishment to physical nourishment, showing that the Compassionate provides for the whole human being, soul and flesh alike.

**The Meaning of Waḍaʿahā — "Laid Down"**

The verb *waḍaʿa* means to set down, to place, to lower and lay out. To say God "laid down" the earth evokes the image of the earth being spread out, made level and settled, prepared as a habitable foundation. This resonates with other verses that describe the earth as a *farsh* (spread-out couch) and a *mahd* (cradle):

> *"[He] who made for you the earth a bed and the sky a canopy."* (Qur'an 2:22)

> *"Have We not made the earth a cradle?"* (Qur'an 78:6)

The earth is portrayed not as a hostile wilderness but as something *arranged for use* — laid out, stabilized, and furnished, the way a host lays out a place for guests. There is care in the verb. The earth was not merely brought into being; it was *set down*, positioned, prepared with its inhabitants in mind.

**For Whom? The Meaning of Al-Anām**

The earth was laid down *li'l-anām*, "for the creatures." The word *al-anām* is somewhat unusual, and the commentators offered several scopes for it. Some, cited by al-Qurṭubī, took it to mean all living creatures — everything that moves upon the earth. Others took it more narrowly as human beings and jinn, the two responsible species addressed throughout the sūrah. Still others included all animals, or specifically all that crawls upon the earth. The breadth of the word is itself instructive: whether narrow or wide, it insists that the earth was made *for* its inhabitants — set down as a provision, not left as a void. The most inclusive reading is the most fitting to the sūrah's spirit of overflowing mercy: the earth is a gift laid out for the whole family of living things.

**The Earth as a Shared Trust**

That the earth was laid down "for the creatures" — for *all* of them — carries an ethical weight the commentators and later thinkers drew out. The earth is not the private estate of any one nation, class, or generation. It was set down for the *anām*, the collective of living beings. This grounds a vision of stewardship: humanity does not own the earth absolutely but holds it in trust, sharing it with the beasts, the birds, the plants, and with the generations yet to come. The Qur'an elsewhere insists that even the animals form communities with their own claims: *"There is no creature on earth, nor bird that flies with its wings, but they are communities like you"* (6:38). To ravage the earth, to poison it, to hoard its resources against the many — these violate the terms on which it was given. The earth was laid down for the creatures; it was not laid down for the greed of a few.

**The Logic: A Prepared Home as Evidence of a Caring Host**

There is a persuasive logic embedded in this simple image. Imagine arriving at a house prepared in every detail for your comfort — the temperature set right, food in the pantry, water running, a bed made, light and air provided. You would infer, without being told, that someone who knew your needs and cared about them had prepared it for you. The earth is exactly such a house. Its distance from the sun permits liquid water; its atmosphere shields and warms; its soil bears food; its cycles of rain and season renew the supply; gravity holds us gently to its surface. Every condition necessary for life is met, and met in advance of our arrival. The believer reads this not as a lucky accident but as the signature of a caring Host — *al-Raḥmān* — who "laid down" the earth for His creatures. The very habitability of the earth is an argument for the mercy that named this sūrah.

**Application: Gratitude for the Ground and Care for the Home**

For the reader, this verse asks for two things. First, a renewed gratitude for the most overlooked of all mercies — the solid, generous, life-bearing earth we tread without a thought. We notice the earth only when it fails us; the verse asks us to notice it while it holds. Second, it summons us to responsibility. If the Compassionate laid the earth down as a shared provision, then to abuse it, waste it, or monopolize it is a betrayal of the gift. The believer walks upon the earth, as the Qur'an says elsewhere, "in humility" (25:63), conscious that the ground beneath him is a laid-out mercy, held in trust for himself, for other creatures, and for those who will come after. The next verses will name what this laid-down earth brings forth — and deepen the case for gratitude.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:11

> **Therein are fruit and date palms bearing sheaths,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earth's Generosity Named**

Having laid down the earth for its creatures, God now names what it yields: *"Therein are fruit and date palms bearing sheaths."* The earth is not a barren floor; it is a living larder, bringing forth the food and delight of its inhabitants. This verse and the next (55:12) itemize the produce of the earth, moving from the delightful (fruit) to the nutritious (grains and herbs), painting a picture of an earth abundantly furnished for the sustenance and enjoyment of those who dwell upon it.

**Fākihah: The Fruit of Delight**

The word for "fruit," *fākihah*, is illuminating in its root. It derives from the same root as *tafakkaha*, "to take delight, to relish, to enjoy oneself." *Fākihah* is therefore not merely food for survival but food for *pleasure* — the sweet, the juicy, the delicious, eaten for the joy of it as much as for nourishment. The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, drew the beautiful inference that this verse alludes to all the blessings of the earth in which people take delight. God did not merely provide the human being with the bland minimum required to stay alive; He provided *delight*. The sweetness of a ripe fruit, its color, its fragrance, its texture — none of this was strictly necessary for survival. It is a surplus of kindness, a mercy beyond the mercy of mere sustenance.

This is a theologically significant point. Some imagine that religion frowns upon earthly pleasure, treating enjoyment as suspect. But here the Qur'an lists *delight* itself — *fākihah*, the food of relish — among the boons of *al-Raḥmān* that it would be ingratitude to deny. Lawful enjoyment of the good things God has provided is not a distraction from gratitude; it is an occasion for it. The Qur'an elsewhere rebukes those who would forbid the good things God made lawful: *"Say: Who has forbidden the adornment of God which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?"* (7:32).

**The Date Palm: The Best of Fruits for the Arabs**

The verse singles out one fruit for special mention: *al-nakhl*, the date palm, described as *dhāt al-akmām*, "bearing sheaths" — the *akmām* being the spathes or protective coverings from which the clusters of dates emerge. Why the date palm specifically? Because for the Arabs to whom the Qur'an was first addressed, the date was the best and most important of fruits — a staple food, a source of sweetness, a preservable provision for travel and lean times, and a tree every part of which was useful. To name the date palm was, for that audience, to name the crown of earthly produce. The commentators therefore read it as a reference to the greatest of God's material blessings, and indirectly as an allusion to all fruits — the finest example standing for the whole category.

**The Date Palm in Prophetic Teaching**

The special status of the date palm is confirmed in the Sunnah. The Prophet ﷺ compared the believer to the date palm in its constant benefit, as recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"Among the trees there is a tree whose leaves do not fall, and it is like the Muslim. Tell me what it is."* The Companions guessed at various trees, and the Prophet ﷺ said, *"It is the date palm."*

He also taught the breaking of the fast with dates, and praised the household that has dates: *"A household that has no dates, its people are hungry."* (Muslim). The prominence of the date palm in this verse thus resonates through the whole tradition: it is the emblem of wholesome, God-given provision.

**The Logic: Beauty and Delight as Signs of a Generous Giver**

There is a rational argument folded into the mention of *delight* here. A purely utilitarian universe — one designed by an indifferent force merely to perpetuate life — would have no particular reason to make food *delicious*, fruit *beautiful*, or the world *pleasant*. Nutrients could be delivered in tasteless, colorless form. That the world instead overflows with flavor, fragrance, color, and delight points beyond bare function toward *generosity* — a Giver who wished His creatures not merely to survive but to enjoy. The sweetness of the date, the perfume of ripe fruit, the visual glory of an orchard in season — these are, in the sūrah's logic, love letters from *al-Raḥmān*, gratuitous kindnesses that reveal the character of the Giver. The atheist eats the fruit and tastes only chemistry; the believer eats it and tastes mercy.

**Application: Eating with Awareness**

For the reader, this verse transforms the ordinary act of eating fruit into an occasion of remembrance. Every time we bite into something sweet and delicious, we are consuming a mercy the verse specifically names. The believer is taught to begin eating with *bismi'llāh*, "in the Name of God," and to end with *al-ḥamdu li'llāh*, "praise be to God" — bracketing the pleasure with acknowledgment of its Source. This is the antidote to the modern condition of mindless consumption, in which we eat abundance without a flicker of gratitude and take endless delight without a thought for the Giver. The verse asks us to slow down and taste with awareness: this sweetness was provided; this delight was a gift; and the One who gave it is *al-Raḥmān*.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:12

> **husked grains and fragrant herbs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nourishing Complement to Delight**

Where verse 11 named the fruit of *delight*, verse 12 names the produce of *nourishment* and *fragrance*: *"husked grains and fragrant herbs"* — *wa'l-ḥabbu dhū al-ʿaṣfi wa'l-rayḥān*. Together the two verses portray a balanced provision: the sweet fruit that pleases and the sturdy grain that sustains, and beyond both, the fragrant herbs that delight yet another sense. The commentators, including al-Ālūsī, noted this complementarity: verse 11 speaks to the earthly blessings people relish, and verse 12 to the more nutritive dimension of food, the staples that feed the body's daily need. The Compassionate provides both the luxury and the necessity.

**Al-Ḥabb Dhū al-ʿAṣf: The Grain with Its Chaff**

The phrase *al-ḥabbu dhū al-ʿaṣf* means "the grain possessing *ʿaṣf*." *Ḥabb* is the grain or seed — wheat, barley, and the like, the staple cereals on which human civilization has always chiefly depended. *ʿAṣf* is understood variously as the husk, the chaff, the dry stalk, or the leaves and blades of the grain plant. Some commentators, cited by al-Qurṭubī, took *ʿaṣf* to mean the stems and straw of the plant, which serve as fodder for livestock. On this reading the verse names a double provision in a single plant: the grain feeds human beings, and the stalk and chaff feed their animals. Nothing is wasted; the same plant nourishes two levels of creation. This is a mercy of design — the staple crop that feeds humanity also, in its remainder, feeds the beasts that serve humanity.

The mention of the *husk* specifically may also point to the way grain comes protected, wrapped in its own covering that preserves it until it is threshed and prepared — another quiet evidence of provision arranged with care, the seed sheltered until harvest.

**Al-Rayḥān: The Fragrant Herb**

The verse closes with *al-rayḥān*, "fragrant herbs" — sweet-smelling plants, aromatic basil and its kin. The word derives from the root *r-w-ḥ*, connected to *rīḥ* (wind, scent) and *rawḥ* (comfort, refreshment). *Rayḥān* is the plant that gives forth fragrance, the scent that refreshes and comforts. Some commentators read *rayḥān* more broadly as the leaves of plants (paired with *ʿaṣf* as the stems), or even as provision generally, since in some Arabic usage *rayḥān* meant sustenance and God's bounty. But its most evocative meaning is the aromatic herb — and its inclusion here is significant.

**Why Fragrance Belongs in the List of Mercies**

Consider what the naming of *fragrance* among the boons of God implies. The list could have stopped at grain — the practical necessity of survival. But it does not. It adds *rayḥān*, the sweet scent that serves no nutritional purpose at all, that fills no belly and builds no muscle. Fragrance is pure grace, a mercy aimed not at the body's survival but at the soul's refreshment. That God includes it among the blessings the human being is warned not to deny reveals, once again, the character of *al-Raḥmān*: a Giver who cares not only that His creatures live, but that they are *comforted*, *pleased*, and *delighted*. The scent of a garden after rain, of basil crushed between the fingers, of blossom on the breeze — these are gratuitous kindnesses, and the verse insists they be recognized as such.

The Prophet ﷺ himself loved good fragrance. It is recorded in the collections that among the things made beloved to him in this world were women, perfume, and prayer as the coolness of his eye (an-Nasāʾī). And he taught, as recorded by Muslim, that one should not refuse a gift of fragrant herbs (*rayḥān*): *"Whoever is offered rayḥān should not refuse it, for it is light to carry and sweet of scent."* The prophetic appreciation of fragrance mirrors its dignified place in this verse.

**The Logic: A Provision Scaled to the Whole Human Being**

The progression of verses 11–12 reveals a designed completeness. The human being is not merely a stomach to be filled; he is a creature of many senses and needs. So the provision is scaled to the whole of him: the sweet fruit for delight (taste), the staple grain for strength (sustenance), the fragrant herb for refreshment (scent) — and by extension the whole sensory richness of the natural world. A God who provided only calories would have provided for the animal in us; a God who provides delight, strength, and fragrance together provides for the *human* in us, the creature of taste, appetite, and aesthetic longing. The comprehensiveness of the provision matches the comprehensiveness of the creature — further evidence that the Provider knows intimately the being He provides for.

**Application: A Life Awake to Small Mercies**

For the reader, verses 10–12 together are a school of attentiveness. They train the eye to see, in the ordinary furniture of the world — solid ground, ripe fruit, daily bread, sweet scent — a catalogue of deliberate mercies. Modern life, with its supermarkets and conveniences, has largely severed us from the astonishment of provision; we forget that bread begins as grain in the earth, that fruit is a yearly gift no factory could manufacture, that fragrance is a grace we did nothing to earn. This verse, and the refrain that immediately follows it, asks us to wake up to what we consume — to recognize that the loaf, the date, the sprig of basil are all fingerprints of the Compassionate, and that a life lived without noticing them is a life of quiet, comprehensive ingratitude. The very next verse will press exactly this question.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:13

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Arrives**

For the first time, the sūrah's great refrain appears: *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — *fa-bi-ayyi ālāʾi rabbikumā tukadhdhibān*. From this point it will recur thirty-one times, threading through the whole chapter like a returning chorus. Its first occurrence comes precisely here, immediately after the catalogue of earthly blessings — revelation, speech, sun and moon, the raised heaven and the set Balance, the laid-down earth, and its fruit, grain, and fragrant herbs. The placement is deliberate: having laid a table of mercies before the reader, God now asks, with the accumulated force of everything just named, *which single one of these could you possibly deny?*

**The Meaning of Ālāʾ**

The word *ālāʾ* is the plural of *ilā* (or *aliyy*), meaning favors, bounties, blessings, boons. It denotes specifically the gifts and kindnesses bestowed — not power or majesty, but *benevolence in action*. The refrain does not ask, "Which of your Lord's powers do you deny?" or "Which of His threats?" It asks about His *ālāʾ*, His gifts. This keeps the whole sūrah in the register of gratitude. Even when later verses turn to the Fire and the terrors of Judgment, the refrain will frame even those as, in a sense, among God's *ālāʾ* — for warning is a gift, and justice is a favor to the wronged. But in its first appearance, following the blessings of earth, the meaning is plain and overwhelming: look at all He has given; which of it will you deny?

**The Dual Address: Humankind and the Jinn**

The most striking grammatical feature of the refrain is its consistent use of the dual form. *Rabbikumā*, "your Lord," is dual — "the Lord of you two." *Tukadhdhibān*, "you two deny," is dual. Throughout the tradition, these two addressees are understood as **humankind and the jinn** — the two species of creation endowed with intellect, free will, and moral responsibility, the two burdened with the choice to believe or deny. This dual address runs through the entire sūrah and is one of its defining features, matching the pervasive theme of duality (two easts, two seas, two gardens, and so on). The sūrah speaks to the whole community of responsible beings, seen and unseen, reminding both that they stand under the same obligation of gratitude to the same Lord.

The involvement of the jinn is confirmed by a moving report about how they received this very sūrah. It is recorded that the Prophet ﷺ recited Sūrat al-Raḥmān to a gathering of his Companions and, finding them silent, said:

> *"Why do I see you silent? The jinn responded better than you. Not once did I recite to them, 'So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?' but that they replied, 'There is nothing among Your blessings, our Lord, that we deny. Unto You be praise.'"* (recorded by al-Tirmidhī and others; cited by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ālūsī)
