The report models the fitting response to the refrain: not silence, but the acknowledgment that not one of God's blessings can honestly be denied.

**The Meaning of Tukadhdhibān — "Deny"**

The verb *tukadhdhibān* comes from the root *k-dh-b*, "to lie, to declare false." *Takdhīb* is to treat something as a lie, to reject it as false. So the refrain literally asks, "Which of your Lord's favors do you two *declare to be false*?" This is subtle and important. There are several ways a person can "deny" a blessing:

He can deny that it *exists* — refusing to acknowledge the gift at all. He can deny its *source* — enjoying the gift but attributing it to himself, to nature, to luck, or to some agency other than God, as the Qur'an says of those who say, *"I was only given it because of knowledge I possess"* (28:78). Or he can deny it by *ingratitude* — receiving it without thanks, using it without acknowledgment, as though it were owed. The commentators note that denial can also mean accepting one aspect of God while rejecting another: believing in a Creator but scoffing at the resurrection, or acknowledging blessings in this world while denying the revelation that came with them. The refrain's open form — *which* of your Lord's favors? — exposes all these forms of denial at once.

**Why a Question Rather Than a Command**

There is deep rhetorical wisdom in delivering this as a *question* rather than a statement or command. A command ("Be grateful!") can be resisted; a statement ("You are ungrateful") can be disputed. But a question — *which* of these gifts will you deny? — forces the listener to actually run through the catalogue in his own mind and try to find one blessing he could honestly reject. And he cannot. Every gift the sūrah names is undeniable: the sun does shine, the earth does bear fruit, speech does distinguish us, the seas do yield their treasures. The question is unanswerable in the only way that would justify ingratitude. By its very form, the refrain traps denial in its own impossibility. The honest heart, running through the list, can only answer as the jinn did: *not one, our Lord; not one.*

**The Logic: Ingratitude as a Kind of Lie**

Notice the profound psychological insight in calling ingratitude a form of *lying* (*takdhīb*). To receive a gift and refuse to acknowledge it is, in effect, to live a lie — to pretend that what was given was not given, that what was earned by another's kindness was somehow one's own by right. The ungrateful person tells himself a false story about his own life: that his blessings are his achievements, his provisions his entitlements, his very existence self-caused. Gratitude, by contrast, is a form of *truthfulness* — the honest acknowledgment of reality as it actually is: received, dependent, gifted. This is why the Qur'an so consistently opposes *kufr* (ingratitude/disbelief, from the root meaning "to cover over") to *shukr* (gratitude). The disbeliever *covers over* the truth of the gift; the grateful believer *uncovers* and confesses it. The refrain, in calling ingratitude *takdhīb*, diagnoses it as a failure not merely of manners but of honesty.

**Application: Answering the Question Daily**

For the reader, this refrain is not a rhetorical flourish to be skimmed thirty-one times; it is a question addressed personally, again and again, demanding an answer each time. Its repetition is a mercy: it gives the heart repeated opportunities to say, with each fresh blessing named, *"None, my Lord; I deny none of it."* The practical discipline the verse teaches is the habit of the recurring inventory — pausing regularly to run through the catalogue of one's own gifts (health, breath, sight, family, provision, faith) and to affirm their Source. The ungrateful heart is not usually one that consciously rejects God's gifts; it is one that simply never counts them. The refrain forces the count. And once the count is honestly taken, denial becomes impossible, and gratitude becomes the only truthful response.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:14

> **He created man from dried clay, like earthen vessels,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Returning to the Origin of Man**

Verse 3 announced that God "created man"; now, after the survey of the heavens and the earth, the sūrah returns to that creation and describes its raw material: *"He created man from dried clay, like earthen vessels"* — *khalaqa al-insāna min ṣalṣālin ka'l-fakhkhār*. The return is purposeful. Having displayed the grandeur of creation — sun, moon, sky, sea, and the fruitful earth — the sūrah reminds the human being, who might grow proud amid such splendor, of his own humble substance. You who walk this magnificent earth were yourself made from its dust and clay. The juxtaposition is a lesson in humility set within a hymn of blessings.

**The Meaning of Ṣalṣāl**

The word *ṣalṣāl* is rich and precise. It denotes dried clay that has hardened to the point that it produces a sound — a ringing or echoing — when struck, like fired pottery or a dry earthen jar. The commentators explain it as "sounding clay," clay dried so thoroughly that it resonates. This is confirmed by the comparison that immediately follows: *ka'l-fakhkhār*, "like earthen vessels" — *fakhkhār* being fired pottery, the baked clay of pots and jars. The image is vivid and deliberately humbling: the human being, for all his intellect and speech, was fashioned from the same stuff as a clay pot. There is even, some noted, a subtle irony in the word — the human tongue, given the noble gift of *bayān* (speech, verse 4), issues from a frame first made of clay that could only "sound" hollowly when struck.

**The Stages of Human Creation**

The Qur'an describes the origin of the human being (through Adam) using several different terms in different verses, and the commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, read these together as describing successive *stages* in the forming of man from earth:

> *"...from dust (turāb)..."* (Qur'an 3:59)

> *"...from a viscous clay (ṭīnin lāzib)..."* (Qur'an 37:11)

> *"...from dried clay, made of molded mud (ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn)..."* (Qur'an 15:26)

> *"...from dried clay, like earthen vessels (ṣalṣālin ka'l-fakhkhār)..."* (Qur'an 55:14)

Reading these in sequence, the commentators traced a progression: first dust, then dust moistened into viscous clay, then that clay aged and shaped into molded mud, and finally that mud dried into *ṣalṣāl*, sounding clay ready to receive form. At that point, the tradition teaches, the human frame was complete as a vessel, awaiting the final act — the breathing into it of the spirit (*rūḥ*) from God, described elsewhere:

> *"So when I have proportioned him and breathed into him of My spirit, fall down to him in prostration."* (Qur'an 15:29; 38:72)

Thus the human being is a union of the lowest and the highest: clay from the earth and spirit from God. The verse before us names the humble half of that union, leaving the sūrah's earlier verses (revelation, speech) to imply the noble half.

**The Meeting of Clay and Spirit — The Full Human Reality**

This dual origin is one of the most profound teachings in Islamic anthropology. The human being is neither pure earth (like the beasts) nor pure spirit (like the angels), but a deliberate synthesis of both. From the clay comes his body, his appetites, his mortality, his kinship with the animal and the mineral world. From the breathed spirit comes his capacity for knowledge, worship, moral choice, and nearness to God. The dignity and the drama of human life lie in this composite: a creature of dust that can nonetheless know its Creator, a vessel of clay that can carry the light of revelation. The verse names the clay to keep the human being honest about one half of himself; the earlier verses named the teaching to remind him of the other.

**The Logic: Humility as the Ground of Gratitude**

Why place this reminder of humble origin within a sūrah celebrating blessings? Because gratitude is impossible without humility. The proud man believes he is self-made and self-sufficient; he feels no gratitude because he acknowledges no gift and no Giver. By reminding the human being that he began as a handful of clay — that his very substance was taken from the ground he treads — the verse dismantles the pretension that stands in the way of thankfulness. A creature made from a clay pot has no grounds for arrogance before its Maker. And a creature that recognizes its lowly origin will be all the more astonished, and all the more grateful, at the heights to which it has been raised: taught the Qur'an, given speech, and made the intended reader of God's Book. The lower the starting point, the greater the mercy of the ascent.

**The Answer to the Question, "What Am I?"**

Every human being, at some point, confronts the question of what he fundamentally is. Modern answers oscillate between two extremes: a materialism that reduces the human being to mere matter — "nothing but" atoms and chemistry, a clay pot and no more — and a self-deifying pride that treats the human self as sovereign and absolute. This verse, read in the context of the whole sūrah, corrects both. To the materialist it says: yes, you are clay — but clay into which the spirit was breathed, clay taught the Qur'an and given speech; you are more than your matter. To the proud it says: yes, you are honored — but the frame you inhabit is sounding clay, like a pot, and to your clay you will return. The truth of the human being lies between reduction and idolatry: a noble creature of humble stuff, dependent on the One who shaped and quickened him.

**Application: Remembering the Clay**

For the reader, the practical fruit of this verse is a settled humility that steadies the soul in both success and failure. In success, the memory of the clay guards against arrogance: whatever you have achieved, you began as dust and will return to it, and every faculty you used was a loan. In failure and grief, the same memory consoles: you are a fragile vessel, not expected to be invincible, and the One who made you from clay knows your frailty intimately — *"He knows best what you are, when He produced you from the earth"* (53:32). And in the daily round, the memory of the clay keeps the ego in its place, so that when the refrain asks which of God's blessings you would deny, you answer from the humility of one who knows he was given even the body that speaks the answer.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:15

> **and He created jinn from smokeless fire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Responsible Species**

Having named the earthy origin of humankind, the sūrah now names the fiery origin of the other addressee of the refrain: *"and He created jinn from smokeless fire"* — *wa khalaqa al-jānna min mārijin min nār*. This verse completes the pair. The refrain addresses two species, humankind and the jinn; verse 14 gave the substance of the first, and verse 15 gives the substance of the second. Man was made from clay, dense and earthy; the jinn were made from fire, subtle and volatile. The two responsible creatures of God are thus fashioned from two contrasting elements — a further instance of the sūrah's pervasive theme of complementary pairs.

**Who Are the Jinn?**

The jinn are a category of created beings distinct from both angels and humans. The word *jinn* derives from the root *j-n-n*, meaning "to conceal, to hide, to be veiled" — the same root gives *janīn* (the hidden embryo in the womb), *jannah* (the garden hidden by its foliage), and *majnūn* (one whose reason is "veiled," i.e., mad). The jinn are so named because they are ordinarily hidden from human sight. They are, according to the Qur'an, beings possessed of intellect and free will, capable of belief and disbelief, obedience and rebellion — and therefore, like humans, accountable to God and subject to reward and punishment. This is precisely why they are addressed alongside humankind throughout this sūrah: they share the human predicament of moral responsibility. An entire sūrah, Sūrat al-Jinn (Chapter 72), records a company of jinn who heard the Qur'an and believed:

> *"Say: It has been revealed to me that a company of the jinn listened and said, 'Indeed we have heard a wondrous Qur'an that guides to right conduct, so we believed in it.'"* (Qur'an 72:1–2)

**Mārij min Nār: The Nature of Their Substance**

The jinn were created *min mārijin min nār*, "from smokeless fire" — or more literally, from *mārij* of fire. The word *mārij* denotes fire in its pure, flickering, flameless intensity — the tip of the flame that burns clear and smokeless, or, on another reading, fire mixed or shot through with a distinct quality. The commentators, including al-Ālūsī, offered a range: pure fire, a substance mixed with fire, or a unique kind of fire. Some read *mārij* as related to *marj*, "mixing" or "confusion," suggesting fire commingled with something. The dominant image, however, is of a smokeless, clear, volatile flame — subtle and swift, in contrast to the heavy, settled clay of man. Iblīs, the jinn who refused to prostrate to Adam, boasted precisely of this fiery origin as grounds for his arrogance:

> *"[Iblīs] said, 'I am better than him. You created me from fire, and You created him from clay.'"* (Qur'an 7:12)

The Qur'an thus presents the jinn's fiery nature not as a mark of superiority but as the occasion of the first arrogance — a warning that origin is no basis for pride, and that the creature who despises another for his substance repeats the sin of Iblīs.

**The Prophetic Confirmation**

The origin of the jinn from fire is confirmed in an authentic ḥadīth recorded by Muslim, on the authority of ʿĀʾishah, in which the Prophet ﷺ enumerated the origins of the three orders of created intelligent beings:

> *"The angels were created from light, the jinn were created from a smokeless flame of fire, and Adam was created from what has been described to you [i.e., from clay]."* (Muslim 2996)

This threefold classification — light, fire, clay — maps the created hierarchy: the angels of luminous obedience, the jinn of fiery volatility, and humankind of earthy substance. Each has its nature; each is tested according to it.

**Why the Jinn Matter to the Human Reader**

Some modern readers are tempted to skim past mention of the jinn as belonging to a pre-scientific worldview. But their presence in this sūrah carries enduring significance for the human being. First, it teaches humility about the extent of creation: the visible world we perceive is not the whole of reality. God has created orders of being we cannot ordinarily see, and human perception is not the measure of existence — a lesson entirely compatible with the modern discovery of countless realities (from microbes to radiation to distant galaxies) invisible to the naked senses. Second, it teaches that moral responsibility is not unique to humankind, which relativizes human self-importance while affirming human dignity: we are one of two responsible species, not the sole center of the moral universe. Third, it prepares for the sūrah's recurring dual address, giving weight to the refrain's insistence that *both* species owe gratitude to the same Lord.

**The Logic: Two Elements, One Obligation**

There is a beautiful logic in creating the two responsible species from opposite elements — earth and fire — and then binding them under a single refrain. It teaches that moral obligation transcends nature and substance. Whether a creature is made of heavy clay or subtle fire, whether it is visible or hidden, whether its appetites are of the body or of some other order, it stands under the same duty to recognize and thank its Maker. The gifts differ, the natures differ, but the Giver is one and the debt of gratitude is one. This is why the refrain does not say "which of *your* boons" to each separately but *"which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — one Lord, one bounty, two grateful (or ungrateful) creatures. The sūrah universalizes the moral summons beyond the human race.

**Application: Neither Arrogance Nor Fear**

For the reader, the mention of the jinn cultivates two balanced attitudes. Against *arrogance*: knowing that we are not the only intelligent creatures God made, and that another whole order of beings exists, checks the human tendency to imagine ourselves the sole and supreme point of creation. Against *fear and superstition*: the Qur'an presents the jinn soberly, as creatures under God's power like ourselves, neither to be worshipped, feared excessively, nor sought for aid. The Qur'an condemns those who sought refuge in jinn and thereby "increased them in burden" (72:6). The believer's protection is in God alone, seeking refuge in Him — *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind... from the evil of the retreating whisperer... who whispers in the breasts of mankind, among jinn and mankind"* (114:1–6). The right response to the unseen is neither denial nor dread, but trust in the One who created seen and unseen alike.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:16

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Mystery of Creation**

The refrain returns, this time immediately after the naming of the two great acts of creating the responsible species — humankind from clay (verse 14) and the jinn from fire (verse 15). Each occurrence of *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* draws its particular force from the blessing it follows, and here that blessing is nothing less than *existence itself* — the bringing into being of the very creatures being addressed. The question therefore lands with special weight: the one being asked whether he will deny God's favors is himself one of those favors, a being who owes his entire existence to the Compassionate.

**Existence as the First and Greatest Boon**

There is a profound rhetorical strategy in placing the refrain here. Before this point, the sūrah listed blessings *external* to the human being — the sun, the earth, the fruit. Now it names the blessing that is the human being. And it asks: will you deny even *this*? Will the creature deny the gift of its own creation? The absurdity of such denial is self-evident, and that is precisely the point. Every other blessing can only be received because one first exists to receive it. Existence is the ground of all gifts, the gift that makes all gifts possible. To be ungrateful for one's own creation is the deepest ingratitude of all, for it is to spurn the very foundation on which every other blessing rests.

The Qur'an poses this exact challenge elsewhere, marveling at the ingratitude of a creature who was nothing and was given being:

> *"How can you disbelieve in God, when you were dead and He gave you life; then He will cause you to die, then bring you to life, and then to Him you will be returned?"* (Qur'an 2:28)

> *"Has there come upon man a period of time when he was a thing not worth mentioning?"* (Qur'an 76:1)

You did not exist; you contributed nothing to your own coming-into-being; you were, as the Qur'an says, "a thing not worth mentioning" — and then, by pure grace, you were made a knowing, speaking, morally significant creature. Which of *that* will you deny?

**The Dual Address Made Personal**

Here the dual form of the refrain — addressed to humankind and jinn — takes on added resonance, because the two immediately preceding verses named the creation of exactly these two species. The pairing is seamless: verse 14 created the first addressee, verse 15 created the second, and verse 16 turns to both and asks them, in the dual, whether they will deny the Lord who made them from clay and from fire. The two creatures of opposite elements are united in a single question about a single debt. Whatever divides them — substance, visibility, nature — they are joined in owing their existence to one Maker.

**The Logic: The Impossibility of Self-Denial**

Consider the peculiar logic the refrain exploits at this point. For a created being to deny its Creator is a kind of performative contradiction — the denial itself is only possible because the Creator granted the denier the existence, the intellect, and the speech (verse 4) with which to deny. The rebel uses gifts from God to reject God; the tongue that says "there is no Giver" was itself given. This is the incoherence at the heart of ingratitude, and the refrain, placed here, exposes it. The very capacity to deny is a boon among the boons; to use it against its Source is to saw at the branch on which one sits, to spend a gift on the rejection of the Giver. The honest mind, confronted with this, cannot sustain the denial.

**Application: Gratitude for Being**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for the most fundamental gratitude of all — gratitude for simply *being*. It is easy to feel thankful for particular good things (a recovery, a success, a kindness) and to forget the underlying, continuous, staggering gift of existence itself: that there is a "you" at all, awake, aware, capable of reading these words and forming a response. The verse asks us to descend beneath our gratitude for the contents of life to a deeper gratitude for the fact of life. Every breath is a renewal of the gift of verse 14 and 15 — the clay quickened, the being sustained. To pause and feel the sheer improbable grace of one's own conscious existence is to answer this refrain honestly: *not this, my Lord; how could I deny even this?* And from that foundational gratitude, gratitude for every lesser gift flows naturally, as branches from a root.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:17

> **Lord of the two easts and Lord of the two wests.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lord of the Horizons**

The sūrah now names God by a title drawn from the daily motion of the sun: *"Lord of the two easts and Lord of the two wests"* — *rabbu al-mashriqayni wa rabbu al-maghribayn*. Having established that God created the responsible species, the sūrah returns to the cosmic scale, presenting God as the sovereign of the horizons themselves — the One who commands the rising and setting of the sun across the whole span of the year. The title is majestic and precise, and its very phrasing, in the dual, continues the sūrah's insistent theme of pairs.

**Why "Two" Easts and "Two" Wests?**

The obvious puzzle is the dual: there is, to ordinary observation, one east where the sun rises and one west where it sets. Why then "two easts" and "two wests"? The commentators offered several complementary explanations.

The first and most widely favored, noted by al-Ālūsī and others, reads the two easts and two wests as the extreme points of the sun's annual journey along the horizon. The sun does not rise from the same spot each day. Across the year, its rising point migrates north and south along the eastern horizon, reaching its northernmost extreme at the summer solstice and its southernmost at the winter solstice — and likewise for its setting points in the west. The "two easts" are thus the two solstitial extremes of sunrise, and the "two wests" the two solstitial extremes of sunset. This is an observation of real astronomical precision, accessible to the careful naked-eye observer, and the Qur'an names it with exactitude.

By naming only the *limits* — the two extremes of rising and the two of setting — the verse implies the whole range between them. To mention the two boundaries is to encompass everything in between: all the daily rising points that the sun occupies as it travels from one solstice to the other, and all its setting points likewise. This is a known rhetorical device in Arabic: naming the poles to signify the entire spectrum. The verse thus quietly claims God's lordship over *every* sunrise and *every* sunset of the entire year.

A related reading, drawing on the root meanings, notes that *mashriq* (east) comes from *sharaqa*, "to rise (of the sun)," and *maghrib* (west) from *gharaba*, "to set." So "the two easts" can mean "the two places of rising" and "the two wests" "the two places of setting" — again the solstitial extremes, framed by their function rather than mere geography.

**Consistency with the Plural Forms Elsewhere**

The Qur'an speaks of the sun's rising and setting points in the singular, the dual (here), and the plural in different places:

> *"Lord of the heavens and the earth and what is between them, and Lord of the sunrises (al-mashāriq)."* (Qur'an 37:5)

> *"So I swear by the Lord of the easts and the wests (al-mashāriq wa'l-maghārib)..."* (Qur'an 70:40)

The commentators reconciled these by noting that each form captures a true aspect: the *singular* names the direction as such; the *dual* names the two solstitial limits; and the *plural* names the many distinct points of rising and setting throughout the year (some counting a distinct rising point for each day). There is no contradiction, only complementary angles on the same reality — the sun's ever-shifting but perfectly governed traverse of the horizon. Far from being a difficulty, this variety displays the Qur'an's precision: it can name the phenomenon at every level of resolution.

**The Logic: Sovereignty Over the Frame of Time and Place**

Why present God as "Lord of the two easts and two wests" rather than simply "Lord of the sun"? Because the easts and wests are the *framework* within which the sun moves — the fixed reference points of direction, season, and time. To be Lord of the horizons is to be Lord of the very coordinate system of earthly life: the directions by which we orient ourselves (the qibla for prayer is a direction), the seasons by which we plant and harvest, the calendar by which we measure our lives. The migrating sunrise is what produces the seasons; the seasons are what make agriculture, and thus civilization, possible. So this title claims God's mastery not merely over a bright object in the sky but over the entire rhythmic structure that makes ordered life on earth feasible. The One who governs where the sun rises governs the turning of the year and the possibility of the harvest.

**Application: Orientation Toward the One Lord**

For the reader, this verse offers a striking image for the spiritual life. The sun swings between its two easts and two wests, north and south, summer and winter — always moving, never fixed — yet it never escapes the lordship of the One who set its bounds. Human life, too, swings between extremes: ease and hardship, joy and grief, expansion and contraction. And just as the sun's every position is governed, so every state of the believer's life falls within the lordship of the same Lord. There is no season of the soul outside His dominion. Moreover, the verse invites a literal orientation: five times a day the Muslim turns his body toward a fixed direction to pray, submitting his very sense of place to the Lord of all directions. To worship the Lord of the two easts and two wests is to acknowledge that wherever the sun rises and wherever it sets, and whatever season one's life is passing through, one stands within the dominion of a single, sovereign, merciful Lord.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:18

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Ordering of the Horizons**

The refrain returns after the declaration of God's lordship over the two easts and two wests. The blessing being underscored here is the ordered framework of direction, season, and time — the reliable migration of the sun that gives human life its rhythm and its bearings. The question *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* now presses the reader to recognize that even the invisible scaffolding of existence — the coordinate system of east and west, summer and winter — is a favor, and one no honest creature could deny.

**The Boon of Orientation Itself**

It is worth dwelling on how easily this particular blessing is overlooked. We do not usually count "having directions" or "having seasons" among our gifts; they seem too basic, too built-in, to notice. Yet consider a world without reliable orientation — where the sun rose from no fixed quarter, where no season followed another in order, where north and south, planting-time and harvest-time, could not be known. Navigation would be impossible; agriculture would be impossible; the very concepts by which we structure space and time would dissolve. The refrain, placed after verse 17, targets exactly this hidden category of blessing: the mercies so foundational that we mistake them for mere facts of nature rather than gifts. By asking which of them we would deny, the verse pries our attention loose from the obvious blessings and directs it toward the deep structure of a world made livable.

**Denial by Attribution to "Nature"**

Recall that *tukadhdhibān*, "you two deny," includes denying a blessing's *source* — enjoying the gift while crediting it to something other than God. This form of denial is especially tempting with the regularities of the cosmos, which modern habits of mind readily assign to "nature," "physics," or "the laws of the universe" as though these were self-existing agents. The refrain gently challenges this. To say "the earth's axial tilt causes the seasons" is to describe the mechanism, not to name the Author who established the mechanism and holds it in being. The believer accepts the mechanism gladly — the Qur'an itself invites the study of the sun's stations (10:5) — but recognizes behind it the Lord of the two easts and two wests. The refrain asks: having enjoyed the ordered year, will you deny the One who ordered it, hiding Him behind the very regularities He authored?

**The Logic: The Grateful Response to Hidden Order**

There is a quiet argument running through the refrain's placement here. The blessings of verse 17 are not spectacular gifts that seize the attention; they are subtle, structural, easy to take for granted. And the sūrah insists that *these too* fall under the question. The logic is that gratitude which extends only to the dramatic — the sudden rescue, the obvious windfall — is a shallow and incomplete gratitude. Mature gratitude reaches down to the foundations, thanking God not only for the fruit on the table but for the seasons that grew it, not only for the safe arrival but for the very existence of direction by which one traveled. The refrain, by recurring after so structural a blessing, trains the heart toward this depth: to be thankful all the way down, for the frame as well as the picture.

**Application: Seeing the Scaffolding as Gift**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain is an invitation to reexamine what one has silently classified as "just how things are." The reliability of the sunrise, the turning of the seasons, the constancy of direction, the very predictability that lets us plan a life — these are not neutral facts but sustained mercies. The practical discipline is to periodically "make strange" the ordinary: to look at the dawn as if for the first time and recognize it as a gift renewed, to feel the turn of a season as a kindness rather than a mere calendar event. When the foundations of the world are seen as gifts, the honest answer to the refrain becomes inescapable — *none of it, my Lord; I deny none of it* — and gratitude ceases to be occasional and becomes the constant atmosphere of the soul.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:19

> **He mixed the two seas, such that they meet one another.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wonder of the Two Seas**

The sūrah now turns from sky to water, naming one of the most remarkable phenomena of the natural world: *"He mixed the two seas, such that they meet one another"* — *maraja al-baḥrayni yaltaqiyān*. Two bodies of water are described as being set to flow together, to meet — and yet, as the next verse will reveal, they do not merge into one. This is the sūrah's continued celebration of God's ordering of creation through pairs: two easts, two wests, and now two seas, meeting but held distinct.

**The Meaning of Maraja and the Two Seas**

The verb *maraja* carries the sense of releasing, letting loose, setting side by side, or mingling — God set the two seas flowing so that they meet at a common boundary. The "two seas" (*al-baḥrayn*) are identified by cross-reference with a verse that specifies their contrasting natures:

> *"And it is not the two seas that are alike: this one fresh and sweet, pleasant to drink, and that one salty and bitter. Yet from each you eat fresh meat and extract ornaments that you wear."* (Qur'an 35:12)

So the two seas are, on the primary reading, the body of *sweet fresh water* (as of great rivers) and the body of *salt water* (the ocean). Where a great river meets the sea, the two waters come into contact along an extended front — they "meet one another" — and yet, remarkably, they do not immediately blend. The fresh water and the salt water maintain their distinct properties across a zone of contact, a phenomenon observable at the mouths of major rivers and known to modern oceanography as an estuarine boundary or halocline, where waters of different salinity and density resist mixing.

**The Barrier Between Them (anticipating verse 20)**

Verse 19 states that the seas meet; verse 20 will state that between them lies a barrier they do not transgress. The commentators, including Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, took this to mean that the two seas, though they meet, do not overwhelm or dilute one another — each retains its character across the boundary. The "barrier" (*barzakh*) is not a visible wall but an unseen boundary of physical properties (density, salinity, temperature) that keeps the two waters distinct even as they touch. This is a striking observation for a text of the seventh century, and believers have long seen in it a sign of the Qur'an's origin — a precise description of a subtle natural phenomenon, framed not as a scientific claim but as a testimony to the wisdom and power of the One who "mixed the two seas."

**The Layers of Interpretation**

The commentators, characteristically, did not confine the verse to its literal oceanographic meaning but drew out further resonances, each an illumination:

Some read the two seas as an allusion to the *sky and the earth*, the two vast realms that meet at the horizon yet remain distinct in nature.

Some read them as *this world and the Hereafter*, two seas of existence that meet at the boundary of death — the *barzakh*, a word the Qur'an uses precisely for the interval between death and resurrection (23:100) — which stands between them so that neither floods into the other.

And some, in the spiritual tradition, read them as *two kinds of knowledge*: the formal knowledge of the law (*ʿilm*) and the experiential knowledge of the heart (*maʿrifah*), which meet in the complete human being, with the intellect standing between them as a barrier so that neither dissolves the other — the outward law not smothering the inner life, nor the inner life dissolving the outward discipline.

Each reading honors the verse's core image: two distinct realities brought into contact yet held in their proper natures by a God who governs boundaries.

**The Logic: The Wisdom of Preserved Distinction**

There is deep wisdom in this sign of two waters that meet yet do not merge. It teaches that God's mercy operates not only through *union* but through *distinction* — through the maintenance of proper boundaries. If the salt sea and the fresh rivers fully merged, the fresh water on which land life depends would be lost, salted into undrinkability; the boundary that keeps them distinct is itself a mercy, preserving the fresh water as a resource. Nature flourishes not through the collapse of all differences into one undifferentiated mass, but through the ordered coexistence of distinct realities, each keeping its place. This is a principle with vast moral and social echoes: healthy order, whether in an ecosystem or a society or a soul, depends on right boundaries — on things meeting and relating without dissolving into confusion. The God who set a barrier between the seas is the God who sets right limits everywhere, and the maintenance of those limits is not a restriction upon mercy but an expression of it.

**Application: Meeting Without Merging**

For the reader, the image of the two seas offers a model for how to live amid difference. Human life constantly brings distinct things into contact: different people, cultures, roles, and loyalties meet and must relate. The verse suggests that the goal is not always fusion — the erasure of all distinction — but harmonious contact within preserved integrity. A person can engage deeply with the world without dissolving into it, can love others without losing himself, can hold firm principles while meeting those who differ. The believer, like the fresh sea meeting the salt, is called to be in the world yet retain his distinct character — to touch everything without being diluted by everything. And underlying it all is the deeper contemplation: the God who so precisely governs the meeting of the waters governs, with the same wisdom, every boundary of your life, including the greatest boundary of all, the *barzakh* between this life and the next.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:20

> **Between them lies a barrier that they transgress not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unseen Boundary**

This verse completes the wonder begun in verse 19. The two seas meet — and yet *"between them lies a barrier that they transgress not"* — *baynahumā barzakhun lā yabghiyān*. They come together but do not overrun one another. The image is of two mighty waters pressing against each other along a shared frontier, each restrained by an invisible boundary from invading and overwhelming the other. It is a picture of immense forces held in perfect, sustained equilibrium by the decree of God.

**The Word Barzakh**

The key term is *barzakh*, "barrier, partition, isthmus." It denotes a separating limit that stands *between* two things, keeping them apart. The Qur'an uses this same word for the most solemn of boundaries — the interval between death and the Day of Resurrection:

> *"...and behind them is a barrier (barzakh) until the Day they are resurrected."* (Qur'an 23:100)

The choice of *barzakh* here, the very word for the partition between this life and the next, deepens the verse's resonance. Just as an impassable boundary stands between the living and the dead until the appointed Day, so an impassable boundary stands between the two seas. In both cases the barrier is unseen, immaterial, yet utterly effective — a limit set by God that the powers on either side cannot breach.

**Lā Yabghiyān: They Do Not Transgress**

The phrase *lā yabghiyān* means "they do not transgress, they do not overstep, they do not wrong one another." The verb *baghā* denotes exceeding one's bounds, encroaching, committing aggression. The same root gives *baghy*, "oppression, transgression." So the two seas are described almost in moral terms: each keeps to its place and does not *wrong* the other by invasion. The grammatical dual (*yabghiyān*, "they two do not transgress") most naturally refers to the two seas themselves, as Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī hold — the salt does not overwhelm the fresh, nor the fresh dilute the salt beyond the barrier. Some commentators, noting the dual, suggested it might even glance back at the two addressees of the refrain (humankind and jinn), but the primary reference is to the seas.

The moral coloring of the language is significant. By describing the seas as *not transgressing*, the verse implicitly holds them up as a model of ordered obedience — and, by contrast, exposes the human being, who *does* transgress. Recall verse 8: *"that you transgress not (taṭghaw) in the balance."* The seas, mindless and mighty, keep the limit God set for them; the human being, endowed with intellect and warned repeatedly, is the creature who oversteps. The natural world's flawless observance of its boundaries stands as a silent rebuke and a summons to the one creature that violates its own.

**The Barrier as Sustained Mercy**

It is essential to grasp that the barrier is not a one-time act but a *continuous* holding. Every moment, the salt sea presses against the fresh, and every moment the barrier holds. The equilibrium is not self-maintaining inertia but ongoing governance — an expression of the truth stated later in this very sūrah, *"every day He is upon a task"* (55:29), and elsewhere, *"Indeed God holds the heavens and the earth, lest they cease to exist"* (35:41). The fresh water we drink, the estuaries that teem with life, the delicate zones where river meets ocean — all depend on a barrier sustained by God at every instant. Were that barrier to fail, the fresh waters would be lost to the sea. So this verse names not merely a curiosity of geography but a standing mercy on which life continually depends.

**The Logic: Boundaries as the Architecture of Existence**

The two verses on the seas (19–20) together teach a principle that runs through all of reality: that existence is structured by boundaries, and that these boundaries are a mercy, not a mere constraint. A cell is alive because its membrane holds; a body is healthy because its organs keep their distinct functions; a society is ordered because roles and rights are bounded; a soul is sane because reason holds its proper limits over the appetites. Everywhere, flourishing depends on the honoring of boundaries. The verse universalizes this by showing it at the grandest scale — the very seas held apart by a divine limit. And it implies the converse: transgression, the overrunning of proper limits, is the source of ruin. The salt that breached the barrier would poison the fresh; the appetite that breaches the limit of law corrupts the soul; the aggression that breaches the rights of others destroys society. The God who set the barrier between the seas set barriers, too, in His law — and to honor them is to align oneself with the deepest order of creation.

**Application: Keeping the Limits God Has Set**

For the reader, verse 20 turns the contemplation of nature into a personal ethic. The seas do not transgress; will you? God has set boundaries — *ḥudūd Allāh*, "the limits of God," as the Qur'an calls them (2:187, and often) — around human conduct: limits on how we treat wealth, marriage, speech, and one another. These are not arbitrary fences but the moral counterpart of the barrier between the seas, meant to keep the powerful forces of human desire from overrunning and destroying what they touch. The verse invites the believer to look at the disciplined obedience of the seas and take instruction: to keep his own appetites, ambitions, and angers within the limits their Lord has set, so that they meet the world productively without transgressing into harm. And it consoles as well: the same God who so reliably holds the seas apart is reliably present, "upon a task" every moment, sustaining the order of one's life — a God whose governance never lapses, whose barriers never fail, in whom the trusting heart can rest.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:21

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Sign of the Seas**

The refrain returns following the twin wonders of the two seas that meet yet remain distinct, held apart by the barrier that neither transgresses. The blessing being underscored is the ordered equilibrium of the waters — and, more broadly, the whole architecture of preserved boundaries by which the world is made habitable. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* Having beheld a mercy as subtle as the halocline between fresh and salt water, could the reader claim there is no gift here to acknowledge?

**Gratitude for the Precision of Provision**

Each recurrence of the refrain, as noted, draws its color from what precedes it, and here the emphasis falls on *precision*. The seas do not merely exist; they are governed with exactitude — the fresh preserved against the salt so that rivers, estuaries, and drinkable water endure. The refrain thus calls attention to a distinctive quality of God's mercy: it is not crude or approximate but finely calibrated. The believer is invited to be grateful not only that provision exists but that it is arranged with such delicate precision that the boundary between salt and sweet holds firm enough to keep the fresh water fresh. Ingratitude toward such precisely engineered mercy is a denial not merely of a gift but of the wisdom evident within the gift.

**The Human Dependence on the Seas**

There is a practical dimension to this occurrence of the refrain. The two verses on the seas open onto blessings the sūrah is about to name: from the seas come pearls and coral (verse 22), upon the seas travel the great ships (verse 24), and within the earlier verses of the Qur'an the sea yields "fresh meat" to eat and "ornaments" to wear (16:14; 35:12). The sea is a vast storehouse of human provision — food, trade, adornment, and the water cycle that waters the land. When the refrain asks which of God's boons the reader would deny, it does so at the threshold of a whole treasury of maritime mercies. To deny any of these — the fish that feeds, the ship that carries, the pearl that adorns — would be to deny gifts one may have enjoyed that very day, perhaps without a thought for their Source.

**The Logic: Repetition as Persuasion, Not Padding**

A reader encountering the refrain for the fourth time might ask why the Qur'an repeats it so insistently. The answer lies in the psychology of persuasion and remembrance. A single statement of a truth is easily heard and forgotten; a truth returned to again and again, each time attached to a fresh piece of evidence, becomes lodged in the heart. The refrain is like a drumbeat beneath a melody: each verse of blessing is a new note, but the beat returns steadily to drive the rhythm home. And crucially, each return is *earned* by new content — a new blessing has been named, so the question is asked afresh with new force. This is not empty repetition but cumulative argument. By the thirty-first occurrence, the reader has been asked the same unanswerable question over an ever-lengthening list of undeniable gifts, until denial becomes not merely wrong but absurd. The repetition is the method by which the sūrah defeats ingratitude — not by scolding, but by patiently, rhythmically making it impossible to sustain.

**Application: Letting the Refrain Do Its Work**

For the reader, the recurrence of the refrain is an invitation not to skim but to *respond*. The Prophet ﷺ, as the earlier report recorded, expected a response — and praised the jinn for giving one where his Companions were silent. The right way to read this sūrah, then, is not to pass over the refrain as a familiar formula but to pause at each occurrence and actually supply the answer the jinn supplied: *"There is nothing among Your blessings, our Lord, that we deny; unto You be praise."* Read this way, the sūrah becomes a litany, a call-and-response between God and the grateful soul, in which the reader is drawn thirty-one times into an active affirmation of gratitude. The repetition is a gift in itself: thirty-one opportunities, in a single chapter, to train the heart in the habit of thankfulness until it becomes second nature.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:22

> **From them come forth pearls and coral stones.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Treasures Drawn from the Deep**

Having described the two seas and the barrier between them, the sūrah now names the treasures they yield: *"From them come forth pearls and coral stones"* — *yakhruju minhumā al-luʾluʾu wa'l-marjān*. The seas are not merely a wonder of ordered water; they are a source of beauty and wealth, giving up from their depths the precious ornaments that human beings have prized in every age. This continues the sūrah's method: each element of creation is shown to be not only ordered but *generous*, pouring forth benefit for the creatures for whom the earth was laid down.

**Luʾluʾ and Marjān: Pearls and Coral**

The two treasures named are *al-luʾluʾ*, "pearls," and *al-marjān*, "coral stones." Pearls are the luminous gems formed within certain shellfish — objects of beauty that require no cutting or polishing, emerging already perfect from the living creature that made them. *Marjān* is understood by the commentators in two ways: some, such as Ibn Kathīr, took it to mean small pearls, while others took it to mean coral — the red or rose-colored stone-like structures built by tiny sea creatures, prized for jewelry and adornment. Whichever meaning is intended, the point is the same: the sea brings forth, from its hidden depths, objects of surpassing beauty and value, freely offered to those who seek them.

**The Qur'anic Framing of Ornament as Blessing**

This verse connects to a broader Qur'anic theme — that God provides not only necessities but also *adornment*, and that lawful beauty is a blessing to be recognized rather than despised:

> *"And He it is Who has made the sea subservient, that you may eat fresh meat from it, and extract from it ornaments that you wear."* (Qur'an 16:14)

The pairing there of *food* and *ornament* is instructive: God provides both what sustains the body and what delights the eye. As with the *fākihah* (fruit of delight) in verse 11 and the *rayḥān* (fragrant herbs) in verse 12, the sūrah again insists that beauty itself is among the boons of the Compassionate. A God who wished only to keep His creatures alive would not have hidden pearls in the sea and taught human hands to draw them out. The existence of gratuitous beauty — beauty beyond the requirements of survival — is, throughout this sūrah, presented as a signature of a generous and loving Giver.

**Beauty That Costs Effort to Obtain**

There is a subtle point in the phrase *yakhruju minhumā*, "comes forth from them." The pearls and coral do not float on the surface; they lie hidden in the depths, and human beings must *seek* them — the pearl-diver descends into danger to bring up the pearl. The commentators noted that some read the verse as "pearls and coral are brought forth," acknowledging the human labor involved. This teaches something about how God's blessings often reach us: not passively, dropped into idle hands, but through effort, skill, and courage that God has also enabled. The treasure is God's gift; the diving is the human striving; and both together bring the pearl to light. This mirrors the whole economy of provision, in which God provides the fish but requires the fishing, provides the harvest but requires the sowing. The gift and the effort are not opposed; the effort is itself a gift, the God-given capacity to reach the God-given treasure.

**The Logic: Value That Points Beyond Itself**

Why do human beings, across every culture and age, treasure pearls and coral, gold and gems? These objects have little practical use; one cannot eat a pearl or shelter beneath coral. Their value lies almost entirely in their *beauty* and *rarity*. This universal human attraction to beauty is itself a sign worth pondering. It points to something in the human soul that hungers for more than function — a longing for beauty, splendor, and the precious. The Qur'an will shortly show where this longing finds its true and lasting satisfaction: in the gardens of Paradise, whose inhabitants are described as adorned with pearls (*"they will be adorned therein with bracelets of gold and pearls,"* 22:23) and whose companions are compared to "ruby and coral" (55:58). The earthly pearl, precious yet perishable, is thus a small foretaste and a pointer — a reminder that the beauty the heart craves is a created echo of a greater beauty prepared in the world to come. To love beauty rightly is to let it lead the heart back to its Source and forward to its fulfillment.

**Application: Enjoying Beauty Without Being Enslaved by It**

For the reader, this verse models a balanced relationship with material beauty and wealth. Islam does not condemn the enjoyment of lawful beauty — pearls, adornment, fine things — as inherently sinful; the verse lists them among God's boons. But it frames them as *gifts*, which means they are to be received with gratitude and held with an open hand, not worshipped, hoarded, or made into idols. The danger is never beauty itself but the disordered love of it — the greed that turns adornment into an end in itself and forgets the Giver in the gift. The believer wears the pearl and thanks the One who hid it in the sea; he enjoys the beauty and lets it lift his heart toward the greater beauty to come. The refrain that follows will press the point: this beauty, too, is a boon — and which of God's boons could you deny?

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:23

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Sea's Treasures**

The refrain returns after the mention of pearls and coral, the beauty and wealth the sea yields. Here the blessing being underscored is *adornment* itself — the gratuitous beauty God has folded into creation and made available to human beings. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* The question now embraces not only the necessities of survival but the delights that make life rich: the gleam of a pearl, the color of coral, the whole realm of lawful beauty.

**Gratitude for What Exceeds Necessity**

This occurrence of the refrain performs a particular service: it insists that gratitude must extend even to the *surplus* of God's generosity — to the gifts that go beyond what is strictly needed. A grudging creature might concede thanks for bare necessities (food, water, shelter) while dismissing beauty and adornment as trivial or even suspect. The refrain forecloses this. By asking, right after pearls and coral, which boon the reader would deny, it teaches that the beautiful and the delightful are as much divine gifts as the useful and the necessary. To enjoy beauty while denying that it is a gift — or to disdain it as beneath religious notice — is itself a subtle ingratitude. The Compassionate gave both the bread and the pearl, and both call for thanks.

**The Danger of Attributing Wealth to the Self**

Because this refrain follows a verse about *precious things* — pearls, coral, wealth drawn from the sea — it is worth recalling that one of the chief forms of denial (*takdhīb*) is attributing one's provision and prosperity to oneself rather than to God. The Qur'an gives the archetype in Qārūn (Korah), who said of his vast wealth:

> *"He said, 'I was only given it because of knowledge I possess.'"* (Qur'an 28:78)

And the Qur'an's answer was his destruction. The one who grows wealthy and forgets the Source, who credits his own cleverness or effort alone for what God provided, is denying a boon in the precise sense the refrain condemns. The pearl-diver who brings up the pearl, the merchant who trades it, the craftsman who sets it — each used skill and effort, but the pearl, the sea, the skill, and the very hands were all God's gifts. To forget this is to slide into the ingratitude of Qārūn. The refrain, placed after the sea's treasures, quietly guards the heart against the peculiar temptation of the prosperous: to mistake gift for achievement.

**The Logic: Beauty and Wealth as Tests, Not Just Gifts**

There is a deeper logic in the refrain's placement here. Beauty and wealth are among God's boons, but they are also among His tests — for they are precisely the gifts most likely to make a heart forgetful and proud. The Qur'an is explicit that provision can be a trial: *"Your wealth and your children are but a trial"* (64:15). So the refrain, asked after the mention of precious things, does double duty: it summons gratitude, and it warns. It reminds the one enjoying beauty and wealth that these very gifts are the arena in which gratitude is most severely tested, and in which denial is most seductive. The right response — to enjoy the gift while confessing the Giver — is also the response that passes the test. Ingratitude fails it. The refrain thus turns each blessing into a moment of moral truth: what will you *do* with this gift — thank God for it, or forget Him in it?

**Application: The Grateful Wealthy Heart**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain speaks with special directness to anyone who possesses beauty, wealth, or comfort — which, in the modern world, is most of us relative to earlier ages. It asks: do you receive your comforts as gifts or as entitlements? When you enjoy something beautiful or valuable, does your heart turn, even briefly, in gratitude to the One who provided it? Or do you consume beauty and wealth in unthinking assumption that they are simply yours by right? The discipline the refrain teaches is to let every enjoyment of beauty become an occasion of thanks — to see the pearl and remember the Sea's Lord, to hold wealth with gratitude and an open hand, and thereby to transform the very gifts that ruin the ungrateful into the means of drawing nearer to God.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:24

> **His are the ships towering aloft upon the sea like standards.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ships That Ride the Sea**

From the treasures within the sea, the sūrah turns to the human mastery *upon* the sea: *"His are the ships towering aloft upon the sea like standards"* — *wa lahu al-jawāri al-munshaʾātu fī'l-baḥri ka'l-aʿlām*. The great ships, their sails or masts rising like flags or mountains against the horizon, are named as a boon of God. The image is majestic: vessels moving across the vast waters, tall and commanding, bearing goods and people across distances no human could otherwise cross. And crucially, the verse begins *"His are"* (*lahu*) — the ships, and the whole possibility of seafaring, belong to God, are enabled by God, and testify to God.

**Al-Jawārī and Al-Munshaʾāt**

The word *al-jawārī*, "the ships," comes from the root *j-r-y*, "to run, to flow," so ships are named as "the runners" — that which runs across the water. *Al-munshaʾāt*, translated "towering aloft," derives from the root *n-sh-ʾ*, "to raise, to bring into being, to construct." It can mean the ships whose sails or masts are *raised high* — hence "towering aloft, like standards" (*aʿlām*, banners or mountains). But the commentators, including Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, noted that *munshaʾāt* can also be read as the passive participle of *anshaʾa*, "to bring into being" — so that the word evokes *all that has been brought into being*, all created things. On this reading the verse's image of the ship opens outward into a sign of God's creative act as such: the ships He "brings into being" upon the sea are an emblem of everything He brings into being. The single image of a great vessel riding the waves thus carries both a concrete blessing (seafaring) and a hint of the vaster truth (all existence is His bringing-forth).

**Seafaring as a Gift of Enablement**

The naming of ships among God's boons is theologically rich because a ship is, obviously, a human artifact — built by human hands, sailed by human skill. In what sense, then, is it *God's* boon and *God's* possession? The Qur'an answers this elsewhere with precision:

> *"God it is Who made the sea subservient to you, that the ships may sail upon it by His command, and that you may seek of His bounty, and that you may be grateful."* (Qur'an 45:12)

> *"And a sign for them is that We carried their offspring in the laden ship."* (Qur'an 36:41)

The insight is that human technology, far from being a rival to God's provision, is itself a gift *from* God — for God gave the sea its buoyancy, the wind its force, the wood its properties, and the human being the intellect to combine these into a vessel that floats and travels. The ship is a collaboration in which God supplies every element and the human being arranges them. To call the ship God's boon is to recognize that human ingenuity is not self-generated but is itself among God's greatest gifts to humankind. This is a strikingly relevant teaching for the modern age: the airplane, the computer, the entire edifice of human technology, are on this view extensions of the same divine enablement — gifts of the God who taught man what he knew not (96:5) and made the elements of the world subservient to him.

**The Dependence of Seafaring on the Earlier Blessings**

The commentators drew a beautiful connection between this verse and verse 5: *"The sun and the moon are upon a reckoning."* Seafaring across the open ocean, before modern instruments, depended entirely upon the stars, the sun, and the moon for navigation — reading position and direction from the heavens. Without the ordered, reckonable motion of the celestial bodies named in verse 5, the ships of verse 24 could not have found their way. Thus the sūrah's blessings interlock: the ordered heavens make possible the navigation that makes possible the seafaring that brings the trade and travel by which human life is enriched. The gifts of God form a connected web, each supporting the others — a further sign of a single wise Giver behind the whole, not a scattering of unrelated accidents.

**The Logic: Mastery as a Trust, Not a Conquest**

There is an important corrective in the phrase *"His are the ships."* Human beings, having built vessels that cross the seas, are tempted to feel themselves masters and conquerors of nature — to imagine the sea "tamed" by human power. The verse gently reorders this. The ships are *His*; the sea was made *subservient* by *His* command; the human being is not the conqueror of the ocean but the beneficiary of a permission. This is confirmed by the terror the Qur'an describes when that permission is withdrawn — the storm at sea that reduces the proud sailor to desperate prayer:

> *"And when waves cover them like canopies, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion. But when He delivers them to land, some of them are moderate [in faith]."* (Qur'an 31:32)

The ship rides the sea only so long as God permits; the same sea that carries can, at His command, swallow. So the mastery is real but borrowed, a trust rather than a conquest. The believer sails with gratitude and humility, not with the swagger of one who imagines he has defeated the deep.

**Application: Technology in the Hand of a Grateful Servant**

For the reader, this verse reframes the whole modern relationship with technology and human achievement. Every tool we wield — from the ship to the smartphone — rests upon properties of creation we did not make and an intellect we were given. To use technology as a grateful servant of God is to remember, in the midst of our cleverness, Whose sea we sail and Whose gifts we combine. This guards against two errors: the technophobic error of treating human ingenuity as somehow opposed to faith, and the technocratic error of treating human power as self-sufficient and God as unnecessary. The verse holds the balance: build the ship, cross the sea, seek God's bounty — and be grateful, remembering that the ship, the sea, and the mind that joined them are all, in the end, *His*.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:25

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Closing the Blessings of This World**

This occurrence of the refrain holds a pivotal position, for it comes at the end of the long catalogue of *earthly* blessings — revelation and speech, sun and moon, heaven and Balance, earth and its produce, the creation of the two species, the horizons, the seas and their treasures, and the ships that ride them. After verse 25, the sūrah will turn (from verse 26 onward) to the great truth of God's permanence amid the passing of all things, and thence to the Hereafter. So this refrain marks the closing of the first great movement of the sūrah: the survey of the world God made and gave. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — asked now with the full weight of the entire earthly catalogue behind it.

**A Question That Has Become Unanswerable**

By this point the refrain has been asked seven times, and each time the list of undeniable gifts has grown longer. The rhetorical effect is cumulative and, by design, overwhelming. A reader who might have hesitated over one blessing cannot hesitate over the whole accumulated inventory. Revelation, existence, speech, the ordered heavens, the fruitful earth, the treasures of the sea, the mastery of seafaring — which single one of these is not real? Which is not a benefit? Which came from any source but God? The question, repeated over so vast a catalogue, has become rhetorically unanswerable in the only way that could justify denial. This is the culmination of the sūrah's patient method: it does not demand gratitude by command but *produces* it by evidence, piling gift upon gift until the honest heart has no ground left to stand on but thankfulness.

**The Transition It Prepares**

There is a deliberate poignancy in placing this refrain just before verse 26, *"All that is upon it passes away."* The sūrah has spent fifteen verses displaying the blessings of the world — and now, having asked one last time whether the reader will deny any of them, it is about to declare that all of them will pass away. The juxtaposition is profound. It prevents the catalogue of earthly gifts from becoming a snare. Lest the reader, dazzled by pearls and ships and fruit, sink his heart into the world and mistake the gifts for the goal, the sūrah immediately reminds him that every one of these blessings is perishable, and that only the Giver endures. The final refrain of the earthly section thus stands on a threshold: it closes the celebration of the world and opens onto the truth that relativizes the world. Enjoy these gifts, be grateful for them — but do not be deceived, for they pass.

**The Logic: Gratitude as the Bridge Between World and Hereafter**

Why does the sūrah structure itself this way — a long celebration of worldly blessings, sealed by the refrain, then a pivot to God's permanence and the Hereafter? Because gratitude is precisely the bridge between the two. The one who receives the world's gifts with gratitude is drawn *toward* the Giver rather than trapped *in* the gift. Gratitude keeps the heart's ultimate attachment on God, so that when the gifts pass away — as verse 26 promises they will — the grateful soul has not lost its true treasure, for its treasure was never the gift but the Giver. Ingratitude, by contrast, sinks the heart into the perishable, so that when the world passes, the ungrateful soul is left with nothing. Thus the refrain, asked one final time over the whole earthly catalogue, is the hinge on which the sūrah turns from earth to eternity: it teaches the reader to hold the world with grateful, open hands, ready to be led beyond it.

**Application: Holding the World Lightly and Gratefully**

For the reader, this pivotal refrain teaches the art of enjoying the world without being enslaved by it. The believer is not asked to despise the earth's blessings — the sūrah has just spent fifteen verses celebrating them as God's boons. He is asked to *hold them rightly*: to enjoy them with gratitude, to acknowledge their Source, and to remember their impermanence. This is the posture that the next verses will make explicit and that runs through the whole of Islamic spirituality: to be *in* the world without being *of* it, to use the perishable as a means toward the imperishable. When the refrain asks, one last time before the great turn, which of these earthly boons you would deny, it invites you to answer with a gratitude already tinged with detachment — *none of them, my Lord; and I know, too, that all of them are Yours to give and Yours to take, and that You alone remain.*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:26

> **All that is upon it passes away.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Great Turn of the Sūrah**

Here the sūrah reaches its pivot. After fifteen verses celebrating the blessings of the world, a single sweeping declaration cuts through the whole splendid catalogue: *"All that is upon it passes away"* — *kullu man ʿalayhā fān*. Everything — the responsible creatures, the fruits and pearls and ships, the whole teeming world laid down for the creatures — is transient. This verse and the one that follows (verse 27) form the sūrah's structural and theological center, standing between the blessings of earth (verses 10–25) and the blessings of the Hereafter (verses 46 onward). At the very heart of a hymn to God's gifts stands the reminder that all gifts, and all who receive them, will pass — and only the Giver remains.

**The Meaning of Fān**

The word *fān* comes from the root *f-n-y*, "to perish, to come to an end, to pass into non-existence." *Fanāʾ* is annihilation, the passing-away of a thing. To say *kullu man ʿalayhā fān* is to declare that everything upon "it" is, by its very nature, perishing — not merely that it *will* perish someday, but that it is perishable in its essence, marked with mortality from the start. The grammatical construction uses *man* ("whoever"), which in Arabic ordinarily denotes intelligent beings, so the verse most directly names the passing of the responsible creatures — humankind and jinn — while the sense extends, as the commentators note, to all of creation.

**"Upon It" — Upon What?**

The verse says "all that is upon *it*" (*ʿalayhā*) without an explicit antecedent, and the commentators asked: upon what? The most common answer is *the earth* — "all that is upon the earth passes away" — the earth being everywhere implied in the preceding verses (it was "laid down for creatures" in verse 10). On this reading the verse declares the mortality of all earthly existence: every person, creature, and thing upon the face of the earth is passing away. Some read the pronoun as referring to the world or existence in general. The effect is the same: everything created and dependent is transient. The pronoun's very lack of a named antecedent may itself be eloquent — as if to say, whatever you point to, whatever "it" you name, all that is upon it passes.

**A Truth Confirmed Throughout the Qur'an**

This declaration is one of the Qur'an's most insistent themes, stated in many forms:

> *"Everything will perish save His Face."* (Qur'an 28:88)

> *"Everyone upon it will perish, and there remains the Face of your Lord..."* (the very next verse, 55:27, which completes the thought)

> *"Every soul shall taste death."* (Qur'an 3:185; 21:35; 29:57)

The consistency is total: the created order is, without exception, mortal. Nothing in it possesses existence in itself; everything holds its being on loan, moment by moment, and will one day return it. This is not a morbid doctrine but a clarifying one — it names the true status of everything the human heart is tempted to cling to.

**Why the Reminder of Mortality Is a Mercy**

It might seem strange that a sūrah so focused on God's mercy should place, at its very center, so stark a reminder of universal death. But the reminder is itself an act of mercy, and for several reasons. First, it tells the truth, and a soul cannot order its life rightly on a lie; to live as though the world were permanent is to build one's house on sand. Second, it liberates. The one who knows that all things pass is freed from the crushing tyranny of clinging — freed from the fear of losing what was always going to be lost, and from the greed of hoarding what cannot be kept. Third, it redirects. By declaring the perishability of every created thing, the verse turns the heart toward the one Reality that does not perish, named in the next verse — the Face of the Lord. The reminder of death is the door to the love of the Eternal. As the Prophet ﷺ advised, as recorded by al-Tirmidhī:

> *"Remember often the destroyer of pleasures — death."*

Not to poison pleasure, but to keep it in its place; not to darken life, but to keep the heart from mistaking the passing for the permanent.

**The Logic: Impermanence as the Signature of the Created**

There is a rigorous logic beneath this verse that has occupied theologians and philosophers alike. To be *created* is to have received one's existence from another; and what receives its existence from another does not possess existence in itself; and what does not possess existence in itself cannot guarantee its own continuation — it is, by nature, capable of ceasing. Perishability is therefore the very signature of the created, the mark that distinguishes everything that was brought into being from the One who brought it into being. The sun, for all its age, is perishable; the mountains, for all their solidity, will be "as tufts of wool" (101:5); the human being, for all his intellect, "tastes death." Only that which possesses existence necessarily, in and of itself — God alone — is exempt. Thus verse 26 is not merely an observation but a metaphysical demonstration: everything you can name is created, and everything created passes; therefore fix your heart on the one thing that does not.

**Application: Living in the Light of Passing**

For the reader, this verse is one of the great disciplines of the spiritual life: to hold everything in the awareness that it passes. This transforms how one relates to every blessing and every trial. Blessings are enjoyed with gratitude but without desperate clinging, since they were never meant to be permanent possessions. Trials are borne with patience, since they too will pass — *"and it too shall pass"* is, in essence, a Qur'anic truth. Relationships are cherished as precious *because* they are fleeting. Wealth and status are seen for what they are: borrowed robes, to be worn lightly and returned. And above all, the heart's deepest attachment is lifted off the perishable and placed on the Imperishable. The one who has truly absorbed *"all that is upon it passes away"* walks through the world as through a beautiful garden he knows he is only visiting — grateful for its beauty, unafraid of leaving it, his heart already turned toward the One who remains.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:27

> **And there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The One Who Remains**

Verse 26 declared that all things pass; verse 27 names the sole exception: *"And there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty"* — *wa yabqā wajhu rabbika dhū al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām*. Against the universal fading of everything created stands the eternal permanence of God. The two verses together form one of the most sublime statements in the Qur'an: everything perishes; He remains. This is the still point at the center of the sūrah, the immovable truth around which all the passing blessings revolve. Having emptied the reader's hands of every perishable thing in verse 26, the sūrah now fills the heart with the one Reality worth holding.

**The Meaning of Yabqā**

The verb *yabqā*, "remains, endures, abides," is the exact counterpart of *fān* (perishes) in the previous verse. Where creation is *fānin* (passing), God is *bāqin* (abiding) — indeed *al-Bāqī*, "the Everlasting," is one of His Names. The contrast is deliberate and total: the whole of created reality on one side, marked *fān*; God alone on the other, marked *yabqā*. This is the fundamental division of all that exists into two utterly unequal categories: the perishing many and the abiding One.

**The Face of the Lord**

The verse speaks of "the Face of thy Lord" — *wajhu rabbika*. In Arabic, the word *wajh* (face) is frequently used to denote the very *essence* or *self* of a thing — as we might say in English "the face of the matter" to mean its reality, or "for the sake of" someone to mean for their very self. The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, took "the Face of thy Lord" here to mean God's very Being and Essence. It is God Himself who remains — His reality, His self — not merely one part or aspect of Him. The Qur'an uses this expression elsewhere to denote the enduring reality of God and the deeds done purely for Him:

> *"Everything will perish save His Face."* (Qur'an 28:88)

> *"And there will remain the Face of your Lord..."* (55:27)

> *"...seeking the Face of their Lord."* (Qur'an 6:52; 13:22; 18:28)

In the theology of the Sunni mainstream, God's "Face" is affirmed as a divine attribute in the manner that befits His majesty, without likening it to any created face and without denying its reality — the balanced path of affirming what God affirmed of Himself while negating any resemblance to creation, in accordance with *"There is nothing like unto Him"* (42:11).

**A Theological Discussion: Do the Attributes Pass?**

The commentators recorded a subtle theological discussion prompted by this verse. Some theologians who distinguished sharply between God's Essence and His Attributes wondered whether verse 26's "all that is upon it passes away" might imply that even God's attributes pass, leaving only the bare Essence. Al-Rāzī addressed this directly and rejected it: for the verse to mean that, he argued, it would have had to say "there remains *nothing except* the Face of thy Lord." As it stands, the verse affirms what remains (the Face of the Lord) without asserting the annihilation of the attributes. Moreover, the very verse describes that remaining Face as *"Possessed of Majesty and Bounty"* — attributing majesty and bounty to it — which would be incoherent if the attributes themselves were passing away. The verse thus affirms the enduring reality of God together with His perfections, not a stripped-down Essence shorn of its attributes.

**Why "Thy Lord" Rather Than "God"?**

The commentators noticed that the verse says "the Face of *thy Lord*" (*rabbika*) rather than "the Face of God," as some other verses do (e.g., 2:115). Al-Rāzī suggested a reason rooted in the theme of the sūrah: the word "Lord" (*Rabb*) connotes nurturing, sustaining, and bounty — the Lord is the one who provides for and cherishes His servants — and this fits perfectly a sūrah whose entire subject is the enumeration of God's gifts. In a chapter about blessing and bounty, God is named by the title that evokes His role as the loving Sustainer. The choice of "thy Lord" keeps the tone of the verse consonant with the surrounding celebration of His generosity: even in declaring His awesome permanence, the sūrah names Him by the title of His nurturing care.

**Majesty and Bounty: Two Faces of the Divine**

The remaining Face is described as *dhū al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām* — "Possessed of Majesty and Bounty" (or "Honor and Generosity"). This pairing is one of the most beloved of the divine descriptions, and it captures the two great modes in which God relates to creation. *Al-Jalāl*, "Majesty," denotes God's overwhelming greatness, transcendence, power, and awe — the attributes that humble the creature and inspire reverence and fear. *Al-Ikrām*, "Bounty/Honor," denotes God's generosity, kindness, and the honor He bestows — the attributes that draw the creature near in love and hope.
