Together they name the complete divine reality: a God to be both feared and loved, revered in His majesty and adored in His generosity. This dual description is not accidental in a sūrah built on pairs; here the ultimate pair is named — majesty and mercy united in the one abiding Lord. And notably, this exact phrase, *dhū al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām*, will return as the sūrah's final words in verse 78, framing the whole chapter: the majesty and bounty that outlast the earth (verse 27) are the same that outlast even Paradise (verse 78).

**The Prophetic Devotion to This Name**

The Prophet ﷺ singled out this divine description for devoted invocation. It is recorded that he advised his Companions to cling to it in supplication:

> *"Persist in [invoking] 'O Possessor of Majesty and Bounty' (yā Dhā'l-Jalāli wa'l-Ikrām)."* (recorded by al-Tirmidhī and cited by Ibn Kathīr)

And ʿĀʾishah reported that after the completion of his prayers the Prophet ﷺ would often say:

> *"O God, You are Peace, and from You comes peace. Blessed are You, O Possessor of Majesty and Bounty."* (recorded by Muslim)

The pairing of majesty and bounty was thus not merely a doctrine the Prophet ﷺ taught but a Name he lived by, returning to it in his most intimate moments with his Lord.

**The Logic: Why the Heart Needs an Anchor That Does Not Pass**

There is a deep existential logic in the movement from verse 26 to verse 27. The human heart is made to love, to attach, to hold fast to something. But verse 26 has just declared that everything the heart naturally reaches for — people, wealth, beauty, the world itself — is passing away. To attach the heart's ultimate love to what passes is therefore to guarantee ultimate grief, for the beloved will be lost. The only escape from this built-in tragedy is to anchor the heart's deepest love in what does not pass. Verse 27 provides that anchor: the abiding Face of the Lord. This is not a denial of love for created things but a right ordering of it — loving the passing things *within* and *for the sake of* the abiding God, so that even when they pass, the heart's true beloved remains. The one whose ultimate treasure is God can lose everything else and yet lose nothing that finally matters, for his treasure abides. This is the deepest peace the sūrah offers: an anchor beyond the reach of loss.

**Application: Living for the Face That Remains**

For the reader, verses 26–27 together prescribe the whole orientation of a well-lived life. Since all things pass and only God remains, the wise course is to invest one's ultimate self in God — to do deeds "seeking the Face of the Lord," to love and act for His sake, to make Him the abiding treasure while holding all else as passing gifts. This is the meaning of *ikhlāṣ*, sincerity: to orient the heart toward the Face that remains rather than the world that fades. Practically, it asks a question of every act: *Is this for something that passes, or for the One who abides?* The deed done for reputation, wealth, or fleeting pleasure passes with the world; the deed done for the Face of the Lord endures with Him. In a life where everything is slipping away, verse 27 offers the one thing worth holding, and the one way to keep what one holds — to give one's heart to the Majesty and Bounty that will still be there when the sun has burned out and the seas have dried and the last pearl has crumbled to dust.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:28

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Truth of Permanence**

The refrain returns immediately after the sūrah's central declaration — that all things pass and only the Face of the Lord remains (verses 26–27). This placement is striking, for what precedes the refrain here is not, on the surface, a "gift" like fruit or pearls, but a solemn metaphysical truth about mortality and divine permanence. Yet the sūrah asks the same question: *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* The implication is profound: the truth that God endures while all else fades is itself among God's greatest boons.

**When Truth Itself Is the Blessing**

Why should the reminder of universal death and God's permanence be counted as a *boon* (*ālāʾ*) worthy of gratitude? Because the knowledge of reality as it truly is — the knowledge that liberates the heart from clinging to the perishable and anchors it in the Eternal — is one of the most precious mercies God grants. A person who believes the world is permanent will invest his whole heart in what he must inevitably lose, and will be shattered when it goes. A person who knows the truth of verses 26–27 is spared this tragedy: he holds the world lightly and holds God fast, and so he cannot finally be ruined. To be given this truth, therefore, is to be given a map that leads out of a maze in which the ignorant wander to their destruction. The refrain, placed here, teaches that guidance — the gift of true understanding — belongs among the boons no less than food and provision. Indeed, it is the greatest of the boons named in this section, for it concerns not the passing gifts but the abiding Giver.

**Gratitude for the Fear That Saves**

The truth of verses 26–27 carries a certain solemnity, even a note of warning — everything you love will pass; you yourself will pass; only God remains. One might ask how such a sobering truth can be received with gratitude. The answer lies in the nature of a saving warning. Consider a physician who tells a patient a hard truth about his condition so that he may still be cured; the hard truth is a mercy, not a cruelty, and the grateful patient thanks the one who told him. So too the reminder of mortality: it is a hard truth told in time, while there is still opportunity to reorder one's life toward the Face that remains. To be warned before it is too late is a mercy of the highest order. The refrain thus invites gratitude even for the sobering truth — for the warning that saves is as much a boon as the pleasure that delights.

**The Logic: Denial of the Truth as the Deepest Denial**

Recall that *tukadhdhibān* means "you two declare false." Placed after verses 26–27, the refrain exposes a particularly grave form of denial: the denial of mortality and of God's permanence — the refusal to accept that one will die and that God alone endures. This is perhaps the most common and most dangerous denial of all, for a person may live his entire life in practical denial of death, behaving as though he and his world will last forever, and thereby squander the one opportunity to prepare for what abides. The refrain, in its context here, asks the reader whether he will "declare false" this most fundamental truth — whether he will live as a denier of his own mortality and of the Eternal before whom he must stand. To affirm the truth of verses 26–27, to live in its light, is to answer the refrain rightly; to suppress it and live as though the world were permanent is to commit the deepest denial the sūrah names.

**Application: Gratitude for a Sobering Gift**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain teaches a mature and counterintuitive gratitude — thankfulness not only for what pleases but for what corrects. The believer learns to thank God for the reminders of death that pierce his forgetfulness, for the losses that loosen his grip on the world, for the sobering truths that turn his heart from the fading to the Abiding. This is a higher gratitude than thankfulness for comforts alone, and it marks a soul that has grown beyond a merely transactional relationship with God. When the funeral procession passes, when illness humbles, when a beloved thing is lost, the mature believer recognizes in these the same merciful hand that gave the fruit and the pearl — the hand now gently prying his fingers off the perishable so that he may take hold of the One who remains. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not even this, my Lord — not even the hard mercy of the truth.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:29

> **Those in the heavens and on the earth entreat Him: every day He is upon a task.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universal Dependence on God**

Having established that God alone abides while all else passes, the sūrah now draws the inevitable consequence: everything that exists depends utterly and continuously upon Him. *"Those in the heavens and on the earth entreat Him: every day He is upon a task"* — *yasʾaluhu man fī al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ; kulla yawmin huwa fī shaʾn*. All creatures, high and low, are perpetual petitioners at the door of God; and God is perpetually active, attending to their needs, never idle, never absent. This verse follows logically from verse 27: if God is the only one who abides in Himself, then everything else must lean on Him for its every moment of existence and every fulfillment of its needs.

**Everyone Asks — Even the Angels**

The verse says "those in the heavens and on the earth" ask of Him — a scope that includes the angels above and human beings and all creatures below. The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, noted the significance of this: even the angels, exalted as they are, are dependent petitioners before God. Those in the heavens are seen as supplicating on behalf of those on earth (the angels praying for the believers, seeking forgiveness for those on earth, as in 40:7), while those on earth supplicate for their own needs. No creature, however high, is self-sufficient; all "ask." And the asking is not only by explicit words. Every creature "asks" of God by the very fact of its need — the plant "asks" for rain by its thirst, the infant "asks" for nourishment by its hunger, the whole of creation "asks" for its continued existence by its dependent nature. As al-Ālūsī observes, even those who do not call upon God verbally do so implicitly, by the mute petition of their neediness. Existence itself is a form of asking, and God's sustaining of existence is a form of answering.

**Every Day He Is Upon a Task**

The second half of the verse is among the most theologically rich phrases in the Qur'an: *kulla yawmin huwa fī shaʾn*, "every day He is upon a task" — or "in a [new] affair," "engaged in a matter." The word *shaʾn* means an affair, a matter, a concern, a task. The verse declares that God is unceasingly active in the governance of His creation: at every moment He is bringing about affairs — creating, sustaining, giving life, giving death, raising, lowering, granting, withholding, forgiving, answering. The Prophet ﷺ was asked about this "task" and explained its scope, as recorded in the commentaries:

> *"Among His tasks is that He forgives a sin, relieves a distress, raises up a people, and brings low others."* (cited by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī on the authority of the Prophet ﷺ)

The picture is of a God intimately and ceaselessly engaged in the affairs of every creature — not a distant deity who set the world in motion and withdrew, but a present, active Sustainer attending to the countless needs of countless beings at every instant.

**A Refutation of the Absent God**

The commentators noted that this verse stands as a direct refutation of the notion of a God who created and then rested or withdrew. It contrasts sharply with the biblical image of God "resting on the seventh day" (Genesis 2:2). The Qur'anic God does not rest, does not tire, does not withdraw:

> *"...neither slumber overtakes Him, nor sleep."* (Qur'an 2:255)

Were God to withdraw His sustaining activity for even an instant, the commentators observe, all of creation would vanish, for created things have no existence of their own to fall back on. This is confirmed by:

> *"Indeed, God holds the heavens and the earth, lest they cease to exist. And if they should cease, no one could hold them after Him."* (Qur'an 35:41)

Existence, on this view, is not a one-time gift but a continuous outpouring — God is *"upon a task"* every day precisely because creation must be sustained anew at every moment, held in being by the same power that first brought it forth.

**The Deeper Reading: God's Inexhaustible Self-Disclosure**

The commentators of the spiritual tradition drew out a profound meaning from *"every day He is upon a task."* Ibn ʿArabī and others understood it to indicate the endless, never-repeating self-disclosure (*tajallī*) of God — that God never manifests Himself in exactly the same way twice, never discloses Himself in the same form to two individuals nor twice in the same form to one individual. On this reading, the verse names the infinite creativity and inexhaustibility of God: the divine "tasks" never run dry, never repeat, never exhaust the treasury of His generosity and power. Each dawn brings genuinely new affairs; each moment is a fresh manifestation of a wisdom that never repeats itself. As an object of knowledge, too, God is inexhaustible — the more the knower knows of Him, the more remains to be known, for there is no end to His self-disclosure. This transforms the verse from a statement about divine activity into a statement about the boundless, ever-fresh richness of the divine reality.

**The Logic: Why Continuous Dependence Is a Comfort, Not a Threat**

At first glance, the teaching that all creatures depend utterly and continuously on God might seem to threaten human dignity or autonomy. But reflection reveals it as the deepest comfort. Consider: if your existence and every need depended on an impersonal mechanism, or on your own frail efforts, you would have real cause for anxiety, for mechanisms fail and efforts fall short. But the verse teaches that your existence and needs are attended to, every moment, by a Lord who is *al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate whose Name opened the sūrah. The One upon whom you continuously depend is the One who overflows with mercy. Your dependence is therefore not a precariousness but a security: you rest, at every instant, in the sustaining care of infinite compassion. Far from being abandoned to blind forces, you are held. The verse thus turns the fact of dependence into the ground of trust (*tawakkul*): the same God who holds the heavens and the earth from ceasing to exist is the God attending to the smallest of your affairs.

**Application: Bringing Every Need to the One Who Attends**

For the reader, this verse has intensely practical consequences for the life of prayer and trust. If God is "upon a task" every day, ceaselessly attending to the affairs of His creatures and answering the petitions of all who ask, then no need is too small to bring to Him and no distress is beyond His reach. The verse is an invitation to constant supplication (*duʿāʾ*) — to be among those who "ask" not merely by the mute petition of existence but by the conscious turning of the heart to God in every need. It also cultivates a deep restfulness: the believer who knows that God never sleeps, never tires, never withdraws, and is engaged at every moment in relieving distress and forgiving sin, can lay down the crushing burden of imagining himself alone and self-reliant. He asks, and he trusts the One who is always attending. And it cultivates humility: even the angels ask; how much more should the human being. The next verse will ask, as always, which of these boons — the boon of a God who never ceases to attend to us — the reader would dare to deny.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:30

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Truth of God's Ceaseless Care**

The refrain returns after the declaration that all creatures depend upon God and that "every day He is upon a task." The blessing being underscored here is God's *unceasing, active governance* — the continuous attention of the Sustainer to every need of every creature. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* The question now embraces the most fundamental mercy imaginable: that we are not abandoned, that our every moment is attended to by a Lord who never rests from caring for His creation.

**The Boon of Being Attended To**

It is worth pausing on how immense this particular boon is. Every other blessing the sūrah has named — the sun, the earth, the sea, the fruit — is a *thing* given. But verse 29 named something deeper than any thing: the ongoing *relationship* of care, the fact that a Lord is continuously and personally engaged in sustaining us and answering our needs. This is the boon that underlies all other boons, for the sun would not continue to shine, the earth to bear, or the sea to yield, were God not "upon a task" every moment to sustain them. To deny this boon — to imagine oneself alone in an indifferent universe, unattended and unheard — is to deny the very framework within which every other gift is given. The refrain, placed here, asks whether the reader will deny not merely a gift but the Giver's continuous presence and care.

**Denial as the Illusion of Abandonment**

One of the great spiritual afflictions of the human heart is the sense of abandonment — the feeling, especially in suffering, that no one is attending, that one's cries go unheard, that the universe is deaf and cold. Verse 29 directly refutes this affliction, and the refrain that follows presses its refutation home. To "deny" this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) is precisely to succumb to the illusion of abandonment — to declare false the truth that God attends to us every moment. The refrain gently confronts the despairing heart: even now, in your distress, He is "upon a task"; even now, your petition is heard; you are not, and never were, alone. To affirm this boon is to be lifted out of the isolation that crushes the human spirit; to deny it is to condemn oneself to a loneliness that the truth never warranted.

**The Logic: Presence as the Answer to Existential Fear**

There is a deep logic in placing this refrain after the affirmation of God's ceaseless attention. The deepest human fears are, at root, fears of *abandonment* and *insignificance* — the fear that we are alone, unseen, and that our struggles matter to no one. The catalogue of physical blessings (sun, earth, sea) addresses the body's needs; but verse 29 addresses the soul's deepest need — the need to be seen, known, and cared for. And it answers that need with the highest possible assurance: the Lord of all the worlds, before whom even the angels are petitioners, is attending to *you*, every day, in every affair. The refrain then asks: will you deny this? The logic is that gratitude for God's presence and care is the true cure for existential dread. The one who knows he is continuously attended to by infinite compassion cannot be finally abandoned, finally alone, or finally insignificant. His significance is guaranteed by the attention of the One who matters most.

**Application: Living as One Who Is Never Unattended**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for a transformation of one's basic sense of being in the world. Most people move through life with a background assumption of aloneness — carrying their burdens as though no one sees, facing their decisions as though no one guides, enduring their pains as though no one cares. Verse 29 and its refrain summon the believer to replace this assumption with its truth: *I am attended to; I am seen; my Lord is upon a task on my behalf even now.* This changes everything. Decisions become consultations with the ever-present Guide; burdens become shared with the ever-attentive Sustainer; pains become endured in the company of the One who never withdraws. To live in the light of this boon is to walk through the world in the quiet confidence of one who is never truly alone — and to answer the refrain, with each fresh awareness of God's care, in unfeigned gratitude: *not this, my Lord; how could I deny that You are always here?*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:31

> **We shall have leisure for you both, O weighty ones.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Turn Toward the Reckoning**

Having declared His ceaseless activity in sustaining creation (verse 29), God now addresses the two responsible species with a solemn announcement pointing toward the Day of Judgment: *"We shall have leisure for you both, O weighty ones"* — *sa-nafrughu lakum ayyuhā al-thaqalān*. The tone shifts from the sustaining care of the present to the coming reckoning of the future. The verse warns humankind and jinn that a day is appointed when God will "turn His full attention" to them for the purpose of accounting — a reckoning from which there is no escape. This begins a new movement in the sūrah, from the blessings and governance of the present world toward the events of the Hereafter, judgment, the Fire, and finally the Gardens.

**The Meaning of Sa-nafrughu**

The phrase *sa-nafrughu* is rendered "we shall have leisure" or "we shall turn [Our attention] to you." The verb *faragha* literally means to become free from one occupation in order to attend to another, to finish one task and turn to the next. Applied to human beings, it implies leaving off one thing to focus on another. But the commentators, including al-Ṭabarī, were careful to note that when this expression is used of God, it cannot carry its literal human meaning — for God is never "occupied" by one task such that it prevents Him from attending to another. Verse 29 has just declared that God is "upon a task" continuously, attending to all affairs at once, unwearied and undistracted. So *sa-nafrughu* here is understood idiomatically: it expresses that God will devote His full attention to the *reckoning* of humankind and jinn — that the Day of Judgment will come, when the accounting of these two species will be, so to speak, the order of the day. It is a warning phrased in the idiom of undivided attention: the time is coming when you two will be fully addressed and called to account.

**No Task Distracts God from Any Task**

There is an important theological subtlety here, and the commentators drew it out with care. A human being who says "I will get to you when I am free" implies that he is currently too busy — that other tasks are keeping him occupied. But this cannot be God's meaning, for God is not burdened or distracted by the multiplicity of His tasks. The very juxtaposition with verse 29 ("every day He is upon a task") makes the point: God attends to all things at once, and the coming reckoning is not something He must clear His schedule to accomplish. Rather, the verse uses the language of "turning to attend" to convey, in terms human beings understand, the certainty and focus of the coming accounting. It warns the two species not to imagine that God is "too busy" running the vast machinery of creation to bother resurrecting and judging them — a delusion the deniers of resurrection entertained. On the contrary: the same effortless power that sustains the heavens will, without the least strain, raise the dead and call them to account.

**The Weighty Ones**

God addresses the two species by a striking title: *al-thaqalān*, "the two weighty ones" (the dual form). This title, used for humankind and jinn together, was explained by the commentators in two complementary ways. First, they are "weighty" because they are made of substances that have weight and density — unlike the angels, who are made of light. Humankind is of clay (verse 14) and jinn of fire (verse 15), both of which are "heavy" relative to the luminous angels. Second, and more profoundly, they are "weighty" because they *bear the weight of moral responsibility* — the burden of accountability for their deeds. The angels have no such burden, for they do only as they are commanded (66:6); but humankind and jinn carry the heavy trust of free will and its consequences. The Qur'an calls this the *amānah*, the trust that the heavens, earth, and mountains declined to bear, but which the human being took up (33:72). To be called *al-thaqalān* is thus to be reminded, in the very form of address, of the gravity of one's responsibility: you are the weighty ones, burdened with choice, and therefore subject to reckoning.

**The Logic: Accountability as the Dignity and Danger of Freedom**

Why should the two responsible species be singled out for reckoning while the stars and seas, the angels and animals, are not called to account in the same way? Because reckoning is the necessary consequence of freedom. The stars obey without choice; the angels obey without deviation; but humankind and jinn were given the fearful gift of genuine moral choice — the power to obey or rebel, to be grateful or to deny. And where there is genuine choice, there must be genuine accountability, for a freedom without consequences would be an empty and meaningless freedom. The very fact that God will "turn to reckon" these two species is therefore a mark of their unique dignity: they alone among earthly creatures are treated as morally responsible agents whose choices matter enough to be weighed. The "weight" they bear is the weight of mattering. This is both their glory and their peril — the glory of being taken seriously as moral agents, and the peril of being answerable for how they used the freedom they were given.

**Application: Living Toward the Reckoning**

For the reader, this verse instills the awareness that shapes the whole moral life: the awareness that a reckoning is coming, and that one's choices are being recorded and will be weighed. This is not meant to paralyze with dread but to dignify and direct. The knowledge that "We shall have leisure for you both" — that a day of full accounting awaits — lifts human choices out of triviality and invests them with eternal significance. Nothing you do is lost or unwitnessed; every act of gratitude or denial, kindness or cruelty, obedience or rebellion, is being recorded for a day when God will turn His full attention to the weighing. The wise response is to live *toward* that day — to prepare for the reckoning now, while there is time, by filling one's record with the deeds one would wish to find there. The Prophet ﷺ captured this orientation, as recorded by al-Tirmidhī: *"The clever one is he who calls his soul to account and works for what comes after death."* To call oneself to account now, before God turns to the accounting, is the whole art of living as one of "the weighty ones" who knows the weight he bears.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:32

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Warning of Reckoning**

The refrain returns after the announcement that God will "turn to reckon" the two weighty species. This is the first occurrence of the refrain following a verse of *warning* rather than a verse of obvious blessing — and it inaugurates a stretch of the sūrah (verses 31–45) in which the refrain recurs among verses about judgment, the Fire, and the terrors of the Last Day. This shift raises a natural question: how can the reminder of a fearsome reckoning be counted among God's *boons*?

**When Warning Is a Mercy**

The answer lies at the heart of the sūrah's vision of mercy. The commentator al-Nasafī, whose analysis of the thirty-one refrains is preserved in the tradition, observed that a group of the refrains follow verses about the Fire and its severities. That the refrain — "which of your Lord's *boons* do you deny?" — should follow verses of warning teaches that *warning itself is a boon*. To be warned of a coming reckoning, while there is still time to prepare, is one of the greatest mercies a person can receive. Consider the logic: if a reckoning is coming whether or not one is warned, then to be told of it in advance is pure kindness — it grants the opportunity to make ready, to repent, to reform, to fill one's record with good. A God who judged His creatures without any warning would be within His rights, but a God who warns them again and again, sends messengers, reveals books, and describes the Day in vivid detail so that none can say "we did not know" — such a God is *al-Raḥmān*, and His warnings are among His boons. The refrain, placed here, insists that the reader recognize the mercy folded within the warning: the reckoning is a terror to the unprepared, but the *warning* of it is a gift to all.

**The Fear That Protects**

There is a profound psychological truth in counting saving fear as a mercy. Fear, rightly directed, is one of the great protective faculties. The child who fears the fire does not burn; the traveler who fears the cliff does not fall; the driver who fears the collision drives carefully and arrives safely. Fear of a real danger is not a defect but a gift — the internal alarm that steers us away from destruction. The fear of the reckoning (*khashyah*, *taqwā*) functions exactly so in the moral life: it is the God-given alarm that steers the soul away from the deeds that would destroy it. To be given this fear — to have one's heart alerted to the reality of judgment so that one avoids the path to the Fire — is therefore a mercy of the highest practical value. The refrain asks: will you deny even this? Will you dismiss the saving fear as though it were a burden rather than the gift that guards you? The one who welcomes the warning and lets it guide him is protected; the one who denies it walks blind toward the edge.

**The Logic: Mercy That Includes Justice**

The recurrence of the refrain among the verses of warning reveals something essential about the divine mercy: it is a mercy that *includes* justice, not one that excludes it. A sentimental notion of mercy imagines that a truly compassionate God would never warn, never judge, never punish. But the sūrah teaches a deeper truth. A world without justice would be a world in which the oppressor and the oppressed, the grateful and the ungrateful, the faithful and the treacherous, all met the same end — and this would be not mercy but a monstrous indifference, a betrayal of the wronged and a mockery of the righteous. True mercy requires justice, and justice requires a reckoning. The warning of that reckoning is therefore itself an expression of mercy — mercy toward the potential victim who is thereby spared, mercy toward the potential wrongdoer who is thereby given the chance to turn back, and mercy toward the moral order itself, which is thereby vindicated. The refrain, placed among the warnings, holds these together: the God who warns of the Fire is the same *al-Raḥmān* who spread the earth and hid pearls in the sea, and His warnings are as much His boons as His gifts.

**Application: Receiving the Warning with Gratitude**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain teaches a subtle but transformative disposition: to receive God's warnings not with resentment or dread alone, but with gratitude. It is easy to thank God for pleasures and hard to thank Him for warnings; yet the mature believer learns to see the warning as an act of love. When the Qur'an describes the Fire, when a sermon shakes the heart with the reminder of death and judgment, when conscience pricks with the fear of consequence — these are not intrusions upon a comfortable life but mercies breaking through to save it. The right response is the response the sūrah models: to let the warning do its saving work, to reform in its light, and to thank the One who cared enough to warn. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not even the warning, my Lord — for the warning, too, is Your mercy, and I receive it with thanks.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:33

> **O company of jinn and men, if you are able to pass beyond the regions of the heavens and the earth, then pass, you shall not pass, save by a warrant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Impossibility of Escape**

Following the announcement of the coming reckoning, this verse confronts the two responsible species with the utter impossibility of escaping it: *"O company of jinn and men, if you are able to pass beyond the regions of the heavens and the earth, then pass — you shall not pass, save by a warrant."* The scene, as many commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī) understood it, is set on the Day of Reckoning, when jinn and humankind, surrounded on every side by the ranks of angels, are challenged: if you think you can flee God's judgment by breaking out of the very fabric of creation, then try. And the answer is given before the attempt is made: *you shall not pass* — except by an authority you do not possess.

**The Address to the Assembled Species**

The verse opens with a direct vocative: *yā maʿshara al-jinni wa'l-ins*, "O company of jinn and men." Throughout the sūrah the two species have been addressed together in the dual (the refrain); here they are named explicitly and gathered into a single "company" (*maʿshar*) — a crowd, an assembly. The naming makes the address unmistakable and universal: no member of either species is excluded from the challenge. This same form of address appears in Sūrat al-Anʿām, where God reproaches the two species on the Day of Gathering (6:130). The scene is one of total assembly and total exposure: every jinn and every human being, gathered before God, with nowhere to hide and no possibility of flight.

**The Regions of the Heavens and the Earth**

The verse speaks of "the regions (*aqṭār*) of the heavens and the earth" — *aqṭār* being the plural of *quṭr*, the sides, zones, or outermost bounds of a thing. The challenge is to pass *beyond* these bounds — to break out of the entire created cosmos, heavens and earth alike, in a bid to escape God's dominion. The image is one of cosmic imprisonment: the created order itself is the enclosure, and there is no exit from it that is not also within God's power. Even if one could somehow flee to the farthest edge of the heavens or bore through the depths of the earth, one would still be *within* creation, and creation is wholly God's. There is no "outside" of God's dominion to escape to.

**Save by a Warrant — Sulṭān**

The one qualification is decisive: *illā bi-sulṭān*, "save by a warrant" (or "authority, power, sanction"). *Sulṭān* denotes authorizing power, a warrant, a sanction from a higher authority. The verse declares that no creature can pass beyond the bounds of creation except by such a warrant — and the clear implication is that no creature possesses it. Only God grants *sulṭān*, and He grants no creature the authority to escape His judgment. The commentators drew out two layers of meaning here. In the context of the Day of Judgment, it means: you cannot flee the reckoning, for you have no authority to break out of the dominion in which you will be judged; there may be intercessors, but none can intercede *"save by His leave"* (2:255), and none can grant escape. In a broader sense, applicable to this life as well, it means: human beings and jinn cannot penetrate, dominate, or master any region of creation except by an authority and enablement that God grants — every advance into the "regions of the heavens and the earth" is by His sanction, not by any independent power of the creature.

**A Verse That Speaks to the Age of Exploration**

This verse has resonated with readers in an age when human beings have, in a literal sense, begun to "pass through the regions" of the heavens — reaching the moon, sending craft beyond the planets, probing the depths of the sea and the earth. Some have read the phrase *illā bi-sulṭān*, "save by a warrant/power," as pointing to exactly this: that whatever penetration of the cosmos human beings achieve, they achieve only by a *sulṭān* — a power, knowledge, and enablement — that God grants. This reading is entirely consonant with the sūrah's theme (recall verse 24, that even the ships are "His" and seafaring is by His enablement). Whether or not one presses the verse toward modern spaceflight, the principle stands: every reach of the creature into creation is by God's permission and God's provision of the means. Human beings are not escaping God's dominion when they explore the cosmos; they are moving *within* it, by faculties He gave, into regions He made. There is no exit; there is only exploration by permission. And on the Day of Judgment, no *sulṭān* of technology or power will avail to escape the reckoning, for that is precisely the authority God does not grant.

**The Logic: The Futility of Fleeing the Inescapable**

There is a stark and clarifying logic in this verse. The deepest instinct of the guilty is to flee — to hide, to escape, to put distance between themselves and the consequence of their deeds. But the verse exposes the futility of this instinct when the judge is the Lord of all creation. One can flee a human authority by crossing a border into another jurisdiction; but there is no border out of God's jurisdiction, for His dominion is coextensive with existence itself. To flee God, one would have to flee *being* — and one cannot, for one's very being is held in His hand at every moment (verse 29). The Qur'an makes this explicit elsewhere:

> *"There is no refuge from God except in Him."* (Qur'an 9:118, in meaning; cf. the Prophet's supplication, *"There is no shelter or escape from You except to You,"* al-Bukhārī 247)

The only escape from God, paradoxically, is *toward* God — to flee from His justice to His mercy, from His wrath to His forgiveness, while the door of repentance is still open. The verse thus drives the guilty not toward the impossible exit but toward the one available refuge: turning back to God before the Day when turning back is no longer possible.

**Application: Where to Run**

For the reader, this verse reframes the human impulse to escape. When we are burdened by guilt, or fleeing from consequence, or seeking to outrun the truth about ourselves, we instinctively look for an exit — a distraction, a denial, a place to hide. But verse 33 teaches that there is no exit from the reality of God; the only movement available is *toward* Him or *against* Him, not *away* from Him. This is, rightly understood, a liberation. It ends the exhausting and hopeless project of flight and offers instead the possibility of return. The Qur'an's counsel is therefore always the same: *"Flee to God"* (51:50) — not away from Him, which is impossible, but toward Him, which is salvation. The one who grasps that there is no passing beyond the regions of the heavens and the earth "save by a warrant" he does not possess will stop running and start returning — while the warrant of repentance is still granted, before the Day when the only reckoning is the one from which no creature escapes.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:34

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Sealing of Every Exit**

The refrain returns after the declaration that neither jinn nor humankind can pass beyond the regions of creation to escape God's dominion. The context here is sobering — the sealing of every exit, the impossibility of flight from the reckoning. Yet the sūrah still asks, *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — insisting that even this hemming-in of the creature within God's dominion is, rightly understood, among His boons.

**The Boon of an Inescapable Justice**

How can the impossibility of escape be counted a blessing? Consider it from the standpoint of the wronged. In this world, the powerful oppressor often *does* escape — he flees across borders, buys his way out, dies before he can be tried, and evades the justice his victims cry out for. To the victim of unpunished injustice, the deepest anguish is precisely that the wrongdoer got away. Verse 33 declares that before God, no one gets away — there is no border to cross, no warrant of escape. For every soul that has ever been wronged and seen its oppressor go free, this is not a terror but a promise: the tyrant cannot flee; the reckoning is inescapable; justice will be done. The refrain, placed here, invites the reader to recognize the mercy in the inescapability of divine justice — a mercy toward all who have suffered wrong and longed for a court from which the guilty cannot flee.

**Security in a Governed Universe**

There is a further boon in the truth that the cosmos is a sealed and governed dominion. A universe with "exits" — regions outside God's authority, zones of lawlessness beyond His reach — would be a universe in which chaos could erupt from the margins, in which the wicked could find sanctuaries of impunity, in which no order could finally be relied upon. The verse's teaching that there is no passing beyond the regions of the heavens and the earth "save by a warrant" is thus a teaching of total, seamless divine governance: there is no corner of existence outside God's authority, no rogue region, no ungoverned frontier. For the believer, this is a source of profound security. The universe is not a patchwork of God's domain and lawless wilderness; it is, in its entirety, the well-ordered kingdom of one wise and merciful Lord. The refrain asks the reader to be grateful for this — for the security of living in a fully governed cosmos rather than a partly abandoned one.

**The Logic: Denial as Wishful Thinking**

Recall that *tukadhdhibān* means to "declare false." Placed after verse 33, the refrain exposes a particular form of denial: the wishful denial of accountability — the guilty creature's hope that he can somehow slip the net, that there is, after all, an exit, that the reckoning can be evaded. This denial is not a reasoned conclusion but a wish disguised as a belief, the mind telling itself what the desires want to hear. The refrain confronts it: will you *declare false* the truth that there is no escape, simply because you wish there were? The sūrah offers no comfort to this self-deception. But it offers something better than the false comfort of imagined escape — the real comfort of return. The one who accepts that there is no fleeing God, and who therefore turns *toward* Him in repentance, exchanges the anxious fantasy of escape for the solid peace of reconciliation.

**Application: Ending the Flight, Beginning the Return**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for an honest reckoning with one's own instinct to flee. Every human being carries some burden he would rather escape than face — a wrong done, a duty shirked, a truth avoided. The natural impulse is flight: to bury the burden in distraction, to deny it, to run. Verses 33–34 together teach that this flight is both futile and unnecessary — futile because there is no exit from God's dominion, unnecessary because the door of return is open. The believer, grasping this, stops running. He faces what he has fled, brings it to the One from whom nothing is hidden, and finds in the very inescapability of God the ground of his healing: since I cannot flee You, I will return to You; since there is no refuge from You, I take refuge in You. And in that turning, the inescapable justice he feared becomes the inescapable mercy that receives him. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the sealing of every false exit, my Lord — for it drove me home to You.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:35

> **A flash of fire and molten brass shall be sent against you, and you shall not prevail.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Terror That Drives the Guilty**

Having established that there is no escape from God's dominion, the sūrah now depicts the fearsome force that will confront any who imagine they might flee: *"A flash of fire and molten brass shall be sent against you, and you shall not prevail"* — *yursalu ʿalaykumā shuwāẓun min nārin wa nuḥāsun fa-lā tantaṣirān*. The scene, as the commentators understood it, belongs to the Day of Resurrection: when the two species are raised from their graves and might seek to flee the gathering, a blaze of fire and molten metal will be loosed against them, herding them toward the reckoning, and they will have no power to resist or defend themselves.

**Shuwāẓ: The Flash of Flame**

The word *shuwāẓ* denotes a flash, a blaze, a pure smokeless flame — the intense leaping fire that gives light and heat without soot. Its use here is vivid and terrifying: not a distant glow but a blaze sent directly *against* the fleeing creatures (*ʿalaykumā*, "upon you two"). The image recalls the substance from which the jinn themselves were made — *mārij min nār*, "smokeless fire" (verse 15) — a subtle irony: the fiery creatures now face a fire they cannot master. The flame that will be sent against the guilty is not a natural fire to be fled or fought but a divinely dispatched force, an instrument of the reckoning from which verse 33 declared there is no exit.

**Nuḥās: Molten Brass or Smoke**

The second element is *nuḥās*, which the commentators understood in two ways. Some, cited by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī, took it to mean *molten brass* or *liquid copper* — searing molten metal poured upon the fleeing. Others took it to mean the *smoke* of the fire — the choking, blinding smoke that accompanies the blaze. Both readings paint a scene of overwhelming assault: fire and molten metal, or fire and suffocating smoke, loosed together against those who would flee. The pairing of a blazing *flash* (giving light) with dark *smoke* or heavy *molten brass* also captures the totality of the affliction — light and darkness, flame and metal, an assault on every sense with no relief.

**"And You Shall Not Prevail"**

The verse concludes with the crushing finality: *fa-lā tantaṣirān*, "and you shall not prevail" — you two shall have no victory, no defense, no rescue. The verb *intaṣara* means to defend oneself, to gain victory, to secure aid against an attacker. The verse strips away every hope of resistance. There will be no fighting back against this force, no shield to raise, no ally to call, no strategy to escape. The guilty, who in this world may have wielded great power — armies, wealth, cunning — will find all of it utterly useless on that Day. The one who could not "pass beyond the regions" (verse 33) also cannot "prevail" (verse 35): both flight and fight are foreclosed. This is the total helplessness of the creature before the justice of its Lord, once the time for return has passed.

**A Parallel Scene of Judgment**

The Qur'an paints similar scenes of the guilty driven and overwhelmed on the Day of Judgment:

> *"They will be dragged, on their faces, into the Fire. Taste the touch of Hell!"* (Qur'an 54:48)

> *"...when the shackles are around their necks and the chains; they will be dragged into the boiling water; then in the Fire they will be kindled."* (Qur'an 40:71–72)

These parallels confirm the scene of verse 35: a herding, an assault, an inescapable driving of the guilty toward their reckoning and their punishment, against which they have no power at all.

**The Logic: Why the Warning Is Delivered So Vividly**

A reader might ask why the Qur'an depicts the punishment of the guilty in such vivid, terrifying terms. Would not a gentle mention suffice? The answer lies in the psychology of warning and the reality of the stakes. Human beings are moved not only by abstract knowledge but by *vivid* awareness. A distant, abstract statement that "wrongdoing has consequences" rarely changes behavior; a vivid, felt awareness of real danger does. The doctor who wants a patient to quit a deadly habit does not merely mention statistics; he makes the danger real. So the Qur'an, out of mercy, makes the consequence of denial *real* to the imagination — not to torment the reader, but to move him, while there is still time, to the path of safety. And the stakes are ultimate: not a passing harm but an eternal one. A mercy proportionate to such stakes must warn with corresponding seriousness. The vividness of verse 35 is therefore itself an act of *al-Raḥmān* — a warning urgent enough to actually turn a heart before the Day it describes arrives. This is exactly why the refrain will follow: even this terrifying warning is among God's *boons*.

**Application: Letting the Warning Work Now**

For the reader, the right use of a verse like this is neither to dwell on it in morbid dread nor to dismiss it in discomfort, but to let it accomplish its saving purpose *now*. The whole point of a warning delivered in advance is that it can still be heeded. The fire and molten brass of verse 35 belong to a Day not yet arrived; between now and then lies the open door of repentance, reform, and return. The believer reads this verse not as a sentence already passed upon him but as a danger he is being shown in time to avoid. It should produce not paralysis but motion — the motion of turning from the deeds that lead toward such a Day and toward the gratitude, faith, and obedience that lead toward the Gardens the sūrah will soon describe. The one who lets the warning work now will never meet the reality it describes. That is the mercy hidden in the terror, and the reason the very next verse asks: *which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:36

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Vision of the Fire's Assault**

The refrain returns after the terrifying image of the flash of fire and molten brass loosed against the guilty. This is among the clearest instances in which the refrain follows a verse of pure warning, and it presses home the sūrah's remarkable teaching that the warning of punishment is itself a mercy to be counted among God's boons.

**The Mercy of Being Shown the Danger**

Imagine a road along which travelers must pass, and along one stretch a hidden precipice. Two guardians are possible. The first says nothing, letting travelers discover the precipice by falling. The second posts vivid, unmistakable signs — "Danger! Cliff ahead!" — so that every traveler who reads them is spared. Which guardian is the more merciful? The question answers itself. Verse 35 is such a sign, and the refrain of verse 36 asks the traveler to recognize the mercy in the signpost. The fire it describes is real, but the *description* of it, delivered in advance, is a kindness — a warning posted precisely so that the reader need never encounter the reality. To deny this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) would be to resent the warning sign, to prefer the false comfort of ignorance to the saving discomfort of truth. The refrain gently corrects this instinct: the warning is a gift; be grateful for it, and heed it.

**Balancing Fear and Hope**

The recurrence of the refrain among the verses of the Fire also serves a crucial function in the emotional and spiritual balance of the believer. Islamic spirituality has always taught that the believer's heart should be poised between two wings — fear (*khawf*) and hope (*rajāʾ*) — like a bird that cannot fly with one wing alone. Too much hope without fear breeds complacency and presumption; too much fear without hope breeds despair. The structure of this sūrah embodies exactly this balance: the verses of the Fire (verses 35, 43–44) supply the fear, and the verses of the Gardens (verses 46–76) supply the hope, with the refrain threading through both, teaching the reader to receive *both* the warning and the promise as boons. The refrain of verse 36, following the Fire, keeps the wing of fear healthy; the refrains among the Gardens will keep the wing of hope healthy. The mature believer needs both, and the sūrah, in its mercy, provides both — asking, after each, the same question that trains the heart in gratitude for the whole.

**The Logic: Gratitude for Correction as a Mark of Wisdom**

There is a mark by which the wise are distinguished from the foolish: the wise are grateful for correction, while the foolish resent it. The Qur'an and the Sunnah repeatedly note this. The one who loves to hear only praise and pleasant things, and who bristles at warning and rebuke, is on a dangerous road, for he has cut himself off from the very feedback that could save him. The one who welcomes the hard truth, who thanks the one who warns him of his fault or his peril, is on the road of growth and safety. The refrain of verse 36, by asking the reader to count the warning among God's boons, is training him in this wisdom — teaching him to receive the frightening reminder not with the resentment of the foolish but with the gratitude of the wise. A person who can genuinely thank God for the warning of the Fire has attained a spiritual maturity that will guard him from ever needing to meet it.

**Application: The Grateful Heeding of Warnings**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for a specific and practical response: to heed the warnings of God with a grateful, active heart. This means not merely acknowledging the reality of judgment in the abstract but allowing it to shape one's choices — turning from what leads toward the Fire and toward what leads toward the Gardens, and doing so with thankfulness rather than resentment for the warning that prompted the turn. It also means extending the same principle in one's own life: welcoming correction, valuing those who tell one hard truths for one's own good, and refusing the seductive company of those who flatter and never warn. The believer who has learned, from this sūrah, to thank God even for the warning of the Fire has learned the deepest lesson of gratitude — that mercy sometimes wears a stern face, and that the sternness, no less than the sweetness, is a boon of *al-Raḥmān*.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:37

> **When the sky shall be rent asunder and when it shall turn ruddy like hide?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unmaking of the Heavens**

The sūrah now turns from the fate of the guilty to the cosmic upheaval that will announce the Day of Judgment itself: *"When the sky shall be rent asunder and when it shall turn ruddy like hide"* — *fa-idhā inshaqqati al-samāʾu fa-kānat wardatan ka'l-dihān*. The heaven that God "raised" in verse 7, the majestic canopy that has sheltered creation, will be split open and will turn a deep red, like tanned hide or molten oil. This is one of the great signs of the end — the very fabric of the cosmos coming undone as the created order gives way before the Day of its Lord.

**The Rending of the Sky**

The verb *inshaqqa* means to split, to cleave, to be rent asunder. The splitting of the sky is among the recurring images by which the Qur'an describes the cataclysm of the Last Day — the dismantling of the physical order that must precede the new order of the Hereafter. Many verses depict it:

> *"When the sky is split open (inshaqqat)."* (Qur'an 84:1)

> *"When the sky is cleft asunder (infaṭarat)."* (Qur'an 82:1)

> *"And the heaven will split open, for that Day it will be frail."* (Qur'an 69:16)

> *"And [mention] the Day when the sky will be split open with clouds, and the angels will be sent down in succession."* (Qur'an 25:25)

The consistency of these images across the Qur'an teaches that the present, seemingly permanent, cosmic order is in fact provisional — raised for a term, and destined to be unmade when its purpose is complete. The sky we take as the very emblem of permanence and stability will prove as impermanent as everything else "upon it" (verse 26).

**Ruddy Like Hide — Wardatan ka'l-Dihān**

The verse describes the rent sky turning *wardatan ka'l-dihān* — "ruddy like hide," or "red like a rose," or "like molten oil / dregs of oil." The word *wardah* is connected to the rose (*ward*) and to the color red; *dihān* denotes oil, grease, or molten/tanned substances, and by extension the reddish, shifting sheen of hot oil or dyed leather. The image is of the sky flushing a deep, molten, shifting red — glowing and roiling like heated oil or freshly tanned hide. The commentators saw in this a picture of the sky in the throes of its dissolution: no longer the calm blue vault but a churning, blood-red, molten expanse, terrifying in its transformation. Some connected the redness to the intensity of the Day's heat and the reflection of the Fire; others simply took it as the sky's own agony as it is unmade. The sheer strangeness of the image — a red, oil-like, roselike sky — conveys how utterly the familiar order will be overturned: even the color of heaven will change.

**The Question Form: "When...?"**

The verse is phrased as the protasis of a condition — "When the sky shall be rent asunder..." — leaving the reader suspended in the awful anticipation of what such a Day will bring. This grammatical suspension is itself rhetorically powerful: it places the reader *before* the event, contemplating its approach, forced to imagine the moment when the sky itself splits and reddens overhead. The unfinished quality of the sentence draws the heart into the scene and prompts the question every soul must ask: *and where will I be, and what will I have prepared, when that Day comes?* The refrain that immediately follows will complete the movement, but the verse itself is designed to make the reader feel the imminence and gravity of the reckoning it heralds.

**The Logic: Why the Cosmos Must End for Justice to Be Complete**

There is a deep logic in the linking of cosmic dissolution with the Day of Judgment. The present order of the world is one in which justice is incomplete — the oppressor often prospers, the righteous often suffer, and the moral accounts of history remain unsettled. For perfect justice to be rendered, this provisional order must give way to a final one in which every account is closed and every deed requited. The rending of the sky is the sign that the age of provisional justice is ending and the age of final justice has come. The very stability of the present cosmos — which allows the wicked their temporary impunity — must be dismantled for the reckoning to proceed. Thus the terrifying unmaking of the heavens is, paradoxically, bound up with the vindication of justice: the old order, in which wrongs went unanswered, is torn away so that the new order, in which every wrong is answered, can begin. For the wronged and the faithful, the rending of the sky is not only terror but the dawn of the day they have awaited.

**Application: Perspective from the End of the World**

For the reader, contemplating the end of the very sky overhead is a powerful discipline of perspective. So much of human anxiety, ambition, and pride is invested in a world we tacitly assume to be permanent — our status, our possessions, our concerns loom large because we imagine the stage on which they play will last forever. Verse 37 shatters this assumption at its root: the stage itself — the sky, the cosmos, the whole physical order — will be rent and rolled away. Against the backdrop of a splitting, reddening heaven, how small the objects of our striving appear; how urgent, by contrast, the things that will still matter on that Day — faith, gratitude, justice, mercy, the state of one's heart before God. The believer who keeps the end of the world in view lives with a clarified sense of proportion, investing himself in what endures rather than in what will be torn away when the sky turns red like hide. And he prepares, so that when the cosmic order gives way and the reckoning begins, he is found among those the sūrah will soon describe — those who feared standing before their Lord, and for whom two Gardens await.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:38

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Before the Backdrop of the Ending Sky**

The refrain returns after the image of the sky rent open and glowing red — the herald of the Day of Judgment. Once more the sūrah asks its unchanging question against a scene of cosmic terror: *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* And once more the placement teaches that even the reminder of the Day's approach, even the vision of the unmaking of the heavens, belongs among God's boons.

**The Boon of Knowing the End Is Coming**

To be told, clearly and in advance, that this world will end and a Day of reckoning will come is a mercy of incalculable value. Consider the alternative: to live one's whole life under the illusion of permanence, investing everything in a world one believes will last, and then to be overtaken by its end without warning and without preparation. That would be a catastrophe of the highest order — a life entirely misdirected because the truth about its horizon was hidden. Instead, God has disclosed the horizon. He has told us, again and again and in vivid detail, that the sky will split, the world will end, and the reckoning will come. This disclosure is a boon precisely because it allows us to live *toward* the truth rather than against it — to align our lives with reality as it actually is rather than with a comforting fiction. The refrain, placed after verse 37, asks the reader to count this disclosure among God's gifts: the mercy of a horizon revealed, so that the journey may be rightly directed.

**Denial of the Last Day as the Root of Heedlessness**

The Qur'an identifies the denial of the Last Day as one of the root causes of moral heedlessness. The one who denies that the sky will ever split, that the reckoning will ever come, lives as though his deeds have no ultimate consequence — and such a person has no deep reason to restrain his greed, his cruelty, or his denial. The Qur'an repeatedly links wrongdoing to the denial of the reckoning:

> *"Nay, but man desires to continue in his licentiousness. He asks, 'When is the Day of Resurrection?'"* (Qur'an 75:5–6)

> *"...those who deny the Day of Judgment... none denies it except every sinful transgressor."* (Qur'an 83:11–12, in meaning)

The refrain of verse 38, following the vision of the splitting sky, confronts precisely this denial. To "declare false" (*tukadhdhibān*) the reality of the Last Day is not a harmless intellectual position; it is the denial that unmoors the whole moral life, cutting the deed from its consequence and freeing the self to pursue its caprice unchecked. The refrain asks the reader whether he will commit this most consequential denial — and, by asking, calls him back to the sober, life-ordering truth that the Day is real and near.

**The Logic: How the End Gives Meaning to the Present**

Far from draining the present of meaning, the certainty of the end *invests* the present with meaning. If nothing ever came to a reckoning, then nothing one did would finally matter — good and evil would dissolve equally into oblivion, and the moral seriousness of life would be an illusion. It is precisely because the sky will split and the reckoning will come that the present moment is charged with significance: every choice is a deposit toward that Day, every deed a line in a record that will be read. The disclosure of the end is thus the guarantor of meaning. This is why its revelation is a boon: it tells the human being that his life is not a meaningless flicker between two voids but a purposeful journey toward a real destination, in which what he does genuinely counts. The refrain invites gratitude for this — for the gift of a life made meaningful by the certainty of its horizon.

**Application: Living Each Day in View of the Last Day**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain translates into a daily orientation the Prophet ﷺ himself modeled and taught. He advised, as recorded by al-Bukhārī, *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a wayfarer,"* and Ibn ʿUmar who reported it added the practical corollary: *"When evening comes, do not expect the morning; and when morning comes, do not expect the evening."* This is not morbidity but wakefulness — living each day as though it might be the last before the sky splits, and therefore filling it with what one would wish to have done. The believer who receives the disclosure of the Last Day as the boon it is does not fall into either denial (living as though the Day will never come) or despair (living crushed by its terror), but into grateful readiness — ordering each day toward the reckoning, so that whenever the horizon arrives, he is prepared. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the truth of the coming Day, my Lord — for by disclosing it, You gave my life its direction and its meaning.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:39

> **On that Day no man shall be questioned as to his sin; nor shall any jinn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Day Beyond Excuses**

The sūrah now describes a feature of the reckoning that at first seems puzzling: *"On that Day no man shall be questioned as to his sin; nor shall any jinn"* — *fa-yawmaʾidhin lā yusʾalu ʿan dhanbihi insun wa lā jānn*. On the Day of Judgment, neither humankind nor jinn will be *asked* about their sins. This does not mean, as the commentators carefully explain, that there will be no accounting — the Qur'an elsewhere affirms a searching reckoning — but that there will be no *interrogation to establish the facts*, for the facts will already be fully known and recorded. The scene is one of total exposure, in which questioning-for-information has become unnecessary.

**Why No Questioning?**

The commentators reconciled this verse with other verses that speak of the guilty being questioned (e.g., *"So by your Lord, We will surely question them all, about what they used to do,"* 15:92–93) by distinguishing different *kinds* of questioning and different *moments* of the Day. Several explanations were offered:

First, there will be no questioning *to establish guilt or elicit information*, because everything is already recorded and manifest. The deeds have been written down in the records:

> *"And the record [of deeds] will be placed, and you will see the guilty fearful of what is within it, saying, 'O woe to us! What is this book that leaves nothing small or great except that it has enumerated it?'"* (Qur'an 18:49)

When the evidence is complete and undeniable, interrogation is superfluous. The judge does not need to ask "did you do this?" when the deed is written, witnessed, and displayed.

Second, the guilty will not be able to *speak in their own defense*, for their own bodies will testify against them and their mouths will be sealed:

> *"On that Day We will seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will bear witness to what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

> *"...until, when they reach it, their hearing, their sight, and their skins will testify against them of what they used to do."* (Qur'an 41:20)

So the silence of the guilty is not the silence of the unquestioned but the silence of the exposed — they are not asked because there is nothing left to ask, and they cannot speak because their own limbs have taken over the testimony.

Third, some commentators held that questioning of different kinds will occur at different stages of the long Day — at some stages the guilty are questioned (as a rebuke), at others their mouths are sealed and they are not asked. The reconciliation, in Ibn Kathīr's summary, is that the Day is long and its scenes are many; the absence of one kind of questioning at one moment does not contradict the presence of another kind at another moment.

**Questioning as Reproach, Not Inquiry**

A further subtlety the commentators noted: even where the guilty *are* addressed on that Day, it is not to gain information but to *reproach* them. When the Fire's keepers or the angels question the guilty, it is a rhetorical rebuke — "Did there not come to you warners?" (67:8) — not a genuine inquiry. So the "questioning" that does occur is a form of reproach and confrontation with the truth they denied, not a court's attempt to discover facts it does not know. God, who knows all, needs no information; any question He or His angels pose to the guilty is for the guilty's own confrontation with their record, not for the establishing of a case.

**The Total Knowledge of God**

At the root of this verse is a truth about God that runs through the entire Qur'an: God's knowledge is total and perfect. He does not need to ask because He already knows — knows every deed, every intention, every secret. This is why the reckoning is not a trial in the human sense, with prosecution and defense seeking to establish uncertain facts, but a *disclosure* — the unveiling of a record already complete in the knowledge of God and already written in the books. The Qur'an affirms:

> *"...and your Lord is never forgetful."* (Qur'an 19:64)

> *"He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills."* (Qur'an 2:255)

The absence of fact-finding questioning on the Day is thus a direct consequence of the perfection of God's knowledge. The court of the Hereafter is unlike any earthly court precisely because its Judge already knows everything, and its records are infallible.

**A Sufi Reading: The Effacement of Sin**

The spiritual commentators offered a further, inward reading of this verse. From the perspective of the purified soul — one who has passed through the stations of annihilation of the ego (*fanāʾ*) and subsistence in God (*baqāʾ*) — no sin remains to be asked about, for such a soul, having effaced the demands of the lower self, no longer acts from caprice but in accord with the will of God. On this reading, "no man shall be questioned as to his sin" points, for the friends of God, to a state in which the very ground of sin has been removed. This inward reading does not displace the plain meaning (the exposure of the guilty on the Day) but adds a hopeful dimension: the goal of the spiritual life is to arrive at that Day already purified, with a record cleansed by repentance and a heart no longer ruled by the self that sins.

**The Logic: Why a Perfect Record Is Both Terror and Comfort**

The truth that everything is recorded and known — so completely that no questioning is needed to establish it — is a double-edged reality, and its edge falls according to how one has lived. For the one whose record is filled with denial and wrong, the completeness of the record is pure terror: there is no defense, no forgotten deed, no possibility of talking one's way out, for the mouth is sealed and the limbs testify. But for the one whose record is filled with faith, gratitude, and good — and whose sins have been erased by repentance and mercy — the completeness of the record is a comfort: no good deed is forgotten, no secret kindness overlooked, no injustice suffered left unrecorded. The same total knowledge that damns the unrepentant vindicates the faithful. This is why the verse should drive the reader not to despair but to *preparation*: since the record is being written now, and will be read then without possibility of revision, the time to write a record one would wish to see is now.

**Application: Writing the Record Now**

For the reader, this verse turns attention to the record being compiled in the present moment. Every deed, word, and intention is being recorded — by the noble scribes the Qur'an describes (82:10–12), in books that "leave nothing small or great" unenumerated (18:49). On the Day, this record will be disclosed without questioning, without defense, without the possibility of concealment. The only wisdom, then, is to attend to the record *now*, while it is still being written and while repentance can still revise it. This means two things: filling the record with good — gratitude, worship, justice, kindness, the very responses to God's boons this sūrah keeps calling for; and erasing the bad through sincere repentance (*tawbah*), which the Qur'an promises can transform even evil deeds into good (25:70). The one who lives with awareness that his record will one day be read without questioning becomes careful, deliberate, and quick to repent — building, day by day, the record he will be glad to meet when no man and no jinn is questioned as to his sin, because the truth is already fully known.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:40

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Certainty of the Record**

The refrain returns after the declaration that on the Day of Judgment no one will be questioned about their sins, for all will already be known and recorded. The blessing being underscored here is the perfect justice of a reckoning grounded in perfect knowledge — a court in which no truth is hidden, no evidence is lost, and no verdict can err. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**The Boon of Infallible Justice**

The perfection of God's knowledge, which makes fact-finding questioning unnecessary, is one of the greatest guarantees of justice imaginable — and therefore among the greatest boons. Every human system of justice is haunted by the possibility of error: the innocent wrongly convicted because evidence was misread, the guilty acquitted because the truth could not be proven, the witness who lies, the deed done in secret that no court can reach. All these failures arise from the *limits of human knowledge*. The Day of Judgment is free of every one of them, because its Judge knows all things perfectly and its records are infallible. No innocent will be wronged; no guilty will escape through lack of evidence; no secret deed will go unaccounted; no lie will succeed, for the very limbs of the liar will testify. For everyone who has ever despaired of justice in this world — who has seen the guilty go free and the innocent condemned — the perfect knowledge behind the reckoning is a profound consolation and a boon worthy of deep gratitude. The refrain, placed here, invites the reader to recognize this: the infallibility of the divine reckoning is a mercy, the final vindication of justice itself.

**Denial by Presuming to Escape the Record**

To "deny" this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) would take the form of living as though one's deeds were *not* being recorded — as though secret wrongs would remain secret, as though the private cruelty, the hidden dishonesty, the concealed injustice would never be brought to light. This is a denial not of a gift but of the accountability that gives life its moral seriousness. The refrain confronts it: will you declare false the truth that all is known and recorded? The one who truly believes his every deed is being written for a Day when it will be disclosed without questioning cannot maintain the illusion that secret sins are safe. The refrain thus works to dissolve the fatal fantasy of the hidden deed — the fantasy that some wrongs, done where no human eye sees, escape all reckoning. Nothing escapes the reckoning, for the Judge sees all.

**The Logic: Perfect Knowledge as the Foundation of Trust**

There is a deep connection between God's perfect knowledge and the believer's trust. Because God knows all — every deed, every intention, every hidden circumstance and secret struggle — His judgment will be not only infallible but perfectly *just to the individual*, weighing each soul in full knowledge of everything that shaped it. No mitigating circumstance will be overlooked; no hidden sincerity will go unseen; no secret struggle against temptation will be forgotten. The believer can therefore trust the reckoning completely, knowing that he will be judged by One who knows him better than he knows himself, and who will do him no injustice: *"and your Lord wrongs no one"* (18:49). This transforms the prospect of judgment from a source of pure dread into a source of trust: the outcome rests in the hands of perfect knowledge joined to perfect mercy. The refrain invites gratitude for exactly this — the boon of being judged by One who knows and who is just.

**Application: The Freedom of Living Before the All-Knowing**

For the reader, the awareness that God knows all — the awareness this refrain reinforces — is meant to produce not anxiety but a peculiar freedom and integrity. The person who lives before an all-knowing God has no need to maintain a false front, no exhausting double life of public virtue and private vice, for he knows that the only Judge who ultimately matters sees straight through every façade. This drives him toward *integrity* — the alignment of the inner and outer self — which is itself a great relief, for the divided life of the hypocrite is a heavy burden. It also frees him from the tyranny of others' opinions: since God sees the truth of his deeds and intentions, he need not exhaust himself performing virtue for human audiences who see only the surface. He does good in secret, knowing the secret is seen; he bears injustice patiently, knowing the wrong is recorded; he strives sincerely, knowing the sincerity is known. To live before the All-Knowing, rightly understood, is to be liberated from pretense and anchored in the sight of the One whose knowledge is the foundation of a perfect and merciful justice. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the perfection of Your knowledge, my Lord — for in it lies the guarantee that justice will finally, and flawlessly, be done.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:41

> **The guilty shall be known by their marks; and they shall be seized by the forelocks and by the feet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Guilt Written on the Body**

The sūrah continues its portrayal of the reckoning with a vivid image of the exposure and seizure of the guilty: *"The guilty shall be known by their marks; and they shall be seized by the forelocks and by the feet"* — *yuʿrafu al-mujrimūna bi-sīmāhum fa-yuʾkhadhu bi'l-nawāṣī wa'l-aqdām*. On that Day, the guilty will not need to be identified by name or interrogated; they will be recognizable at a glance, marked in a way that betrays their state, and they will be laid hold of — seized by the forelock and the feet — and dragged to their fate. This verse follows naturally from verse 39: the guilty are not questioned, because they are already known — known by their very marks.

**Known by Their Marks — Sīmā**

The word *sīmā* means a distinguishing mark, a sign, a token by which something is recognized. The Qur'an speaks elsewhere of marks by which people are known on the Day of Judgment — and strikingly, it speaks of marks for *both* the guilty and the righteous, in pointed contrast. The guilty bear the marks of their guilt; the commentators described these variously as faces blackened or darkened, downcast and disgraced, marked with the traces of their denial and wrongdoing:

> *"On the Day some faces will turn white and some faces will turn black."* (Qur'an 3:106)

> *"And the guilty will be recognized by their marks, and they will be seized by the forelocks and the feet."* (55:41)

By contrast, the righteous bear the luminous marks of their devotion — the light shining from the limbs they washed in ablution, and the mark of prostration upon their faces:

> *"Their mark is on their faces from the trace of prostration."* (Qur'an 48:29)

The Prophet ﷺ described the believers of his community rising on the Day of Judgment radiant and marked with light from the traces of ablution, as recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim: *"My community will be summoned on the Day of Resurrection with bright faces, hands, and feet from the traces of ablution."* Thus the marks cut both ways: the deeds of a lifetime, inscribed upon the very body, so that on that Day each soul wears the visible sign of what it made of itself.

**Seized by the Forelocks and the Feet**

The guilty, once recognized, are "seized by the forelocks and by the feet" (*al-nawāṣī wa'l-aqdām*).
