> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Ease of the Gardens**

The refrain returns after the image of the blessed reclining upon brocaded couches with fruit within easy reach. The blessing being underscored is the perfect rest and effortless abundance of Paradise — the end of all toil and the fulfillment of the deep human longing for repose. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**The Boon of Promised Rest**

To be promised an eternal rest — a permanent end to the toil, anxiety, and striving that mark this life — is among the most consoling of all God's boons, and the refrain here invites the reader to receive it with gratitude. Every weary human being who has felt the endlessness of labor, the recurrence of need, the impossibility of ever finally "arriving" and resting in this world, is offered, in the Gardens, the promise that this weariness has an end. The toil of this life is real, but it is not eternal; beyond it lies the rest of the couches of brocade. This promise is a boon precisely because it transforms the experience of present toil: the believer labors not as one condemned to endless, meaningless struggle, but as a traveler nearing a home of rest, whose present exertion is the last stretch of a journey with a destination. To deny this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) would be to deny that the toil has any end — to believe oneself condemned to endless struggle with no rest to come, a belief that drains hope from the heart. The refrain calls the reader instead to affirm the promised rest and to be strengthened by it.

**Rest as a Sign of Being Received and Honored**

There is a further dimension to the boon of rest that the refrain invites the reader to contemplate. In the customs of hospitality that the original audience knew intimately, to be given rest — to be seated, reclined, served, and relieved of all labor — is the mark of being an *honored guest*. The host who truly honors his guest does not put him to work but bids him recline and be served. The reclining of the blessed thus signifies not only physical comfort but *honor* — the honor of being received by God as a cherished guest, relieved of all labor, served rather than serving. The Qur'an makes this explicit, describing the servants of Paradise moving among the blessed to serve them (52:24; 76:19). To be granted such rest is to be granted the highest honor: to be treated by the Lord of all the worlds as a beloved and honored guest in His own abode. The refrain invites gratitude for this honor — the boon not merely of rest but of being received and honored by God Himself.

**The Logic: Rest Earned Through Restlessness for God's Sake**

There is a fitting logic in the fact that eternal rest is the reward of those who were, in this world, restless *for God's sake* — who did not rest content with heedlessness but strove, labored, and disturbed their own ease in the pursuit of righteousness. The one who sought comfort and rest in this world at the expense of his duty to God will find no rest in the next; but the one who forwent ease in this world for God's sake — who rose for prayer when he might have slept, who restrained himself when he might have indulged, who labored in good works when he might have taken his ease — will find the rest he sacrificed here repaid, eternally and perfectly, there. The rest of the Gardens is thus the just reward of a holy restlessness — the exchange of a temporary ease forgone for an eternal ease gained. This is the divine economy the sūrah keeps revealing: what is given up for God is never lost but returned, redoubled and made everlasting.

**Application: Trading Temporary Ease for Eternal Rest**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain teaches a wise investment of one's ease. The natural human tendency is to seek maximum comfort and minimum exertion in the present — to prefer the ease of now over the effort that leads to reward later. But the believer, contemplating the eternal rest of the Gardens, learns to trade wisely: to accept the temporary exertion of righteousness in exchange for the permanent rest of Paradise, and to refuse the temporary ease of heedlessness that leads to eternal loss. This does not mean despising all rest in this world — for the body has its rights, and the Prophet ﷺ balanced worship with rest — but it means never sacrificing the eternal rest for the sake of temporary ease, never choosing the comfort of sin over the exertion of obedience. The believer, in his moments of weariness, strengthens himself with the promise of the couches of brocade: *this toil is temporary; the rest is forever; let me not trade the eternal rest for a moment's ease.* *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the promised rest, my Lord — and by its light, I will bear the toil of the road and never trade the everlasting for the fleeting.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:56

> **Therein are maidens of modest gaze, whom neither man nor jinn has ever touched.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Companions of the Gardens**

The sūrah now names another of the Gardens' blessings — the pure companions prepared for the dwellers of Paradise: *"Therein are maidens of modest gaze, whom neither man nor jinn has ever touched"* — *fīhinna qāṣirātu al-ṭarfi lam yaṭmithhunna insun qablahum wa lā jānn*. Among the delights of the Gardens are pure, modest companions, whose beauty is matched by their fidelity and purity. The description continues the theme of the Gardens as the place where every wholesome human longing — including the longing for love and intimate companionship — finds its perfected and purified fulfillment.

**Maidens of Modest Gaze — Qāṣirāt al-Ṭarf**

The phrase *qāṣirāt al-ṭarf* means "those who restrain their glance" — literally, those whose gaze is limited or held back. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ālūsī) explained this beautifully: these are companions who look upon *none but their own spouses* — whose eyes, and whose love, are devoted entirely to their partners. The phrase describes not a downcast timidity but a *fidelity of the gaze* — a love so complete and content that it seeks no other, desires no other, and turns to no other. This is a quality of profound beauty, for it names the deepest longing of the human heart in love: to be loved with an undivided, faithful devotion, to be the sole object of a beloved's gaze. The companions of the Gardens embody this perfect fidelity, and their "modesty of gaze" is thus not merely a moral virtue but the very fulfillment of the heart's desire for a love that is wholly and faithfully one's own.

**Untouched and Pure**

The companions are further described as those *"whom neither man nor jinn has ever touched"* — *lam yaṭmithhunna insun qablahum wa lā jānn*. The verb *ṭamatha* implies the taking of virginity when a woman is its object. The description conveys that these companions are pure, chaste, untouched — reserved entirely for the dwellers of Paradise. The mention of both "man" and "jinn" reflects the sūrah's consistent dual address to the two responsible species: the companions of the human dwellers are untouched by any man, and (on the reading that includes the jinn among the dwellers) the companions of the jinn dwellers are likewise untouched by any jinn. The emphasis on purity and chastity underscores that the intimacy of the Gardens is wholly pure, entirely free of the corruptions, jealousies, betrayals, and impurities that mar human relationships in this world. It is love and companionship in their perfected, uncorrupted form.

**Freedom from the Burdens of This World**

The commentators drew out a further dimension of the word *ṭamatha* and its relation to purity. Because the verb, in a related usage, connects to menstruation, some commentators (cited in the tradition) understood the description to indicate that the companions of Paradise are free of the physical burdens and difficulties — menstruation, childbearing, and their attendant hardships — that beset women in this world. On this reading, the purity of the companions is not only moral but a freedom from all the bodily discomforts and impurities of earthly existence. This fits the larger vision of Paradise as the place where all the burdens, pains, and imperfections of this world are removed — where even the natural difficulties of bodily life give way to a state of unmixed ease and purity. The Gardens are free not only of sin and sorrow but of every hardship, including the physical hardships that are part of life in this world.

**The Dignity of Fulfilled and Purified Desire**

It is important to understand how the Qur'an frames the companionship of Paradise, lest it be misread. The Qur'an does not present the intimacy of the Gardens as a base or shameful thing grudgingly permitted, but as a *pure and honored blessing* — one of the wholesome delights of the perfected human being. The longing for love, companionship, and intimacy is a genuine and God-given part of human nature; the Qur'an itself describes marriage in this world as a sign of God and a source of tranquility and mercy:

> *"And of His signs is that He created for you from yourselves mates that you may find tranquility in them, and He placed between you affection and mercy."* (Qur'an 30:21)

In Paradise, this God-given longing finds its perfected fulfillment — purified of every corruption, freed from every burden, and made everlasting. The companions of the Gardens represent the redemption and perfection of a wholesome human desire, not its indulgence in some debased form. This is consistent with the sūrah's whole vision: that Paradise is the place where every good and natural human capacity — for delight, for rest, for love — is fulfilled in its highest and purest form.

**A Note on the Blessings for All the Dwellers**

The classical and later commentators have noted, in discussing the companions of Paradise, that the delights of the Gardens are prepared for *all* their righteous inhabitants, and that the Qur'an elsewhere makes clear that the righteous women who enter Paradise are themselves honored with its perfected blessings, joy, and companionship. The Qur'an addresses the reward of the righteous inclusively:

> *"Whoever does righteousness, whether male or female, while being a believer — We will surely cause him to live a good life, and We will surely give them their reward according to the best of what they used to do."* (Qur'an 16:97)

> *"Enter Paradise, you and your spouses, delighted."* (Qur'an 43:70)

Thus the pure companionship and perfected joy of the Gardens is the reward of the God-fearing among both men and women — the fulfillment, for every righteous soul, of the wholesome longing for love and companionship in its purest and most honored form.

**The Logic: The Fulfillment of Every Wholesome Longing**

There is a consistent logic running through the sūrah's description of the Gardens, and the mention of the pure companions fits it exactly. The Gardens are portrayed as the place where *every* wholesome human longing finds its perfected fulfillment: the longing for beauty (the green branches), for life and refreshment (the flowing fountains), for delight (the fruits), for rest (the couches), and now for love and companionship (the pure companions). The human being was created with these longings, and in this world each of them is met only partially, imperfectly, and temporarily, always mixed with lack and loss. Paradise is the place where each longing is met fully, perfectly, and eternally, purified of every flaw. This teaches that the deepest human desires are not to be despised or extinguished but to be rightly ordered and directed toward their true fulfillment. The desire for love is not evil; it is a longing planted by God that finds its perfection in the Gardens. The refrain will ask whether this boon, too — the promise of perfected, purified love — could be denied.

**Application: Ordering Love Toward Its Perfection**

For the reader, the pure companions of the Gardens teach a right relationship with the powerful human longing for love and intimacy. This longing is one of the strongest forces in human life, and one of the most easily corrupted — leading, when disordered, to betrayal, exploitation, and grief. The Qur'an's teaching is neither to suppress this longing nor to indulge it lawlessly, but to *order* it: to keep it pure in this world (through the honored bond of marriage) and to direct its ultimate fulfillment toward the Gardens, where it is perfected and made everlasting. The believer thus neither despises the longing for love nor makes an idol of it, but holds it as a God-given desire whose true and perfect satisfaction lies in the Hereafter. This ordering frees him from the desperate and idolatrous pursuit of a perfect love in this world — a pursuit doomed to disappointment, for no earthly love is free of flaw — and lets him receive the imperfect loves of this life with gratitude while longing for their perfection in the world to come. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the promise of perfected love, my Lord — the fulfillment of a longing You planted in the human heart, made pure and everlasting in the Gardens.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:57

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Companions of the Gardens**

The refrain returns after the mention of the pure companions of Paradise. The blessing being underscored is the perfected, purified companionship of the Gardens — the fulfillment of the human longing for a faithful and everlasting love. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**The Boon of Perfected Relationship**

Among the deepest sources of both joy and sorrow in human life are relationships — the bonds of love and companionship that, at their best, are the sweetness of existence, and at their worst, the source of its bitterest pains. In this world, even the most beautiful relationships are marred by imperfection: by misunderstanding, by betrayal, by the fading of affection, by jealousy and conflict, and ultimately by loss, for death parts every earthly love. The companionship of the Gardens is the promise that this deepest source of human joy will one day be freed of every source of pain — a love without betrayal, without conflict, without fading, without loss, faithful and pure and everlasting. This is a boon of immense consolation, for it answers one of the deepest wounds of the human condition: the fact that our loves in this world, however precious, are always shadowed by imperfection and always ended by loss. The refrain invites gratitude for the promise that love itself will be redeemed — perfected and made deathless in the Gardens.

**The Denial of Perfected Joy**

To "deny" this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) would be to disbelieve that the wounds of human relationship can ever be healed — to accept as final the imperfection, betrayal, and loss that mar earthly love, and to deny that there is any place where love is made perfect and permanent. This is a denial that consigns the human heart to a kind of despair about its deepest longing, teaching it to expect nothing better than the flawed and fleeting loves of this world. The refrain confronts this despair with the promise of the Gardens: love *will* be perfected; the wounds *will* be healed; the deepest longing of the heart *will* be fulfilled, purely and eternally. To affirm this boon is to hold fast to hope for the very thing the human heart most longs for and most fears to lose.

**The Logic: Why Even Companionship Is Part of the Reward**

Some might wonder why the reward of Paradise should include *companionship* at all — why the perfected soul, drawn near to God, should need or receive the company of others. The answer lies in the Qur'an's understanding of the human being as a fundamentally *relational* creature, made for connection and not for solitude. Recall that the word *insān* (human being) may derive from *uns*, "intimacy, sociability" — the human being is, by his very nature, a creature made for relationship. It would therefore be no perfection of the human being to isolate him even in Paradise; his perfection includes the perfection of his relationships. The Gardens are not a solitary bliss but a shared one, in which the human being enjoys not only the nearness of God but the perfected company of beloved companions — and, as the Qur'an describes, the reunion with righteous family and loved ones:

> *"...they and their spouses, in shade, reclining on adorned couches."* (Qur'an 36:56)

> *"And those who believed and whose descendants followed them in faith — We will join with them their descendants."* (Qur'an 52:21)

The inclusion of companionship in the reward thus reflects the truth that the human being is made for relationship, and that his perfect happiness includes the perfection of his bonds of love. The refrain invites gratitude for this — for a reward that fulfills not only the individual soul but its longing for perfected connection.

**Application: Tending Relationships as Seeds of the Eternal**

For the reader, the companionship of the Gardens casts the relationships of this world in a new light. The bonds of love and family in this life are not merely temporary arrangements to be left behind but, when built on faith and righteousness, seeds of an eternal companionship. The Qur'an's promise that the righteous will be reunited with their believing loved ones in the Gardens (52:21) means that the love one nurtures in this world, on the foundation of faith, can endure into the next — perfected and made everlasting. This gives profound weight to how one tends one's relationships now: the marriage built on faith, the family raised in righteousness, the friendships grounded in the love of God, are investments in an eternal companionship. The believer therefore tends his relationships with care, building them on the foundation that can survive death — faith and righteousness — so that the loves of this world may be among the loves reunited and perfected in the Gardens. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the promise of perfected companionship, my Lord — the healing of love's every wound, and the reunion, made eternal, of hearts that loved for Your sake.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:58

> **Even as the ruby and as the coral stone are they.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Beauty of the Companions in Imagery**

The sūrah completes its description of the companions of the first two Gardens with a comparison of surpassing beauty: *"Even as the ruby and as the coral stone are they"* — *ka-annahunna al-yāqūtu wa'l-marjān*. The companions of Paradise are likened to the ruby (*yāqūt*) and the coral stone (*marjān*) — gems of luminous purity and radiant color. This imagery draws on the most precious and beautiful objects the human eye knows, using them to convey a beauty that words cannot directly describe, only gesture toward through comparison.

**Ruby and Coral — Purity and Rosy Radiance**

The commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ālūsī) explained the comparison in terms of two qualities the gems evoke. The *ruby* (*yāqūt*) suggests *purity and clarity* — a gem so clear that, in the descriptions of the tradition, one could see through it, radiant and flawless, without blemish or cloudiness. The *coral stone* (*marjān*) — which, as noted earlier (verse 22), may mean either coral or small white pearls — suggests *whiteness tinged with rose*, a delicate rosy fairness of complexion. Together, the two gems convey a beauty that is at once *pure and radiant* (like the clear ruby) and *delicately colored* (like the rose-tinted coral) — a luminous, flawless, glowing beauty beyond anything found in this world. The comparison to gems also conveys *preciousness*: the companions of the Gardens are treasures, precious and rare, of a beauty as valued and as flawless as the finest jewels.

**A Beauty That Illuminates**

The tradition preserves striking descriptions of the beauty and radiance of the companions of Paradise, conveying a splendor that exceeds all earthly measure. A report cited by Ibn Kathīr in his commentary describes this luminous beauty:

> *"If a woman among the people of Paradise were to look upon the earth, she would fill what is between them [heaven and earth] with fragrance, and all that is between them would be filled with her light. And the veil upon her head is better than this world and all that is in it."* (cited by Ibn Kathīr)

The description conveys a beauty so radiant that it illuminates, so fragrant that it perfumes the distance between heaven and earth — a beauty of an entirely different order from the fading, partial beauty of this world. This is beauty raised to a perfection the earthly eye has never seen, fitting for the perfected companionship of the Gardens.

**The Connection Back to the Sea's Treasures**

The commentators noted a deliberate echo in this verse. The comparison of the companions to *pearl/coral* (*marjān*) hearkens back to verse 22, where the sea was described as bringing forth *"pearls and coral stones"* (*al-luʾluʾu wa'l-marjān*) among the treasures of this world. The connection is meaningful. The earthly treasures of verse 22 — the pearls and coral drawn from the sea — were among the boons of this world, precious and beautiful. Now, in the Gardens, the same imagery of pearl and coral is applied to the far greater treasures of Paradise. The beauty the human being prized in the earthly gem is shown to be a faint foretaste of the beauty prepared in the Gardens: the earthly coral was precious, but the companions of Paradise are *"as the coral stone"* — the earthly treasure become the measure of a heavenly one. This is a recurring pattern in the sūrah: the blessings of this world are foretastes and pointers toward the greater blessings of the next, and the beauty the heart loves here is a dim reflection of the beauty prepared there.

**The Logic: Beauty as a Reflection of the Divine**

There is a deep logic in the way the Qur'an uses the most beautiful earthly things — gems, gardens, flowing water, radiant light — to describe Paradise. Beauty, in the Islamic understanding, is not a trivial or merely decorative quality but a reflection of a divine reality: God Himself is described in the tradition as beautiful and loving beauty. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by Muslim:

> *"Indeed, God is beautiful and loves beauty."* (Muslim 91)

The beauty of the Gardens — the radiance of the ruby, the rose of the coral, the luminous companions — is thus a created reflection of the beauty of their Maker, and the human heart's love of beauty is, at its root, a longing for the ultimate Beauty that is God. The beauty of Paradise draws the heart toward God not by distracting from Him but by reflecting Him — each created beauty a ray of the uncreated Beauty. This is why the Gardens are described in such radiant terms: their beauty is meant to awaken the heart's longing for the Beautiful One who made them, and whose nearness is the deepest joy of Paradise. The refrain will ask whether this boon of beauty, too, could be denied.

**Application: Letting Beauty Lead the Heart to God**

For the reader, the radiant beauty of the Gardens and their companions teaches a right relationship with beauty itself. Beauty is a powerful force in human life, capable of enslaving the heart when loved for its own sake, or of lifting the heart toward God when received as a reflection of Him. The believer learns to receive beauty — in this world and in the promise of the next — as a pointer toward its Source: to see in every earthly beauty a faint ray of the perfect Beauty of Paradise, and in the beauty of Paradise a reflection of the Beauty of God Himself. This ordering of beauty frees the heart from being enslaved by the fading beauties of this world, for it recognizes them as mere foretastes, and directs the heart's love toward the ultimate Beauty that alone can satisfy it. The believer thus loves beauty rightly — not as an idol but as a signpost, letting every glimpse of the beautiful, from the earthly gem to the promised radiance of the Gardens, lift his heart toward the Beautiful One who is the source and goal of all beauty. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the beauty of the Gardens, my Lord — for in it I glimpse a ray of Your own Beauty, toward which every beautiful thing has been quietly pointing all along.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:59

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Closing the First Description of Beauty**

The refrain returns after the comparison of the companions of the Gardens to the ruby and the coral stone. The blessing being underscored is the radiant, flawless beauty of Paradise — a beauty that reflects the Beauty of its Maker and awakens the heart's longing for Him. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**Gratitude for Beauty as Worship**

To receive beauty with gratitude to its Giver is itself a form of worship — the recognition that the beautiful comes from the Beautiful, and the turning of the heart, through the gift, toward the Giver. The refrain, placed after the description of Paradise's radiant beauty, invites exactly this: to let the contemplation of beauty become an occasion for gratitude, and gratitude an occasion for the remembrance of God. This transforms the experience of beauty from a merely aesthetic pleasure into a spiritual act. The one who beholds beauty — whether the earthly beauty that foreshadows the Gardens or the promised beauty of the Gardens themselves — and responds with gratitude to God is worshipping through his very appreciation, converting the enjoyment of beauty into the remembrance of its Source. The refrain teaches this conversion: beauty acknowledged as a boon becomes a doorway to God.

**The Danger of Beauty Denied or Idolized**

There are two ways to "deny" the boon of beauty, and the refrain guards against both. The first is to *deny beauty its due* — to be blind or indifferent to it, to move through a world and a promise filled with beauty without ever recognizing it as a gift or turning through it to the Giver. This is the denial of the hardened, unappreciative heart that receives beauty without gratitude. The second, subtler denial is to *idolize* beauty — to love the beautiful thing so entirely for its own sake that one forgets its Source, making the gift into a rival of the Giver. This too is a denial, for it denies that the beauty is a *boon from the Lord*, treating it instead as an end in itself. The refrain, by asking the reader to count beauty among *the Lord's* boons, corrects both errors: it summons the indifferent to appreciate beauty as a gift, and it recalls the idolater from worshipping the gift to thanking the Giver. The right response is between the two: to love beauty deeply *as a gift*, and through it to love the Giver more.

**The Logic: Beauty as Evidence of a Generous, Loving God**

There is an implicit argument in the sūrah's lavish attention to beauty — the beauty of the earth (fruit, pearls, fragrant herbs) and the beauty of the Gardens (green branches, flowing water, radiant companions). A God concerned only with function, only with the bare maintenance of existence, would have had no reason to fill creation and its reward with such abundant, gratuitous beauty. The sheer profusion of beauty — beauty far exceeding any practical necessity — is evidence of a Giver who is not merely functional but *generous* and *loving*, who wished His creatures not only to survive but to delight, not only to exist but to be surrounded by splendor. The beauty of the Gardens, in particular, reveals the character of the reward-Giver: He prepares for those He loves not a bare and functional reward but a place of overwhelming beauty, radiant and precious beyond imagining. This lavishness is the signature of *al-Raḥmān*, whose mercy overflows into beauty at every turn. The refrain invites the reader to read the beauty rightly — as evidence of the generous, loving character of the God who made and promised it.

**Application: A Life Attuned to Beauty and Its Source**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for the cultivation of a heart attuned to beauty and, through beauty, to God. This is a discipline of attention: to notice the beauty that fills both the world and the promise — the beauty of a sunrise, a flower, a kind face, a well-ordered life, and the promised beauty of the Gardens — and to let each noticing become an act of gratitude and remembrance. Such a heart moves through the world enriched, finding in beauty everywhere a continual reminder of the Beautiful One and a continual foretaste of the Gardens. It neither hardens into indifference nor melts into idolatry, but holds beauty as a beloved gift that points always beyond itself. And it answers the refrain, at each fresh encounter with beauty, in grateful worship: *not this, my Lord; I do not deny the beauty You have made and promised, but through it I remember You, the Beautiful, from whom all beauty flows and toward whom all beauty leads.*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:60

> **Is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle That Underlies All Reward**

After describing the Gardens and their blessings, the sūrah pauses to state the great principle that underlies the entire economy of reward: *"Is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?"* — *hal jazāʾu al-iḥsāni illā al-iḥsān*. This is a rhetorical question whose answer is self-evident: of course the reward of goodness is nothing but goodness. The verse distills, into a single luminous sentence, the whole logic of the Gardens: they are the fitting, natural, inevitable reward of a life of goodness. Goodness given is met with goodness returned — this is the moral law of the universe, as reliable as any physical law.

**The Meaning of Iḥsān**

The word *iḥsān* — used here twice, for both the deed and its reward — is one of the richest terms in the Qur'an and the Islamic tradition. It derives from the root *ḥ-s-n*, "to be good, beautiful, excellent." *Iḥsān* means not merely "goodness" but *excellence*, *the doing of good in the most beautiful way*, *the perfection of virtue*. It is the highest of the three levels of religion defined by the Prophet ﷺ in the famous ḥadīth of Gabriel — *islām* (submission), then *īmān* (faith), then *iḥsān* (excellence) — where the Prophet ﷺ defined *iḥsān* itself:

> *"Iḥsān is to worship God as though you see Him; and though you do not see Him, He surely sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

So *iḥsān* is worship and goodness raised to the level of excellence — done with the awareness of God's presence, done beautifully, done as though in the very sight of God. The verse's use of this specific word is significant: the reward of the Gardens is not merely for minimal goodness but is the fitting return for *iḥsān*, for goodness done excellently and beautifully. And the reward itself is *iḥsān* — goodness and beauty from God, given in the most excellent and beautiful way.

**Rich Interpretations from the Tradition**

The commentators recorded several beautiful interpretations of this verse, each illuminating a facet of its meaning:

According to many, the plain meaning is that the reward for doing good deeds in this life is goodness in the Hereafter — the Gardens are the *iḥsān* (goodness) that God returns for the *iḥsān* (goodness) of the believer's deeds. This is confirmed by:

> *"For those who do good is the best [reward] and more."* (Qur'an 10:26)

> *"And whoever does a good deed — We will increase for him good therein."* (Qur'an 42:23)

The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have explained the verse in terms of the reward of monotheism (*tawḥīd*): *"Is the reward of one whom I have blessed with the testimony of God's oneness anything but Paradise?"* (cited by al-Ālūsī). On this reading, the greatest *iḥsān* of the servant is the acknowledgment of God's oneness, and its fitting reward is the Garden.

Al-Suddī understood it as: *"Is the reward of those who live a life of obedience in this world anything but generosity in the Hereafter?"* (cited by al-Ālūsī).

A saying attributed to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq gives it a mystical depth: *"Is the reward of those to whom I showed My goodness in pre-eternity (before creation) anything but that I shall continue to show them My goodness in post-eternity (forever after)?"* (cited by al-Ālūsī and al-Qurṭubī). On this reading, God's goodness to His servants in eternity is itself the reward of the goodness He first showed them in bringing them into being and guiding them.

Some connected it to the ḥadīth of Gabriel's definition of *iḥsān* (worshipping God as if you see Him), so that the verse means: the reward of worshipping God with such excellence and presence is to *see* Him — the supreme reward of the Gardens, the beholding of God's Face.

And some understood it at the deepest level to mean that *a virtuous act is its own reward* — that goodness carries its own goodness within it, so that the doing of good is already, in itself, a form of the reward it earns.

**The Logic: The Moral Symmetry of the Universe**

The verse states, as a rhetorical question that admits only one answer, a principle of profound moral logic: that the universe is built on a moral symmetry in which goodness is answered with goodness. This is not merely a promise but a statement about the very structure of reality — that God has woven into existence a moral order as reliable as its physical order, in which good deeds are met with good returns as surely as causes are met with effects. This principle is the foundation of all moral seriousness and all hope in righteousness. If goodness were *not* answered with goodness — if the good and the wicked met the same end, if virtue led nowhere and vice cost nothing — then the moral life would be a cruel joke, and the striving of the righteous a pointless waste. The verse affirms the opposite: goodness *is* answered with goodness, reliably, inevitably, by the decree of a just and generous God. This affirmation vindicates the entire moral effort of the believer's life and grounds his hope in the ultimate meaningfulness of goodness.

**The Beauty of a Reward That Matches the Deed**

There is a particular beauty in the *symmetry* the verse expresses — the matching of *iḥsān* with *iḥsān*, goodness with goodness. The reward is not arbitrary or disconnected from the deed but is its natural fulfillment and completion, the same quality returned in perfected and everlasting form. The one who did good in this world — imperfectly, temporarily, amid difficulty — receives good in the next, perfectly, eternally, amid ease. The goodness he gave is the very goodness he receives, raised to perfection. This symmetry reveals the harmony and justice of God's dealings: He does not reward goodness with something alien to it but with its own perfected form, so that the reward is recognizable as the flowering of the deed. The believer's good deeds are thus seeds, and the Gardens are their harvest — the same nature, brought to fruition.

**Application: A Life Built on the Certainty of This Principle**

For the reader, this verse offers one of the most motivating and consoling principles for the whole of life: that goodness is never wasted, that every good deed will be answered with good, that the moral effort of one's life is building, reliably, toward a good return. This certainty transforms the experience of doing good, especially when it is difficult, costly, or unrewarded in this world. The kindness that went unthanked, the honesty that cost one advantage, the restraint that forwent pleasure, the good done in secret that no one saw — none of it is lost; all of it is stored, to be answered with goodness by the One who sees all. This principle guards the believer against the disillusionment that can come from doing good in a world that often fails to reward it: he knows that the true reward is not from the world but from God, and that it is certain. It therefore frees him to do good generously, excellently, and without concern for earthly recompense, confident that *"the reward of goodness is nothing but goodness."* Let the reader, then, fill his life with *iḥsān* — with goodness done beautifully, as though in the sight of God — assured that its reward is the Gardens, and beyond even the Gardens, the beholding of the Face of the Good and Beautiful One Himself. *And is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:61

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After the Great Principle**

The refrain returns after the sūrah's statement of its central principle of reward — "Is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?" The blessing being underscored here is the reliability of the moral order itself: the certainty that goodness is answered with goodness. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* Coming immediately after so foundational a truth, the refrain invites the reader to count among God's boons the very justice and generosity of His moral economy.

**The Boon of a Just Moral Order**

That the universe is built on a moral order in which goodness is reliably rewarded is, when rightly understood, one of the greatest of all boons — perhaps the boon that makes the moral life possible at all. Imagine the alternative: a universe in which goodness led nowhere, in which the good and the wicked met identical ends, in which virtue was as futile as vice. Such a universe would be a moral horror, draining all meaning from every good deed and every act of restraint. The verse's affirmation that "the reward of goodness is nothing but goodness" is therefore not a small comfort but the very foundation of hope and meaning in the moral life. It guarantees that the believer's striving is not in vain, that his goodness matters, that the moral effort of his life is building toward a real and certain reward. The refrain invites gratitude for this — for the boon of living in a universe where goodness counts, where the good one does is stored and will be answered, where the moral order is just and generous rather than empty and indifferent.

**Denial as Moral Nihilism**

To "deny" this boon (*tukadhdhibān*) would be, at its root, a kind of moral nihilism — the denial that goodness ultimately matters, that righteousness leads anywhere, that the moral effort of life has any final point. This denial, whether held explicitly or lived out practically, is corrosive to the soul, for it removes the ground of all moral seriousness. If goodness is not answered with goodness, then why be good at cost to oneself? Why restrain, why sacrifice, why strive? The denial of the moral order thus tends, by its own logic, toward the abandonment of goodness itself. The refrain confronts this denial with the sūrah's central affirmation: goodness *is* answered with goodness; the moral order is real and reliable; your striving is not in vain. To affirm this boon is to hold fast to the meaningfulness of the moral life against the corrosive whisper that says goodness does not matter.

**The Logic: Why the Reliability of Reward Sustains Righteousness**

There is a practical logic in the placement of this refrain after the principle of reward. The doing of good, especially difficult and costly good, requires sustaining motivation — and the most powerful sustaining motivation is the certainty that the good will be rewarded. A person who is uncertain whether his goodness will ever be answered will find his resolve wavering under the pressure of sacrifice and the absence of earthly recompense. But a person who is *certain* that "the reward of goodness is nothing but goodness" has an unshakeable ground for perseverance: he knows that every good deed is a certain investment, that no goodness is ever wasted, that the harvest is guaranteed even when the earthly returns are absent. The refrain, by calling the reader to affirm this certainty as a boon, is fortifying the very motivation that sustains a life of goodness. Gratitude for the reliability of the moral order becomes, in practice, the fuel of perseverance in righteousness.

**Application: Persevering in Goodness on the Strength of the Promise**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for the practical fortifying of one's perseverance in goodness. The doing of good is often hard, often costly, often unrewarded or even punished in this world. In such moments, the believer steadies himself on the certainty this refrain celebrates: that the reward of goodness is nothing but goodness, guaranteed by a God who is just and generous and who sees all. This certainty allows him to persevere in goodness precisely when it is hardest — to keep being honest when honesty costs him, to keep being kind when kindness is unreturned, to keep restraining himself when restraint is difficult, to keep doing good in secret when no one sees. He does so not out of naïve hope but out of reasoned trust in the moral order God has established and confirmed. And he answers the refrain with the gratitude of one who has found, in the reliability of God's justice, the ground of his own perseverance: *not this, my Lord; I do not deny that goodness is answered with goodness — and by the certainty of that boon, I will keep doing good, whatever it costs, until I meet the goodness You have promised.*

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:62

> **And beyond these two are two Gardens—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Pair of Gardens**

The sūrah now turns to a second pair of Gardens, distinct from the first: *"And beyond these two are two Gardens"* — *wa min dūnihimā jannatān*. Having described the first two Gardens (verses 48–60) and their blessings, the sūrah now names two further Gardens, opening a new section (verses 62–76) that will describe them in their turn. The phrase *min dūnihimā* — "beyond these two" or "besides these two" or "lower than these two" — indicates that these are additional Gardens, related to but distinct from the first pair.

**The Meaning of Min Dūnihimā**

The phrase *min dūnihimā* is understood by the commentators in two complementary ways, and the difference carries meaning. On one reading, *dūna* means "besides" or "in addition to" — so the verse names two *further* Gardens alongside the first two, an additional abundance. On another reading, *dūna* means "lower than" or "beneath" in rank — so the verse names two Gardens of a *lesser degree* than the first two. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr and others), connecting this to the sūrah's earlier discussion and to Sūrat al-Wāqiʿah, generally understood these second two Gardens as being of a *lower rank* than the first — the Gardens of "the companions of the right" (*aṣḥāb al-yamīn*), the general body of the righteous, as distinct from the higher Gardens of "the foremost" (*al-sābiqūn*). This reading is supported by the report cited earlier: two golden Gardens for the foremost, two silver Gardens for the companions of the right. The distinctions the sūrah will draw between the two pairs of Gardens (which we will examine in the following verses) confirm this understanding: the second pair, though gardens of surpassing beauty and blessing, are described with features suggesting a rank below the first.

**The Grades of Paradise**

This second pair of Gardens introduces, or rather confirms, one of the important truths of Islamic eschatology: that Paradise is not a single uniform place but is *graded*, containing many levels and degrees, corresponding to the varying ranks of the righteous. The Qur'an and Sunnah affirm this repeatedly:

> *"...they have degrees [of rank] with their Lord..."* (Qur'an 8:4)

> *"See how We have preferred some of them over others; and the Hereafter is greater in degrees and greater in preference."* (Qur'an 17:21)

The Prophet ﷺ described the highest rank of Paradise, as recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"Paradise has one hundred levels, and between each level and the next is like the distance between the heaven and the earth. When you ask God, ask Him for al-Firdaws, for it is the highest part of Paradise and the middle of Paradise, and above it is the Throne of the Most Merciful, and from it gush forth the rivers of Paradise."* (al-Bukhārī 2790, in meaning)

The two pairs of Gardens in this sūrah are thus a glimpse of this graded reality — the higher pair for those who excelled and outstripped others in righteousness, the lower pair for the general body of the faithful. Even the "lower" Gardens are, as the following verses will show, places of overwhelming beauty and blessing; the grading is not between good and bad but between good and better, between blessing and greater blessing.

**The Logic: Why Paradise Is Graded**

There is a profound justice and wisdom in the grading of Paradise. Human beings differ enormously in the degree of their faith, their striving, their sacrifice, and their goodness. Some give their whole lives to God with extraordinary devotion, forgoing much, striving greatly, excelling in every virtue; others believe and do good but with less intensity, less sacrifice, less excellence. It would be a kind of injustice — and a discouragement to excellence — if all these met an identical reward regardless of the vast differences in their striving. The grading of Paradise ensures that the reward is *proportioned to the striving*: those who gave more receive more, those who excelled are elevated, and the extraordinary devotion of the foremost is answered with an extraordinary reward. This both honors the justice of God (each rewarded according to his deeds) and provides a powerful motivation for excellence (the higher one strives, the higher one's rank). At the same time, the generosity of God ensures that even the "lowest" rank of Paradise is a place of surpassing beauty and blessing — for the least of the dwellers of Paradise, as the tradition describes, receives more than the whole of this world and its like many times over. The grading motivates striving without making the general believer despair; it rewards excellence without impoverishing the ordinary faithful.

**Application: Aspiring to the Highest**

For the reader, the existence of grades in Paradise carries a clear and motivating lesson: to aspire not merely to enter Paradise but to attain its highest ranks. The Prophet ﷺ taught his community to ask specifically for *al-Firdaws*, the highest Garden — teaching that the believer should not be content with the minimum but should strive for the summit of the reward. This aspiration shapes the whole tenor of a believer's striving: he is not satisfied with doing just enough to scrape into Paradise but seeks to excel, to outstrip, to be among "the foremost," precisely because he knows that the reward is graded and that greater striving yields greater elevation. This is a holy ambition — the desire for nearness to God and for the highest ranks of His reward — that the grading of Paradise is designed to awaken. The believer, contemplating the two pairs of Gardens, sets his sights on the highest, and lets that aspiration lift his striving above the merely sufficient toward the excellent. *And beyond these two are two Gardens* — a reminder that the reward has heights to be sought, and that the striving of this brief life determines the elevation of the eternal reward.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:63

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Opening the Second Pair of Gardens**

The refrain returns as the sūrah begins its description of the second pair of Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the abundance of God's reward — that He has prepared not merely two Gardens but four, not a single measure of blessing but grade upon grade of it. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**The Boon of Overflowing Abundance**

There is a particular quality of God's generosity that this refrain, placed at the opening of the second pair of Gardens, invites the reader to contemplate: its *overflowing abundance*. God does not give the reward of Paradise grudgingly or in bare measure; He gives it in overflowing profusion — two Gardens, and then two more, grade upon grade, blessing upon blessing. This lavishness is characteristic of *al-Raḥmān*, whose mercy overflows at every turn. The sūrah has shown this overflowing quality throughout: the earth not merely made habitable but filled with delight and fragrance; the sea not merely useful but stocked with treasure; the Gardens not merely provided but doubled and doubled again. The refrain, opening the second pair of Gardens, asks the reader to recognize and be grateful for this overflowing abundance — the boon of a Giver whose generosity exceeds all measure and all need, who gives not the minimum but the maximum, not a single Garden but four, and each surpassing all earthly imagining.

**Gratitude for What Exceeds One's Own Rank**

There is a subtle grace in the refrain's placement here. Even the reader who might attain only the "lower" pair of Gardens is invited to be grateful for the existence of the "higher" pair — and even the reader who aspires to the higher is invited to be grateful for the abundance that provides Gardens for others too. The refrain thus cultivates a gratitude that is not narrowly self-interested but rejoices in the whole abundance of God's generosity, including the blessings prepared for others and the ranks beyond one's own. This is a mark of a purified heart: to rejoice in God's generosity as such, to be grateful for the whole overflowing abundance of His mercy, and not merely for the portion that falls to oneself. The one who has learned to answer the refrain rightly rejoices that God is so generous — that there are four Gardens and a hundred levels — even beyond his own hope of attainment, glad simply that the mercy of *al-Raḥmān* is so vast.

**The Logic: Abundance as the Answer to Human Scarcity**

There is a deep contrast, which the refrain invites the reader to feel, between the *scarcity* that marks this world and the *abundance* that marks the reward. This world is a place of scarcity: resources are limited, one person's gain is often another's loss, and the anxious competition for scarce goods drives much of human conflict, greed, and cruelty. Paradise, by contrast, is a place of overflowing abundance, where the blessings are limitless, where no one's gain diminishes another's, where the four Gardens and the hundred levels provide for all without stint. This abundance is the answer to the scarcity of this world and to the anxieties it breeds. In the reward, the zero-sum competition of earthly life gives way to an infinite generosity in which all are lavishly provided for. The refrain, celebrating this abundance as a boon, invites the reader to lift his eyes above the scarcity and competition of this world toward the overflowing abundance of the world to come — and to let that vision loosen the grip of the greed and anxiety that scarcity breeds.

**Application: Living Generously in the Light of Abundance**

For the reader, the contemplation of God's overflowing abundance carries a practical fruit: it frees the heart to be generous. So much of human stinginess, greed, and hoarding arises from a felt scarcity — the anxious sense that there is not enough, that what one gives away is lost, that one must grasp and hold to be secure. The believer who has truly grasped the overflowing abundance of God's reward is freed from this anxiety. He knows that what he gives for God's sake is not lost but stored, that the true abundance lies not in what he hoards here but in what awaits him there, and that the God who prepared four Gardens and a hundred levels will not let his generosity go unrewarded. This frees him to give generously, to spend in the way of God without fear of loss, to release his grip on the scarce goods of this world in confidence of the abundant goods of the next. The vision of abundance thus makes him abundant; the gratitude the refrain cultivates flowers into a generosity that mirrors, in small measure, the overflowing generosity of *al-Raḥmān*. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not Your overflowing abundance, my Lord — and by the vision of it, teach my grasping heart to give as You give.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:64

> **of deepest green.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Color of the Second Gardens**

The sūrah now begins to describe the second pair of Gardens with a single, vivid detail: *"of deepest green"* — *mudhāmmatān*. These two Gardens are of a green so dark and intense that it verges on black — the deep, saturated green of dense, lush, abundant vegetation. This is the first descriptive feature given of the second pair, just as "abounding in green branches" (verse 48) was the first feature of the first pair. The parallel is deliberate, and the difference between the two descriptions is significant.

**The Meaning of Mudhāmmatān**

The word *mudhāmmatān* comes from a root indicating a green so deep and dark that it approaches blackness — the color of vegetation so dense and well-watered that its green becomes almost black in its intensity. The commentators explained this deep, dark green as the sign of *lush density* — vegetation so thick, so abundantly watered, so full of growth, that its color deepens toward black. This is a familiar phenomenon: the greenest, most thriving vegetation, seen in mass, appears almost black-green in its saturation, while sparser growth appears a lighter green. The deep green of these Gardens thus signifies an intensity and density of life — a vegetation so thriving that it darkens with its own abundance.

**A Difference in the Two Descriptions of Green**

The commentators noted a meaningful distinction between the greenery of the first pair of Gardens and that of the second. The first pair was described as *"abounding in green branches"* (*dhawātā afnān*, verse 48) — a description emphasizing *branches*, the boughs of trees laden with fruit and leaf, suggesting Gardens in which lofty, fruit-bearing trees predominate. The second pair is described as *"of deepest green"* (*mudhāmmatān*) — a description emphasizing the *dark green of dense ground-covering vegetation*, suggesting Gardens carpeted with thick, low, lush growth. Some commentators drew from this the observation that in the first (higher) Gardens, the noble fruit-bearing trees predominate, while in the second (lower) Gardens, the dense ground-vegetation predominates. This distinction, subtle as it is, was taken as one of the signs that the second pair of Gardens, though beautiful, is of a rank below the first — the difference between Gardens graced by lofty fruit-trees and Gardens carpeted with dense green growth. Yet it must be stressed that both are Gardens of surpassing beauty; the "deepest green" of the second pair is itself a mark of intense, thriving, abundant life.

**The Restfulness of Deep Green**

There is a deep human resonance in the color green, and especially in the deep, saturated green described here. Green is, across cultures, the color most associated with life, freshness, tranquility, and rest — the color of thriving vegetation, of well-watered land, of the relief that follows drought. The deep green of these Gardens conveys a restfulness and vitality that soothes and refreshes the beholder. Modern observation confirms what human intuition has always known: that the color green has a calming, restful effect on the human eye and spirit, associated with nature, growth, and peace. The Qur'an's description of Paradise as suffused with deep green — the couches, too, in verse 76, will be described as green — draws on this deep human response, painting Paradise in the color the heart most associates with life, peace, and rest. Even the color of the Gardens is chosen to convey their character as places of perfect tranquility and thriving life.

**The Logic: The Details That Make Paradise Vivid**

One might ask why the Qur'an troubles to specify so fine a detail as the precise shade of green in the Gardens. The answer lies in the power of concrete, sensory detail to make a promise *vivid* and *real* to the imagination. A vague promise of "a beautiful place" moves the heart little; a specific, vivid image — Gardens of a deep, dark, saturated green, dense with thriving life — makes the promise concrete, imaginable, and therefore desirable. The Qur'an, in describing Paradise, does not deal in abstractions but in vivid, particular images precisely because it aims to awaken real longing in the heart, and real longing is awakened by vivid, concrete beauty, not by pale abstraction. Each specific detail — the deep green, the flowing fountains, the low-hanging fruit, the brocaded couches — adds to the vividness and reality of the promise, making Paradise something the heart can picture, desire, and strive toward. This is the mercy of the concrete: God describes His reward in imaginable detail so that His servants may long for it enough to seek it.

**Application: Letting the Vivid Promise Draw the Heart**

For the reader, the vivid detail of the "deepest green" Gardens is an invitation to let the promise of Paradise become real and concrete in the imagination, and thereby to awaken genuine longing. The discipline is to read the descriptions of the Gardens not as abstract theology but as a real place to be pictured, longed for, and sought — to let the mind rest on the image of deep-green, thriving, tranquil Gardens until the heart begins to desire them as a real destination. This vivid longing is a powerful spur to righteousness, for the heart strives most earnestly toward what it can vividly imagine and deeply desire. The believer who has made the Gardens real to his imagination — who can picture the deep green, the flowing water, the perfect rest — finds the fleeting attractions of this world losing their power over him, for he has set his heart on a beauty far greater, and made it, through vivid contemplation, a living presence in his soul. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the deep-green Gardens, my Lord — and by picturing them, let me long for them, and by longing for them, let me strive toward them.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:65

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Second Gardens**

The refrain returns after the mention of the deep-green Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the tranquil, thriving beauty of the second pair of Gardens — the restful, life-filled green prepared for the companions of the right. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* As the description of the second pair of Gardens unfolds, the refrain continues to mark each feature, drawing the reader into the same grateful contemplation it has cultivated throughout.

**The Consistency of the Summons Across the Grades**

There is significance in the fact that the same refrain — unchanged — accompanies the description of both the higher and the lower Gardens. The summons to gratitude does not diminish for the "lesser" reward; the boons of the second pair of Gardens are as worthy of grateful acknowledgment as those of the first. This teaches an important lesson about gratitude: that it is due for every measure of God's blessing, great or small, higher or lower, and that no gift of God is beneath thankfulness. The one who would be grateful only for the highest and best, and who would withhold gratitude for the lesser blessings, has not learned true gratitude at all. The refrain, recurring identically across the grades of the Gardens, trains the heart to be grateful for every measure of God's generosity — to thank Him as fully for the deep-green Gardens as for the branch-laden ones, for the silver as for the gold. This is the gratitude that finds cause for thankfulness in every gift, and that therefore never lacks occasion for praise.

**Contentment as the Companion of Gratitude**

Closely related to gratitude is the virtue of contentment (*riḍā* and *qanāʿah*) — the settled satisfaction of the heart with what God has given. The refrain, recurring over the "lower" Gardens, quietly cultivates this contentment. It teaches that even the lesser reward of God is a cause for overflowing gratitude, and that the one who attains it should rejoice in it fully, without the resentment of comparison. This is a vital disposition, for one of the great thieves of joy in human life is the habit of comparison — the discontent that arises from measuring one's own portion against another's greater portion, and finding one's own lacking. The refrain guards against this by calling for gratitude for one's own blessings as they are, without the poison of comparison. The dweller of the deep-green Gardens is not called to envy the dweller of the branch-laden Gardens but to rejoice, with full gratitude, in the surpassing beauty of what he himself has been given. This contentment, joined to gratitude, is the secret of a joyful heart in this world and the next.

**The Logic: Gratitude Proportioned to the Giver, Not Only the Gift**

There is a deep logic in the refrain's insistence on gratitude for every grade of blessing. Gratitude, rightly understood, is directed not only at the *gift* but at the *Giver* — and the Giver is the same, whether the gift is great or small. The generosity, love, and mercy behind the lesser gift are the same generosity, love, and mercy behind the greater; the deep-green Gardens flow from the same *al-Raḥmān* as the branch-laden ones. When gratitude is directed at the Giver rather than merely measured against the size of the gift, it becomes constant and full for every blessing, for every blessing reveals the same generous Giver. This is why the refrain does not diminish for the lesser Gardens: because the object of gratitude is not the relative rank of the Garden but the boundless generosity of the Lord who prepared it. The one who has learned to be grateful to the Giver, and not merely for the gift, finds every blessing — great or small — an equal occasion for thankfulness, for every blessing is an equal revelation of the same overflowing mercy. The refrain trains exactly this Giver-directed gratitude, unchanging across the changing grades of the gifts.

**Application: The Contentment of the Grateful Heart**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain teaches the joined virtues of gratitude and contentment — the disposition of a heart that is fully thankful for what it has been given and free from the poison of comparison. This is one of the great secrets of happiness in this life and the next. The believer learns to receive whatever measure of blessing God gives him — in this world and in the next — with full gratitude and settled contentment, directing his thankfulness to the generous Giver rather than measuring his portion resentfully against another's. This does not preclude the holy aspiration to the highest ranks (which verse 62 encouraged), for one can strive earnestly for the best while remaining fully content and grateful for whatever one is given. The two are held together in the mature believer: aspiring to the highest, yet content and grateful for whatever God grants. Such a heart is free — free from envy, free from the discontent of comparison, free to rejoice in every gift as a revelation of the same beloved Giver. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord — neither the greater nor the lesser, for every gift, whatever its measure, reveals the same boundless generosity, and for that I am wholly grateful and wholly content.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:66

> **In each are two fountains bubbling.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Springs of the Second Gardens**

The sūrah describes the waters of the second pair of Gardens: *"In each are two fountains bubbling"* — *fīhimā ʿaynāni naḍḍākhatān*. Like the first pair of Gardens, the second pair contains two springs in each — but here the springs are described with a different word than before, and the difference is meaningful. Where the springs of the first Gardens were *"flowing"* (*tajriyān*, verse 50), the springs of these second Gardens are *"bubbling"* or "gushing" (*naḍḍākhatān*). The parallel structure invites comparison, and the commentators drew from the difference a distinction in rank.

**The Meaning of Naḍḍākhatān**

The word *naḍḍākhatān* comes from a root meaning to gush, to spout, to bubble up, to spray forth. It describes springs that *bubble up* or *gush forth* from their source. The commentators (al-Ālūsī, Ibn Kathīr) contrasted this with the *tajriyān* ("flowing") of the first Gardens' springs (verse 50), and drew a distinction: the springs of the first Gardens *flow* — that is, they run and course freely, suggesting copious, abundant, continuously coursing water; while the springs of these second Gardens *bubble up* — that is, they spout and gush from their source, which some understood as suggesting water somewhat less copious or less freely coursing than the flowing springs of the higher Gardens. On this reading, the difference between "flowing" and "bubbling" is another of the subtle marks distinguishing the higher pair of Gardens from the lower: both have abundant, beautiful springs, but the flowing springs of the higher Gardens suggest a yet greater abundance than the bubbling springs of the lower.

**Beauty in Both Kinds of Spring**

It is important not to overstate the distinction, for both kinds of spring are images of surpassing beauty and delight. A bubbling, gushing spring is itself one of the most beautiful and refreshing sights in nature — water leaping up from its source, sparkling and lively, fresh and pure. The description of the second Gardens' springs as "bubbling" conveys not a deficiency but a particular beauty: the lively, sparkling, gushing freshness of springs bubbling up amid the deep-green vegetation. The distinction between the two pairs of Gardens is, again, a distinction between the excellent and the more excellent, between beauty and greater beauty — not between the beautiful and the plain. The dweller of the second Gardens finds his springs a source of endless delight, however they may compare to the yet-greater springs of the higher Gardens.

**The Consistency of Water as the Emblem of Life**

The reappearance of springs in the description of the second Gardens confirms how central water is to the Qur'an's vision of Paradise. As with the first Gardens, the naming of water immediately after the naming of greenery follows the natural logic: the deep green of verse 64 is sustained by the bubbling springs of verse 66, for vegetation thrives only where water flows. Water remains, throughout the sūrah's description of the Gardens, the emblem of life, purity, refreshment, and the satisfaction of thirst — the most precious of blessings to the people of the desert and the deepest image of the quenching of the soul's longing. Whether flowing or bubbling, the springs of the Gardens signify the same great reality: the perpetual, inexhaustible life and refreshment of Paradise, where thirst is quenched forever and the springs never fail.

**The Logic: The Fine Gradations of a Perfectly Just Reward**

The subtle distinctions the sūrah draws between the two pairs of Gardens — branches versus deep green (48/64), flowing versus bubbling springs (50/66), and others to follow — reveal something profound about the precision of divine justice. God's reward is not crude or approximate but finely calibrated, distinguishing even between grades of the blessed with exquisite precision. The foremost receive flowing springs; the companions of the right receive bubbling springs; and the difference, subtle as it is, reflects the precise proportioning of reward to striving. This fineness of gradation testifies to the perfection of God's justice: He weighs each soul's striving with perfect precision and rewards it with perfectly proportioned exactness, so that no one receives more or less than his striving merits. And yet, alongside this precise justice runs the overflowing generosity that makes even the "lesser" reward a Garden of surpassing beauty. The fine gradations honor justice; the surpassing beauty of every grade honors generosity. Both together reveal the character of a Lord who is at once perfectly just and boundlessly generous — the two attributes the sūrah keeps holding together.

**Application: Trusting the Precision of the Reward**

For the reader, the fine gradations of the Gardens teach a deep trust in the precision and justice of God's reward. Whatever the measure and quality of one's striving, one can trust that it will be answered with perfectly proportioned exactness — that no good deed will be undervalued, no excellence overlooked, no striving unrewarded or misjudged. The one who strives greatly will receive the flowing springs; the one who strives less will receive the bubbling springs; and each will receive exactly what his striving merits, weighed by a Judge whose justice is flawless. This trust frees the believer from anxiety about whether his efforts will be fairly valued, for he knows they will be weighed with perfect precision by One who sees all and wrongs none. It also spurs him to strive for more, knowing that greater striving yields a proportionally greater reward — that the difference between flowing and bubbling springs, between gold and silver, between the higher and lower Gardens, is real and worth striving for. The believer thus strives earnestly and trusts completely, confident that his reward will be both perfectly just and overflowingly generous. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the bubbling springs, my Lord — and I trust the perfect precision of the justice that measures every spring to every striving.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:67

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Bubbling Springs**

The refrain returns after the mention of the two bubbling fountains in each of the second Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the life-giving water of the second pair of Gardens — the sparkling, gushing springs that sustain their deep-green abundance. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**Gratitude for the Precision of Justice**

The subtle distinction between the "flowing" springs of the higher Gardens and the "bubbling" springs of the lower — a distinction the previous verse illuminated — invites a particular form of gratitude that this refrain can draw out: gratitude for the *precision and justice* of God's reward. It is a boon that God's justice is so precise, so finely calibrated, that it distinguishes even between grades of blessing with perfect exactness. This precision means that every soul's striving is weighed with flawless accuracy and rewarded with perfectly proportioned exactness — a guarantee that no one is wronged, no excellence overlooked, no effort undervalued. The refrain invites gratitude for this: for the boon of a reward measured by perfect justice, in which each receives exactly what his striving merits, weighed by a Judge who neither errs nor wrongs. This is a boon above all to those who fear they might be undervalued or forgotten — the assurance that God's justice is precise enough to weigh even the finest gradations of striving and to reward them with corresponding exactness.

**The Boon That Justice and Generosity Coexist**

There is a further boon that this stretch of the sūrah invites the reader to contemplate: the remarkable coexistence, in God's reward, of perfect justice and overflowing generosity. These two might seem to be in tension — strict justice giving each exactly his desert, boundless generosity giving beyond all desert — yet in God's reward they are perfectly reconciled. The gradations of the Gardens honor justice: each rank of the blessed receives precisely according to its striving. Yet even the lowest rank receives, by God's generosity, a Garden of surpassing beauty — more than any striving could strictly earn, for no finite striving could ever merit an eternal Garden. Thus every dweller of Paradise receives both perfect justice (his rank proportioned to his striving) and overflowing generosity (his reward exceeding all his deeds could earn). This reconciliation of justice and generosity is itself among the greatest of God's boons, for it means that the believer can trust both that he will not be wronged (justice) and that he will be given far beyond his deserving (generosity). The refrain invites gratitude for this — for a Lord in whom perfect justice and boundless generosity meet.

**The Logic: Why the Reward Exceeds the Deed**

There is a profound logic worth drawing out here, one the tradition dwelt upon. No finite deed, however excellent, could strictly *earn* an eternal reward — for a finite cause cannot, by strict justice alone, produce an infinite effect. The eternal Gardens are therefore, in the deepest sense, always a gift of God's generosity, never merely a wage strictly owed. This is why the Prophet ﷺ taught, as recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, that *"none of you will enter Paradise by his deeds alone,"* and when asked, "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" he replied, *"Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy."* The reward, though proportioned to striving by God's justice, always exceeds what striving could strictly earn, by God's mercy. This does not diminish the value of striving — for the striving is the condition God has appointed, and the gradations of reward do follow the grades of striving — but it locates the ultimate ground of the reward in God's generosity rather than in the creature's desert. The refrain, celebrating the boons of the Gardens, thus points beyond the deeds that earned them to the mercy that gave them: the Gardens are boons — gifts of grace — not merely wages, and gratitude, not entitlement, is their fitting reception.

**Application: Striving Earnestly While Relying on Mercy**

For the reader, this understanding shapes the whole posture of the believer's striving. He strives earnestly, knowing that the reward is proportioned to striving and that greater striving yields a greater rank; yet he relies ultimately on God's mercy, knowing that no striving could strictly earn the eternal Gardens and that they are, in the end, a gift of grace. This dual posture — earnest striving joined to humble reliance on mercy — is the balance the tradition has always taught. It guards against two errors: the presumption of imagining one's deeds have earned Paradise as a strict right, and the despair of imagining that one's deeds are too poor to matter. The believer neither presumes nor despairs: he strives with all his effort, and he hopes in God's mercy, knowing that his reward will be both perfectly just and overflowingly generous. And he receives whatever he is granted not as an entitlement claimed but as a boon bestowed — answering the refrain, at each remembrance of the Gardens, with the gratitude of one who knows that even his best deeds could never have earned so great a gift. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord — and I know that even to attain the least of them is a mercy beyond what all my striving could ever have deserved.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:68

> **In each are fruits, date palms, and pomegranates.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fruits of the Second Gardens**

The sūrah names the produce of the second pair of Gardens: *"In each are fruits, date palms, and pomegranates"* — *fīhimā fākihatun wa nakhlun wa rummān*. Like the first pair of Gardens, the second pair bears fruit — but here the fruits are named specifically: general fruit, and then two named in particular, the date palm and the pomegranate. The naming of specific fruits, and the comparison with the description of the first Gardens' fruit, again yields a meaningful distinction that the commentators drew out.

**A Comparison with the First Gardens' Fruit**

The first pair of Gardens was described as containing *"two kinds of every fruit"* (verse 52) — every fruit, and each in doubled variety, a description of overflowing and comprehensive abundance. The second pair is described as containing *"fruits, date palms, and pomegranates"* — fruit named more specifically and, some commentators observed, somewhat more limited than the comprehensive "every fruit, two kinds" of the higher Gardens. On this reading (noted by the commentators), the fruits of the second Gardens are named particularly (fruit, dates, pomegranates) where the fruits of the first were named comprehensively (two kinds of every fruit), marking another subtle distinction in rank between the two pairs. Yet, as always, the distinction is between abundance and greater abundance: the second Gardens are still rich with fruit, including the two most prized and beneficial fruits named specifically.

**Why the Date Palm and the Pomegranate?**

The commentators asked why, among all fruits, the date palm and the pomegranate are singled out for special mention, and their answer reveals the wisdom of the choice. The *date palm* (*nakhl*) — as noted in the commentary on verse 11 — was for the Arabs the noblest and most beneficial of fruits: a staple food of great nutritional value, sweet and sustaining, preservable, the very emblem of wholesome provision. The *pomegranate* (*rummān*) was prized for its unique qualities and, as Ibn Kathīr noted, for its *medicinal properties* — a fruit valued not only for its taste but for its benefit to health. Thus the two fruits named are singled out because the one (the date) is preeminent in *nutrition* and the other (the pomegranate) in *medicinal benefit* — together representing the fruits most beneficial to human well-being. The commentators observed that these two, along with the fig and the olive (named in Sūrat al-Tīn), are among the fruits given to human beings in this world that still retain something of their paradisal quality — earthly foretastes of the fruits of the Gardens.

**A Question and Its Answer: Are These Fruits Earthly or Heavenly?**

The mention of familiar earthly fruits — dates and pomegranates — in the description of Paradise raises a question the tradition addressed: are the fruits of Paradise the same as the fruits of this world? The answer, drawn from the Qur'an itself, is that they are *recognizable* but *transcendent* — the same in name and appearance, so that the dwellers receive them with the joy of the familiar, but incomparably superior in taste, quality, and perfection. The Qur'an says of the fruits of Paradise:

> *"...Whenever they are provided with a fruit therefrom, they will say, 'This is what we were provided with before,' for they will be given things in resemblance to it."* (Qur'an 2:25)

Thus the date and the pomegranate of Paradise are like the date and pomegranate of earth in name and form, so that the blessed recognize and welcome them, yet they surpass their earthly counterparts beyond all comparison — perfected, everlasting, and free of every flaw.
