The familiar fruits of earth are, in this sense, gentle previews of the perfected fruits of the Gardens, given to us here so that we may recognize and rejoice in them there.

**The Logic: The Redemption of the Familiar**

There is a beautiful logic in the naming of familiar, homely fruits — dates and pomegranates — among the delights of Paradise. It teaches that the reward of the Hereafter is not the abolition of the good things of this world but their *perfection and redemption*. The date and the pomegranate that nourished and delighted the human being on earth are not left behind as belonging to a lower order; they are taken up, perfected, and made everlasting in the Gardens. This reveals a deep truth about the Islamic vision of the reward: it is continuous with the good of this world, fulfilling and perfecting the wholesome delights the human being already knew, rather than replacing them with something wholly alien. The familiar goods of earth — food, drink, rest, love, beauty — are, in their lawful and wholesome forms, foretastes of the perfected goods of Paradise, and their presence in the reward, perfected and everlasting, testifies to the goodness of God's creation and the continuity of His mercy from this world to the next. The good things of this world are not tricks or traps but genuine goods, whose perfection awaits in the Gardens.

**Application: Receiving Earthly Fruits as Foretastes**

For the reader, the naming of dates and pomegranates among the fruits of Paradise transforms the experience of eating such fruits in this world. Every date, every pomegranate — every wholesome, delicious fruit — becomes a foretaste and a reminder of the perfected fruits of the Gardens, a sample here of a delight to be perfected there. This recognition adds a dimension of gratitude and hope to the simple pleasure of eating: the believer tastes the earthly fruit and remembers its perfected counterpart in Paradise, letting the sweetness of the one awaken longing for the other. It also teaches him to receive the good things of this world rightly — not despising them as beneath spiritual notice, nor idolizing them as the final good, but enjoying them with gratitude as genuine goods and foretastes, whose fullness lies ahead. The believer thus eats the date and the pomegranate with a double joy: the joy of the present delight, and the joy of the promise it foreshadows. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the fruits of the Gardens, my Lord — and let every earthly fruit I taste awaken my longing for their perfection in Your Gardens.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:69

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Fruits of the Second Gardens**

The refrain returns after the mention of the fruits, date palms, and pomegranates of the second Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the wholesome, nourishing, delightful fruit of Paradise — the perfected form of the good things the human being knew and loved on earth. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**Gratitude for the Continuity of God's Goodness**

This occurrence of the refrain, following the naming of familiar earthly fruits among the delights of Paradise, invites a particular gratitude: gratitude for the *continuity* of God's goodness from this world to the next. The same generous Lord who gave the date and the pomegranate in this world gives them, perfected, in the next; the goodness the believer has tasted here is a pledge and foretaste of the goodness prepared there. This continuity is itself a boon, for it assures the believer that the good he has known is not an illusion or a trap but a genuine gift, whose fullness is yet to come. It teaches him to trust the goodness of God's creation — to receive the good things of this world as real goods from a generous Giver — while looking forward to their perfection. The refrain invites gratitude for this trustworthy continuity: the God who has been good to us here will be good to us there, and the goods of this world are the reliable foretastes of the goods of the next.

**The Gratitude That Bridges the Two Worlds**

There is a beautiful function in the way the refrain, recurring through the description of the Gardens, bridges the believer's gratitude for this world and his gratitude for the next. The same question — "which of your Lord's boons do you deny?" — was asked over the fruits of the earth (verses 11–13) and is now asked over the fruits of the Gardens (verses 68–69). The identical refrain over both binds them together, teaching the believer that his gratitude is one continuous response to one continuous generosity, flowing from this world into the next. The believer's thankfulness for the date he eats today and his thankfulness for the date he hopes to eat in the Gardens are not two separate gratitudes but one — the single, continuous gratitude of a servant to a Lord whose generosity spans both worlds. The refrain thus unifies the believer's whole life of gratitude into a single response to a single Giver, from the first fruit of the earth to the last fruit of the Gardens.

**The Logic: Gratitude as the Thread That Runs Through Everything**

By this point in the sūrah, the reader can see clearly that gratitude is the thread running through the whole chapter — through the blessings of the earth, the warnings of the Fire, and the promise of the Gardens alike. The refrain, recurring over every category of God's dealing with His creatures, teaches that gratitude is the fitting and unifying response to the whole of existence: to the gifts of nature, to the mercy of warning, to the promise of reward, and to everything in between. This is a profound teaching about the nature of the religious life itself. Faith, at its heart, is a kind of gratitude — the grateful recognition of God as the source of every blessing and the trusting response to His every dealing. The opposite of faith, *kufr*, is at its root ingratitude — the covering-over and denial of the gifts and their Giver. The refrain, running through the whole sūrah, thus reveals gratitude as the very substance of the right relationship with God: to be a believer is to be grateful, and to be grateful is to answer, at every turn, "none of Your boons, my Lord, do I deny." The whole sūrah is, in this sense, a training in the gratitude that is the essence of faith.

**Application: Making Gratitude the Continuous Thread of One's Life**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain calls for making gratitude the continuous thread of one's own life, as it is the continuous thread of the sūrah. This means cultivating a gratitude that spans all circumstances and both worlds: thankful for the gifts of this life, thankful even for its warnings and trials, and thankful in advance for the promised gifts of the next — one continuous response to one generous Lord. Such a life of continuous gratitude is the practical form of faith itself, and it is the source of a deep and abiding joy, for the grateful heart finds cause for thankfulness in everything and therefore never lacks for joy. The believer who has learned, from this sūrah, to answer the refrain continuously — over every blessing, every warning, every promise — has learned to live the whole of his life as an unbroken act of gratitude, and thereby to live as a true believer, bound to his Lord by the unbreaking thread of thankfulness. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord, in this world or the next — for my whole life, from the first breath to the last, and beyond it into Your Gardens, is one continuous gift, met with one continuous gratitude.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:70

> **Therein are good and beautiful ones;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Companions of the Second Gardens**

The sūrah now names the companions of the second pair of Gardens: *"Therein are good and beautiful ones"* — *fīhinna khayrātun ḥisān*. Just as the first pair of Gardens contained pure companions (verse 56), so the second pair contains companions described here as *khayrāt* (good ones) and *ḥisān* (beautiful ones). The description pairs, in a single phrase, the two qualities that together constitute the perfection of a person: goodness of character and beauty of form. This is a description of surpassing richness, for it names not merely outward beauty but the union of inner goodness with outer beauty — the wholeness of a perfected being.

**Khayrāt: Goodness of Character**

The word *khayrāt* is the plural of *khayrah*, from the root *kh-y-r*, "good, excellent, the best." It denotes those who are *good* — good in character, in morals, in disposition, in all the inward qualities that make a person truly excellent. The commentators explained *khayrāt* as referring to companions of excellent character — good-natured, virtuous, noble, and refined in their inner qualities. This is a crucial point: the description of the companions of Paradise begins not with their outward beauty but with their inward goodness. Their first-named quality is *character*, not appearance. This ordering reflects a deep truth about the Islamic valuation of the human being: that the truest and most important beauty is the beauty of character, and that outward beauty, however great, is empty without the inner goodness that gives it worth. The companions of Paradise are, first and foremost, *good* — and their outward beauty is the fitting outward form of their inward goodness.

**Ḥisān: Beauty of Form**

The word *ḥisān* is the plural of *ḥasnāʾ*, from the same root *ḥ-s-n* that gives *iḥsān* (excellence, goodness done beautifully) and *ḥusn* (beauty). It denotes those who are *beautiful* — beautiful in form, in appearance, in outward comeliness. Paired with *khayrāt* (good in character), it completes the description: the companions of Paradise are both good and beautiful, uniting inward excellence with outward beauty. This union is significant, for in this world inward goodness and outward beauty are often separated — the beautiful may lack goodness, and the good may lack beauty. In Paradise, the two are perfectly joined: goodness and beauty coincide, so that the outward form perfectly expresses the inward reality, and beauty is never divorced from the goodness that gives it meaning. This is part of the perfection of Paradise — the healing of the tragic separation, so common in this world, between the beautiful and the good.

**The Union of the Good and the Beautiful**

There is a profound teaching in the pairing of *khayrāt* and *ḥisān*, goodness and beauty. The root *ḥ-s-n*, from which *ḥasan* (good/beautiful) and *iḥsān* (excellence) derive, itself unites the meanings of goodness and beauty — for in the deepest understanding, the truly good is beautiful and the truly beautiful is good. This is why the same word can mean both "good" and "beautiful": at the highest level, goodness and beauty are one. The companions of Paradise, described as both *khayrāt* and *ḥisān*, embody this union — the coincidence of the good and the beautiful in a single perfected being. And this union reflects the nature of Paradise as a whole, and ultimately the nature of God, who is both perfectly good and perfectly beautiful, and in whom goodness and beauty are eternally one. The description of the companions thus points, like all the beauty of the Gardens, toward the ultimate union of goodness and beauty in God Himself, whose nearness is the deepest reward of Paradise.

**The Logic: Why Character Is Named Before Beauty**

The ordering of the description — *khayrāt* (good) before *ḥisān* (beautiful) — carries a lesson of great practical importance. It teaches that character precedes appearance in the true valuation of a person: that inward goodness is the first and most important quality, and outward beauty the second, deriving its worth from the goodness it accompanies. This is a direct corrective to the disordered valuation of the human being that prevails in much of the world, where outward beauty is prized above all and inward character is neglected or ignored. The Qur'an's ordering reverses this: it names goodness first, teaching that a person's truest worth lies in their character, and that beauty without goodness is hollow. The Prophet ﷺ taught this same valuation in his guidance on choosing a spouse, as recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"A woman is married for four things: her wealth, her lineage, her beauty, and her religion. So choose the one of religion, may your hands be rubbed with dust [i.e., may you prosper]."* (al-Bukhārī 5090; Muslim 1466)

The priority of religion and character over wealth, lineage, and beauty in the choice of a spouse mirrors the priority of *khayrāt* over *ḥisān* in the description of the companions of Paradise: goodness first, beauty second. The one who orders his valuations this way — prizing character above appearance — is aligning himself with the divine valuation and preparing himself for the perfected union of goodness and beauty in the Gardens.

**Application: Prizing and Pursuing Goodness of Character**

For the reader, this verse's ordering of goodness before beauty carries a clear practical lesson: to prize character above appearance, both in others and in oneself. In valuing others, the believer learns to look first at goodness of character — at kindness, honesty, faith, and virtue — rather than at outward beauty, wealth, or status, valuing people as God values them. And in cultivating himself, the believer learns to pursue goodness of character above outward appearance — to invest his effort in becoming *good* (developing virtue, refining character, purifying the heart) rather than merely in appearing beautiful or impressive. This is the truest self-improvement, for the beauty of character is the beauty that lasts, that God values, and that is perfected in the Gardens, while the beauty of mere appearance fades and passes with the world. The believer who prizes and pursues goodness of character is building the beauty that will be perfected in Paradise — the union of *khayrāt* and *ḥisān*, goodness and beauty, that the companions of the Gardens embody. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the good and beautiful companions, my Lord — and teach me to prize, and to pursue, the goodness of character that is the truest and most lasting beauty.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:71

> **so which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Good and Beautiful Companions**

The refrain returns after the mention of the good and beautiful companions of the second Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the perfected companionship of Paradise — companions uniting goodness of character with beauty of form. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**Gratitude for the Union of Goodness and Beauty**

This occurrence of the refrain, following the description of companions who are both good and beautiful, invites gratitude for one of the deepest healings that Paradise offers: the union of goodness and beauty that, in this world, is so often tragically divided. In this life, the good and the beautiful are frequently separated — beauty found without goodness, goodness found without beauty — and this separation is a source of much of the disappointment and disorder of human relationships and human valuations. Paradise heals this separation, uniting goodness and beauty perfectly in its companions and, indeed, in all its blessings. To be promised a world in which the good is always beautiful and the beautiful is always good — in which one need never again choose between character and comeliness, nor be deceived by beauty without goodness — is a boon of profound consolation. The refrain invites gratitude for this healing: the perfected coincidence of the good and the beautiful, restoring the wholeness that this world so often breaks.

**The Boon of a World Without the Deception of Appearances**

There is a related boon that this stretch of the sūrah invites the reader to contemplate: the freedom, in Paradise, from the deception of appearances that so plagues this world. In this life, appearances constantly deceive — beauty conceals corruption, charm masks cruelty, the pleasing exterior hides the rotten interior. This deception is a source of much human suffering, as people are drawn by beauty into relationships and situations whose inner reality betrays the outer promise. In Paradise, this deception is abolished: the outward beauty perfectly expresses the inward goodness, so that appearance and reality coincide, and no one is ever deceived by a beauty that conceals corruption. The companions who are *khayrāt* and *ḥisān* — good and beautiful together — represent this abolition of deception: their beauty is the true expression of their goodness, and their goodness the true foundation of their beauty. To be promised a world free of the deception of appearances, in which what one sees is truly what is, is a boon the refrain invites the reader to recognize and receive with gratitude.

**The Logic: Why the Perfected World Unites What This World Divides**

There is a coherent logic in the way Paradise unites what this world divides — goodness and beauty, appearance and reality, the outer and the inner. This world is a place of separation, fragmentation, and deception, in which good things are mixed with bad, beauty with corruption, promise with betrayal. This fragmentation is part of the very nature of a fallen, testing world, in which nothing is perfect and everything is mixed. Paradise, as the perfected world, is the healing of this fragmentation: the place where the good and the beautiful, the outer and the inner, the appearance and the reality, are made whole and one. This wholeness is part of what makes Paradise *Paradise* — not merely a place of pleasures but a place of *integrity*, in which all the separations and deceptions of this world are healed and all things are made whole. The union of goodness and beauty in the companions is a particular instance of this general healing — a sign of the wholeness that pervades the perfected world. The refrain, celebrating this boon, invites the reader to long for the wholeness of Paradise as a healing of the fragmentation he knows so well in this world.

**Application: Seeking Wholeness and Integrity Now**

For the reader, the promise of the healed union of goodness and beauty in Paradise carries a present-tense aspiration: to seek, even now, the integrity and wholeness that Paradise perfects. This means striving to unite goodness and beauty in oneself — to make one's outward conduct the true expression of one's inward goodness, so that appearance and reality coincide and one becomes, in one's small measure, both *khayr* (good) and *ḥasan* (beautiful) in the truest sense. It means pursuing integrity — the alignment of the inner and the outer, the healing of the gap between what one appears to be and what one is — which is itself a foretaste of the wholeness of Paradise. And it means valuing this integrity in others and in one's relationships, seeking the good-and-beautiful rather than the merely beautiful, and building bonds on the foundation of goodness that alone can survive into the perfected world. The believer thus seeks, in this fragmented world, to cultivate the wholeness that Paradise will perfect — becoming, as far as he is able, one of the good and beautiful even now. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the healed union of goodness and beauty, my Lord — and let me seek that wholeness even now, as a foretaste of the integrity of Your Gardens.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:72

> **Maidens secluded in pavilions;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sheltered Companions**

The sūrah continues its description of the companions of the second Gardens: *"Maidens secluded in pavilions"* — *ḥūrun maqṣūrātun fī al-khiyām*. The companions are described as *ḥūr* — a word denoting the pure, lustrous-eyed companions of Paradise — who are *maqṣūrāt*, "secluded" or "reserved," within *khiyām*, "pavilions" or "tents." The image is one of shelter, honor, and reservation: companions dwelling in the protected seclusion of pavilions, guarded and honored, reserved for the dwellers of the Gardens.

**The Meaning of Ḥūr**

The word *ḥūr* is the plural associated with *ḥawrāʾ*, denoting those with a striking contrast between the deep black of the eye and the pure white surrounding it — a feature considered the height of beauty in the eye. The *ḥūr* are the pure companions of Paradise, celebrated for the beauty of their eyes and their overall loveliness and purity. The word conveys both beauty and purity — the lustrous beauty of the eye and the untouched purity of the companion. The *ḥūr* of Paradise are mentioned in several places in the Qur'an as among the blessings of the Gardens (e.g., 44:54; 52:20; 56:22), and they represent, like the pure companions of verse 56, the perfected and purified fulfillment of the human longing for beautiful and faithful companionship.

**Maqṣūrāt: Secluded and Reserved**

The word *maqṣūrāt* comes from the root *q-ṣ-r*, and the commentators explained it in complementary ways. It can mean "secluded, kept, reserved" — companions kept in the honored shelter of their pavilions, reserved exclusively for the dwellers of the Gardens, not roaming or exposed. It can also mean those whose gaze or attention is "restrained" or "confined" to their spouses — echoing the "modest gaze" (*qāṣirāt al-ṭarf*) of the companions of the first Gardens (verse 56). The commentators (Ibn Kathīr and others) drew a subtle grammatical distinction here that carries meaning: the companions of the *first* (higher) Gardens were described with the *active* participle *qāṣirāt al-ṭarf* — "those who restrain their gaze," implying that they restrain their own glances by their own chastity — while the companions of the *second* (lower) Gardens are described with the *passive* participle *maqṣūrāt* — "those who are secluded/restrained," implying that their glances are guarded *for* them, or that they are kept in seclusion. This distinction, drawn from the active versus passive forms of the same root, was taken as another of the subtle marks distinguishing the higher companions (who are chaste by their own active restraint) from the lower (whose chastity is guarded for them). Both are pure and honored; the distinction is a fine one, reflecting the graded ranks of the two pairs of Gardens.

**Khiyām: The Pavilions of Paradise**

The companions are secluded in *khiyām*, "pavilions" or "tents." These are not the humble tents of earthly life but magnificent pavilions of Paradise, described in the tradition as structures of surpassing beauty and vast dimensions. A famous ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim describes the pavilion (*khaymah*) of Paradise:

> *"Indeed, in Paradise the believer will have a tent (khaymah) of a single hollowed pearl, its length in the sky being sixty miles. In each corner of it the believer will have family (companions) whom the others cannot see."* (al-Bukhārī 4879; Muslim 2838, in meaning)

The pavilions of Paradise are thus structures of immense scale and beauty — a single hollowed pearl sixty miles high — within which the blessed dwell in honor and privacy with their companions. The seclusion of the companions in these pavilions signifies not confinement in any negative sense but the honored privacy and shelter of a cherished dwelling — the intimate, protected, honored home of the blessed in the Gardens.

**The Logic: Honor Expressed Through Shelter and Reservation**

There is a meaningful logic in the description of the companions as *secluded* and *reserved* in *pavilions*. In the customs of honor that the original audience understood, that which is most precious and most honored is that which is sheltered, protected, and reserved — the treasure kept in the vault, the honored guest given the private chamber, the cherished person shielded from the common gaze. The seclusion of the companions in their pavilions thus signifies their preciousness and honor: they are treasures, sheltered and reserved, not exposed or common. This valuation runs counter to a modern sensibility that may read "seclusion" as confinement or diminishment; but in the logic of the verse, seclusion is the mark of honor and preciousness, the shelter given to what is most cherished. The companions are secluded because they are precious; their pavilions are the honored dwellings of the treasured. This teaches a valuation of persons — and of intimacy — as precious things to be honored and sheltered rather than exposed and made common, a valuation the believer is invited to carry into his own relationships.

**Application: Honoring the Precious Through Shelter**

For the reader, the image of the honored, sheltered companions of Paradise carries a lesson about the valuation and treatment of what is precious. In a world that increasingly exposes and makes common what ought to be honored and sheltered — that treats intimacy as public, privacy as weakness, and the cherished as common — the verse recalls a different valuation: that the precious is to be honored through shelter, that intimacy is a treasure to be reserved rather than displayed, and that the cherished person is to be protected and honored rather than exposed. This valuation shapes how the believer treats his own relationships and intimacies — as precious things to be honored, sheltered, and reserved, not made common or exposed — and how he regards the honor and privacy of others. It reflects the deep dignity that Islam accords to the intimate bond and to the honored person, a dignity mirrored in the sheltered pavilions of the Gardens. The believer thus learns, from the honored companions of Paradise, to honor the precious through shelter, and to see in privacy and reservation not confinement but the mark of preciousness and honor. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the honored companions of the Gardens, my Lord — and teach me to honor what is precious as You have honored them.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:73

> **so which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Amid the Sheltered Companions**

The refrain returns after the mention of the pure companions secluded in the pavilions of Paradise. The blessing being underscored is the honored, sheltered, perfected companionship of the Gardens — the pure companions dwelling in the magnificent pavilions of pearl. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"*

**Gratitude for the Honored Home**

This occurrence of the refrain, following the image of companions sheltered in the vast pearl-pavilions of Paradise, invites gratitude for the boon of an *honored home* in the Gardens — a dwelling of surpassing beauty and honor, a private and protected abode of joy. The longing for a home — a place of one's own, secure, honored, and peaceful, shared with beloved companions — is one of the deepest of human longings, and one that this world satisfies only partially and temporarily, for every earthly home is imperfect, insecure, and ultimately left behind at death. The pavilions of Paradise are the fulfillment of this longing: an eternal, perfect, honored home, secure forever, shared with perfected companions, in a dwelling of pearl beyond all earthly splendor. The refrain invites gratitude for this — the boon of a true and everlasting home, the fulfillment of the homelessness and impermanence that mark the human condition in this passing world.

**The Cumulative Portrait of Paradise**

By this point the sūrah has assembled, through its measured unveiling of feature after feature, a rich and cumulative portrait of Paradise: the deep-green Gardens, the flowing and bubbling springs, the abundant fruits, the good and beautiful companions, the honored pavilions, the couches of brocade, the effortless rest. The refrains between these features have allowed each to register fully, so that the reader has been led, not through a blur of undifferentiated pleasure, but through a detailed and vivid vision of a place he can picture, desire, and long for. This cumulative portrait serves the sūrah's deepest purpose: to awaken in the reader a real and motivating longing for the Gardens, a longing strong enough to draw him toward the righteousness that leads there. The refrain, recurring through this cumulative unveiling, is the drumbeat that has carried the reader through the whole vision, marking each feature with the same summons to gratitude and, through gratitude, to longing and striving.

**The Logic: Why the Vision Must Be Detailed to Move the Heart**

There is a reason the sūrah does not merely say "Paradise is wonderful" and leave it there, but unveils its features one by one in vivid detail, punctuating each with the refrain. The human heart is moved to longing and striving not by abstractions but by concrete, vivid images that it can picture and desire. A vague promise stirs little; a detailed vision stirs much. By unveiling Paradise feature by feature — each made vivid, each marked with the refrain — the sūrah builds in the reader's imagination a Paradise that is real, picturable, and deeply desirable, and thereby awakens the longing that motivates the whole striving of the religious life. This is the mercy and the wisdom of the detailed vision: God describes His reward in such vivid particularity precisely so that His servants may long for it enough to seek it, letting the beauty of the promise draw them toward the good that leads to its fulfillment. The refrain, recurring through the vision, ensures that this longing is bound always to gratitude — so that the reader longs for the Gardens not with mere covetous desire but with grateful love for the Giver who prepared them.

**Application: Letting the Complete Vision Shape One's Striving**

For the reader, this occurrence of the refrain, near the close of the vision of the Gardens, is an invitation to let the complete, cumulative portrait of Paradise shape the whole tenor of his striving. Having been led through feature after feature — the green, the springs, the fruits, the companions, the pavilions, the rest — the reader is invited to hold the whole vision in his heart as a living presence, a real destination toward which his life is directed. This vision, held vividly and lovingly, becomes a powerful spur to righteousness and a powerful shield against the fleeting attractions of this world: for the heart that has set itself on the Gardens, made real and beloved through detailed contemplation, finds the passing pleasures of this world losing their grip, and finds in every hardship and every act of restraint a step toward a beloved and vividly imagined home. The believer thus lets the complete vision of the Gardens draw and shape him, striving toward it with grateful longing, answering the refrain — over the whole assembled vision — with the gratitude and the longing it was designed to awaken. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord — and by the vision of the Gardens You have unveiled, draw my whole striving toward the home You have prepared.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:74

> **Untouched as yet by man or jinn;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purity of the Companions Reaffirmed**

The sūrah reaffirms the purity of the companions of the second Gardens: *"Untouched as yet by man or jinn"* — *lam yaṭmithhunna insun qablahum wa lā jānn*. This is the same description given of the companions of the first Gardens in verse 56 — that they are pure and untouched, reserved entirely for the dwellers of the Gardens, touched by no man and no jinn before. The repetition of this description for the second pair of Gardens, as for the first, emphasizes the consistent purity of the companionship of Paradise across all its grades: whether in the higher Gardens or the lower, the companionship is wholly pure, chaste, and reserved.

**The Meaning Reaffirmed**

As explained in the commentary on verse 56, the verb *ṭamatha* implies the taking of virginity, and the description conveys that the companions are pure, chaste, and untouched — reserved exclusively for the dwellers of the Gardens. The mention of both "man" and "jinn" reflects the sūrah's consistent dual address to the two responsible species: the companions are untouched by any of either kind. The repetition of this exact description for both pairs of Gardens underscores that the purity of the Gardens' companionship is universal and constant — not a feature of the higher Gardens alone but of all the Gardens, at every grade. The intimacy of Paradise, in all its ranks, is wholly pure, entirely free of the impurities, betrayals, and corruptions that mar human relationships in this world.

**The Significance of the Repetition**

The Qur'an's repetition of this description for the second Gardens, exactly as for the first, carries meaning beyond mere emphasis. It teaches that the essential *purity* of the reward does not diminish with rank. Though the sūrah has drawn subtle distinctions between the higher and lower Gardens — branches versus deep green, flowing versus bubbling springs, comprehensive versus specific fruits, active versus passive restraint — the *purity* of the companionship is identical in both: "untouched by man or jinn" in both the first pair (verse 56) and the second (verse 74). This teaches that while the *abundance* and *grade* of the reward may vary, its *purity* is constant and complete at every level. Every dweller of Paradise, of whatever rank, enjoys a companionship wholly pure and untouched. Purity is not a feature of the elite reward alone but of the whole reward, for Paradise is, by its very nature, the abode of purity — the place from which all impurity is forever excluded.

**Paradise as the Abode of Purity**

The reaffirmation of purity here points to a defining characteristic of Paradise as a whole: it is the abode of purity, from which every impurity — physical, moral, and spiritual — is forever excluded. The Qur'an describes the dwellers of Paradise as entering it purified, greeted with the word of peace and purity:

> *"...and their Lord will give them a pure drink."* (Qur'an 76:21)

> *"...and they will be led to the Garden in groups until, when they reach it, its gates are opened and its keepers say to them, 'Peace be upon you; you have become pure, so enter it to abide eternally.'"* (Qur'an 39:73)

Paradise is entered only by the purified, contains only the pure, and is itself the very abode of purity. The purity of its companions is one instance of this all-pervading purity — the purity of a place where nothing corrupt, nothing defiled, nothing impure can enter or remain. This is part of what makes Paradise the fulfillment of the deepest human longing: not merely for pleasure, but for a purity and cleanness of existence that this world, mixed and defiled as it is, can never provide.

**The Logic: The Human Longing for Purity**

There is a deep human longing for purity — for cleanness, wholeness, and freedom from corruption — that runs beneath much of human moral and spiritual striving. This world constantly falls short of it: even the best things are mixed with impurity, even the purest relationships are shadowed by flaw, even the cleanest joys are tainted by some corruption. The human heart longs for a purity it never fully finds here — a longing expressed in the universal human sense that things ought to be cleaner, wholer, and purer than they are, and in the grief and disgust we feel at corruption and defilement. Paradise is the fulfillment of this longing: the abode of perfect purity, where the deep human yearning for cleanness and wholeness is at last completely satisfied. The reaffirmed purity of the companions is a particular sign of this general fulfillment — a token of the perfect purity that pervades the whole reward. The believer's longing for purity, so often frustrated in this world, finds its answer in the Gardens.

**Application: Pursuing Purity as Preparation for the Pure Abode**

For the reader, the emphasis on the purity of Paradise carries a clear preparatory lesson: that the pure abode is entered only by the purified, and that the pursuit of purity in this life is preparation for the purity of the next. This means striving for purity in every dimension: purity of body (which Islam cultivates through its practices of physical cleanliness and ritual purification), purity of conduct (freedom from the corruptions of sin and injustice), and above all purity of heart (freedom from the inward defilements of arrogance, envy, hatred, and hypocrisy). The Qur'an makes the purity of the heart the very criterion of salvation:

> *"The Day when neither wealth nor children will avail — except one who comes to God with a sound (pure) heart."* (Qur'an 26:88–89)

The believer thus pursues purity now — of body, conduct, and heart — as both a good in itself and a preparation for the pure abode of the Gardens, striving to become the kind of purified soul that Paradise admits and befits. He seeks the purity he longs for, cultivating it in this defiled world as a foretaste and a preparation for the perfect purity of the world to come. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* Not the pure companionship of the Gardens, my Lord — and let me pursue the purity of heart that alone may enter Your pure abode.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:75

> **so which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Nearing the Close**

The refrain returns after the reaffirmation of the purity of the companions of the second Gardens. The blessing being underscored is the constant, complete purity of the reward at every grade — the purity that pervades the whole of Paradise. *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* This is among the last occurrences of the refrain, which has threaded through the whole sūrah and now nears its final appearance.

**Gratitude for the Purity That Pervades the Reward**

This occurrence of the refrain invites gratitude for the pervading purity of Paradise — the boon of an abode in which every impurity is forever excluded and every longing for cleanness and wholeness is completely fulfilled. This is a boon that speaks to a longing often unnamed but deeply felt: the longing to be free, at last, of all corruption and defilement, to dwell in a purity that this mixed and defiled world can never provide. To be promised such an abode — pure in its companionship, pure in its pleasures, pure in its very air, from which nothing corrupt can enter or remain — is to be promised the fulfillment of one of the deepest and most often frustrated of human longings. The refrain invites gratitude for this: the boon of a perfectly pure home, the answer to the heart's yearning for a cleanness it has never fully known.

**The Refrain's Long Journey Nearing Its End**

As the refrain nears its final occurrences, it is worth pausing to reflect on the whole journey it has traced. The same question — "So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?" — has recurred through the blessings of the earth, the warnings of the Fire, and the promise of the Gardens; over the seen and the unseen; over gifts of nature, mercies of warning, and promises of reward. Thirty-one times it has asked the two responsible species the same unanswerable question, and thirty-one times the honest heart has been unable to name a single boon it could deny. This long journey of the refrain has been a sustained training of the heart in gratitude — a patient, rhythmic, cumulative summons to the thankfulness that is the essence of faith. As it nears its end, the refrain has accomplished its work: it has led the reader through the whole range of God's dealings with His creatures and taught him to answer each with the same grateful affirmation. The one who has followed the refrain through its whole journey has been schooled, comprehensively, in gratitude.

**The Logic: The Cumulative Power of the Refrain's Repetition**

The full cumulative power of the refrain's repetition can now be appreciated. A single asking of the question might be dismissed or forgotten; but the question asked thirty-one times, over an ever-lengthening and ever-deepening catalogue of blessings, becomes irresistible. By its final occurrences, the refrain carries the accumulated weight of the entire sūrah — every blessing of earth, every mercy of warning, every promise of the Gardens — and asks, with all that weight behind it, whether the reader will deny any of it. The answer, by now, is overwhelmingly clear: none of it, not one boon, can honestly be denied. This is the cumulative power of the refrain's repetition — not the tedium of empty repetition, but the mounting, irresistible force of a question asked again and again over a mounting catalogue of undeniable gifts, until denial becomes not merely wrong but impossible, and gratitude becomes the only truthful response the heart can give. The refrain, by its very repetition, has made ingratitude untenable and gratitude inevitable for the honest heart.

**Application: The Heart Trained to Gratitude**

For the reader, this near-final occurrence of the refrain marks the near-completion of the heart's training in gratitude — a training that the whole sūrah has been conducting. The fruit of this training is a heart habituated to gratitude, a heart that answers, spontaneously and truthfully, "none of Your boons, my Lord, do I deny" — over every blessing, every trial, every promise. Such a heart has learned the deepest lesson of the sūrah and the deepest posture of faith: to receive the whole of existence, from the gifts of the earth to the promise of the Gardens, with continuous, truthful gratitude to the Giver of all. The believer, having followed the refrain through its long journey, emerges with his heart schooled in this gratitude, ready to carry it beyond the sūrah into the whole of his life — answering, at every turn, the question the refrain has taught him, and living the thankful life that is the life of faith. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord — and by asking me so many times, You have trained my heart to answer, always and truthfully, in gratitude.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:76

> **They recline upon green cushions and beautiful wonders.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Image of the Blessed at Rest**

The sūrah gives its final image of the dwellers of the Gardens at their ease: *"They recline upon green cushions and beautiful wonders"* — *muttakiʾīna ʿalā rafrafin khuḍrin wa ʿabqariyyin ḥisān*. As with the first pair of Gardens (verse 54), the dwellers are depicted reclining in perfect rest and luxury — here upon green cushions and objects of surpassing, wondrous beauty. This completes the description of the second pair of Gardens and, indeed, the whole description of Paradise, ending on the image of the blessed at their perfect and everlasting rest.

**Green Cushions — Rafraf Khuḍr**

The word *rafraf* denotes cushions, thickly padded and richly made — luxurious furnishings for reclining, spread wherever the dwellers of Paradise desire. The commentators (al-Ālūsī) explained *rafraf* as thickly brocaded cushions or fabrics, and some understood the word more broadly as a metaphor for ease, comfort, and abundant furnishing. The cushions are described as *khuḍr*, "green" — the same restful, life-associated color that has pervaded the description of the Gardens (recall the deep green of verse 64). The green cushions complete the Gardens' suffusion with the color of life, tranquility, and rest, and provide the dwellers with the utmost comfort for their eternal repose. The image of reclining upon green cushions is the image of perfect, restful ease — the body at complete comfort in a setting of tranquil beauty.

**Beautiful Wonders — ʿAbqariyyin Ḥisān**

The dwellers recline also upon *ʿabqariyyin ḥisān*, "beautiful wonders" — objects of surpassing beauty, genius, and rarity. The word *ʿabqarī* is a remarkable one. It is used in Arabic to denote anything of extraordinary genius, magnificence, rarity, or excellence — anything beyond the ordinary ken of human skill and understanding. The commentators (al-Rāzī, Ibn Kathīr) explained that *ʿabqar* was said to be a legendary land of the jinn, and that anything of surpassing skill, beauty, or wonder — anything seemingly beyond human capacity to produce — was called *ʿabqarī*, "of ʿabqar," as we might call something "otherworldly" or a "work of genius." The furnishings of Paradise are thus described as *ʿabqarī* — of a beauty, richness, and craftsmanship beyond anything human hands could make or human minds fully conceive, wonders of surpassing excellence. Paired with *ḥisān*, "beautiful," the phrase conveys objects of a beauty and wonder beyond all earthly comparison — the furnishings of Paradise as works of a genius and splendor exceeding the limits of human imagination.

**Reclining: The Repose That Ends the Description**

It is fitting that the description of Paradise ends, as it largely began (verse 54), with the image of the blessed *reclining* — at perfect rest, needing nothing, striving for nothing, at peace forever. This framing of the description of Paradise with the image of reclining rest is deeply significant. It teaches that the deepest character of the reward is *rest* — the end of all toil, the arrival at a peace so complete that one reclines forever, at ease in the presence of one's Lord. The whole of this life is toil, striving, and unrest; the whole of the reward is rest, peace, and repose. The description of Paradise ends on this note of perfect rest because rest is the fulfillment of the deepest longing of the toiling human creature — the longing, beneath all its particular desires, for a peace that this restless world can never give. The blessed recline upon green cushions and beautiful wonders because they have arrived, at last, at the rest for which the human heart was always longing.

**The Logic: The Beauty Beyond Imagination**

The word *ʿabqarī* — denoting wonders beyond the human ken — points to a truth that runs through the whole description of Paradise: that its beauty and blessing ultimately exceed all human imagination and description. The sūrah has described the Gardens in vivid, concrete images — green branches, flowing springs, fruits, companions, cushions — precisely to awaken the heart's longing; yet these images are, in the end, only the imaginable threshold of an unimaginable reality. The furnishings are *ʿabqarī*, beyond human conception; and the whole reward, as the sacred tradition declares, is beyond what any eye has seen, any ear has heard, or any heart has conceived (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824). The vivid descriptions are the doorway of images through which the heart is drawn toward joys that exceed all images. This is the final logic of the description: it gives the heart beauty enough to long for, while pointing always beyond itself to a reality that surpasses all it names — so that the believer longs not for the images alone but for the unimaginable reality they foreshadow, and ultimately for the nearness of the Lord who prepared it, the joy beyond all joys.

**Application: Longing Beyond the Images Toward the Giver**

For the reader, this final image of the Gardens — reclining upon green cushions and wonders beyond imagination — is an invitation to let his longing be drawn beyond even the beautiful images toward the unimaginable reality they foreshadow, and ultimately toward God Himself. The believer contemplates the vivid beauty of the Gardens and lets it awaken longing; but he understands that the images are only the threshold of a reality that exceeds them, and that the deepest joy of Paradise is not the cushions or the fruits or even the companions, but the nearness of the Lord who prepared them all — the beholding of His Face, the joy beyond all joys, before which every other blessing of Paradise pales. The Qur'an names this supreme reward:

> *"[Some] faces, that Day, will be radiant, looking at their Lord."* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

The believer thus lets the beauty of the Gardens draw his longing forward — through the imaginable to the unimaginable, and through all the blessings to the Blesser Himself — so that his ultimate desire is not merely for the reward but for the Rewarder, not merely for the Gardens but for the God who made them and whose nearness is their crown. *Which of your Lord's boons do you deny?* None, my Lord — and beyond every boon, let my heart long most of all for You, the Giver, whose nearness is the joy that surpasses even the Gardens.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:77

> **So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Refrain**

This is the thirty-first and final occurrence of the sūrah's great refrain: *"So which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* It comes after the last image of the Gardens — the blessed reclining upon green cushions and wonders beyond imagination — and it seals the whole long series of refrains that has threaded through the entire chapter. With this final asking, the refrain completes its work: it has led the reader through the whole range of God's boons, from the teaching of the Qur'an to the wonders of the highest Gardens, asking at every turn the same unanswerable question, and training the heart, thirty-one times over, in the gratitude that is the essence of faith.

**Al-Nasafī's Reflection on the Thirty-One Refrains**

The commentator al-Nasafī offered a beautiful reflection on the structure and number of the refrain's thirty-one occurrences, which the tradition has preserved:

> *"Eight are mentioned following verses that enumerate the marvels of God's creation. Seven follow verses wherein are mentioned the Fire and its severities, in accordance with the number of the gates of the Fire. After these seven, there are eight that follow a description of the two Gardens and their inhabitants, in accordance with the number of the gates of the Garden. And there are eight after that for the two Gardens that are below them. So whoever affirms the first eight and acts in accordance with what they require, the gates of the Garden will be opened to him and the gates of the Fire will be shut to him."*

This reflection reveals a hidden architecture in the placement of the refrains. The seven refrains that follow the verses of the Fire correspond to the seven gates of Hell (cf. 15:44), as if to say: affirm these boons and each gate of the Fire is shut against you. The sixteen refrains that follow the descriptions of the Gardens correspond to the eight gates of Paradise (doubled for the two pairs of Gardens), as if to say: affirm these boons and each gate of the Garden is opened to you. And the eight that follow the marvels of creation ground the whole in the recognition of God the Creator. The structure teaches that gratitude for God's boons is itself the key that shuts the gates of the Fire and opens the gates of the Garden — that the thankful affirmation the refrain calls for is not a mere sentiment but the very substance of salvation.

**The Refrain as the Key of Salvation**

Al-Nasafī's insight illuminates the deepest meaning of the refrain and of the whole sūrah. The refrain is not a decorative repetition but a summons to the disposition that determines one's eternal destiny. To affirm God's boons — to answer the refrain rightly, with grateful acknowledgment — is to be among the faithful and grateful, for whom the gates of the Garden open; to deny them (*tukadhdhibān*) is to be among the ungrateful deniers, for whom the gates of the Fire stand ready. Gratitude and denial are thus not trivial responses but the two roads that divide at the fork of every one of God's boons, leading respectively to the Gardens and the Fire. The refrain, asked thirty-one times, has placed the reader again and again at this fork, calling him each time toward the road of gratitude that leads to the Gardens. Its final asking is the last call before the sūrah's closing verse — the last summons to choose the road of thankful affirmation that opens the gates of Paradise.

**The Logic: Why Gratitude Is the Substance of Faith**

The whole sūrah, sealed by this final refrain, reveals a profound truth about the nature of faith itself: that faith is, at its root, gratitude, and disbelief is, at its root, ingratitude. The Arabic word for disbelief, *kufr*, means literally "to cover over, to conceal" — and what the disbeliever covers over is precisely the truth of God's boons and their Giver. The disbeliever is the one who covers over the gifts, denying their Source, refusing to acknowledge the Giver. The believer, by contrast, is the *shākir*, the grateful one, who uncovers and confesses the gifts and their Giver, answering the refrain with thankful affirmation. This is why the refrain is the key of salvation: because to answer it rightly, with gratitude, is to be a believer, and to be a believer is to be among those for whom the Gardens are prepared. The sūrah, in training the heart to answer the refrain in gratitude, has been training it in the very substance of faith. The final refrain seals this training: the whole chapter has been a school of the gratitude that is faith, and its final question is the last examination — will you, having seen all these boons, answer in the gratitude that opens the Gardens, or in the denial that opens the Fire?

**Application: The Choice Sealed by the Final Refrain**

For the reader, this final occurrence of the refrain calls for the decisive sealing of the choice the whole sūrah has been urging: the choice of gratitude over denial, of the road that opens the Gardens over the road that opens the Fire. Having been led through the whole catalogue of God's boons and asked, thirty-one times, whether he will deny any of them, the reader is now called to make his answer final and to live it — to become, decisively and for the whole of his life, one of the grateful who affirm God's boons and act upon that affirmation. This is the practical fruit of the entire sūrah: a life of gratitude, in which every blessing is acknowledged, every gift is traced to its Giver, and the whole of existence is received with thankful affirmation. The believer, standing at this final refrain, gives his final answer and resolves to live it: *None of Your boons, my Lord, do I deny — not one, in this world or the next; and by this gratitude, shut against me the gates of the Fire and open to me the gates of Your Gardens.* And with that answer given, he is ready for the sūrah's closing benediction.

---

## Sūrah al-Raḥmān 55:78

> **Blessed is the Name of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing Benediction**

The sūrah closes as it opened — with the exaltation of the Lord: *"Blessed is the Name of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty"* — *tabāraka ismu rabbika dhī al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām*. After the long catalogue of boons — the blessings of earth, the warnings of the Fire, the promise of the Gardens — and after the thirty-one askings of the refrain, the sūrah comes to rest in a final act of blessing and praise directed at God Himself. The chapter that began by naming the Lord (*al-Raḥmān*, verse 1) ends by blessing His Name — a perfect closing of the circle, returning the reader from the contemplation of the gifts to the exaltation of the Giver.

**The Frame of Majesty and Bounty**

The closing verse echoes, almost exactly, the central verse of the sūrah. In verse 27, at the sūrah's midpoint, God was described as *"the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty"* (*dhū al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām*), marking the end of the discussion of earthly blessings and the great truth that God alone remains while all else passes. Now, in verse 78, the same phrase — *"Possessed of Majesty and Bounty"* (*dhī al-jalāli wa'l-ikrām*) — returns to mark the end of the discussion of heavenly blessings. The commentators noted this deliberate framing: just as the phrase marked God's transcendence over the passing blessings of earth (verse 27), so it now marks His transcendence over the everlasting blessings of Heaven (verse 78). The lesson is profound: just as God transcends and outlasts the blessings of this world, so He transcends even the blessings of Paradise. The Gardens, glorious and everlasting as they are, are still His gifts, still pointing beyond themselves to the Giver, whose Majesty and Bounty surpass even the reward He bestows. The frame teaches that God alone is the ultimate reality and the ultimate goal — greater than the earth He made, and greater even than the Paradise He prepared.

**A Note on the Reading of the Verse**

The commentators noted a subtle point of Qur'anic reading here. In verse 27, the phrase "Possessed of Majesty and Bounty" (*dhū*) grammatically modified "the Face of thy Lord." Here in verse 78, in the primary reading, "Possessed of Majesty and Bounty" (*dhī*) modifies "thy Lord" — so the verse blesses the Name of the Lord who is Himself Possessed of Majesty and Bounty. According to another canonical reading, the phrase could be read as modifying "the Name" itself (*dhū* rather than *dhī*) — so that the *Name* is Possessed of Majesty and Bounty. Both readings are meaningful: whether it is the Lord or His Name that is Possessed of Majesty and Bounty, the verse exalts the Majesty and Bounty that pervade the divine reality and its very Name. The Name of God is blessed because the One it names is the Possessor of all Majesty and all Bounty.

**The Meaning of Tabāraka**

The verse opens with *tabāraka*, "blessed is" or "exalted and full of blessing is" — a word from the root *b-r-k*, connected to *barakah*, "blessing, abundance, the increase and endurance of good." *Tabāraka* is a form used in the Qur'an exclusively of God, expressing His supreme blessedness, the abundance and permanence of good that belongs to Him, and the overflowing of blessing from Him upon His creation. It is the word that opens Sūrat al-Furqān (*"Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion,"* 25:1) and Sūrat al-Mulk (*"Blessed is He in whose hand is dominion,"* 67:1). To say *tabāraka ismu rabbika* — "blessed is the Name of thy Lord" — is to declare the supreme blessedness of God, the source and fullness of all blessing, from whom every boon the sūrah has named has flowed. It is the fitting climax of a sūrah devoted to the enumeration of God's boons: having named the countless gifts, the sūrah declares blessed the Giver from whom they all flow, the Possessor of all Majesty and all Bounty.

**The Prophet's Devotion to This Name**

As noted in the commentary on verse 27, the Prophet ﷺ held the divine description "Possessor of Majesty and Bounty" in special devotion, and its appearance as the sūrah's closing words gives it particular prominence. The Prophet ﷺ advised his followers to cling to this Name in supplication:

> *"Persist in [invoking] 'O Possessor of Majesty and Bounty' (yā Dhā'l-Jalāli wa'l-Ikrām)."* (cited by Ibn Kathīr)

And ʿĀʾishah reported that after the completion of his prayers, the Prophet ﷺ would often sit and say:

> *"O God, You are Peace (al-Salām), and from You comes peace. Blessed are You, O Possessor of Majesty and Bounty (Dhā'l-Jalāli wa'l-Ikrām)."* (recorded by Muslim)

It is deeply fitting that the very phrase with which the Prophet ﷺ concluded his prayers is the phrase with which this sūrah concludes. Just as the believer, having completed his worship, exalts his Lord as the Possessor of Majesty and Bounty, so the sūrah, having completed its enumeration of God's boons, exalts Him with the same words. The closing of the sūrah and the closing of the prayer share the same benediction — the blessing of the Lord of Majesty and Bounty.

**The Logic: From the Gifts to the Giver**

There is a profound and fitting logic in ending a sūrah about God's boons not with the boons themselves but with the exaltation of the Giver. The whole sūrah has been a tour of God's gifts — the earth and its produce, the sea and its treasures, the creation of man and jinn, the warnings of the Fire, the promise of the Gardens. But gifts, however glorious, are not the final point; they point beyond themselves to the One who gives them. To rest in the gifts and forget the Giver would be to miss the deepest purpose of the whole sūrah. So the sūrah, having displayed the gifts, turns the reader's gaze finally to the Giver, exalting His blessed Name and His Majesty and Bounty. This is the culmination of the whole chapter's logic: the boons were never ends in themselves but were always signs and pointers toward their Source, and the fitting end of contemplating the boons is the exaltation of the Lord from whom they flow. The sūrah thus completes the movement it has been making throughout — from the gifts to the Giver, from the blessings to the Blessed One, from *al-Raḥmān*'s bounty to *al-Raḥmān* Himself. The one who has truly received the sūrah's teaching ends not merely grateful for the gifts but in love with and exalting the Giver, the Possessor of all Majesty and all Bounty.

**Application: Ending Every Contemplation in the Praise of God**

For the reader, the closing verse teaches the fitting end of all contemplation of God's blessings: the exaltation and praise of God Himself. Whenever the believer contemplates God's gifts — the beauty of creation, the mercies of his life, the promise of the reward — the fitting culmination of that contemplation is not to rest in the gifts but to be lifted through them to the praise of the Giver, exalting His blessed Name and His Majesty and Bounty. This is the movement the sūrah models and the believer is called to make his own: to let every blessing become a doorway to the praise of the Blesser, so that the contemplation of the gift always ends in the exaltation of the Giver. The believer thus makes his whole life an echo of this closing verse — receiving the boons with gratitude, and letting that gratitude rise, always, into the praise of the Lord of Majesty and Bounty. And he takes to heart the deepest teaching of the sūrah's frame: that God transcends even His greatest gifts, that the Giver is greater than the Gardens, and that the ultimate goal of the believer is not the reward but the Rewarder — the blessed Lord, Possessor of all Majesty and all Bounty, whose nearness is the crown of Paradise and whose praise is the fitting end of every contemplation. *Blessed is the Name of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty.*

---

**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Raḥmān]**
